# Errata and decisions against ISA r20

[← pdp64 index](../README.md)

The ISA document (`docs/11-64W/11-64W-isa-r20.md`, written under the working name 11/64W; the machine is **pdp64**) is a paper design.
Writing it down as `spec/pdp64.json` found the following. Each item says what the document says, what the spec does, and why.
Items marked **DECISION** are choices the document left open; change them in the spec and everything downstream regenerates.

1. **Opcode bits in three diagrams are stale.** §4.6, §4.7 and §4.8 draw parcel 0 with opcode `000011` (BESC, MBLK) and `001110` (TAGOP).
   The §4.16 map says `0x0C`, `0x0D`, `0x15` (`001100`, `001101`, `010101`). The spec follows §4.16, which every other section agrees with.
2. **Opcode count.** §4.16 says "fifty-six of sixty-four assigned, eight reserved". The map it prints has 12 + 6 + 4 + 13 + 4 + 16 = **55**
   assigned, the spare ALU slot `0x23`, and eight reserved at `0x28`-`0x2F` (55 + 1 + 8 = 64). `specgen.py check` asserts the real numbers.
3. **DECISION: PC-relative base.** The document says displacements are "relative to a stated point" and never states it. Spec: **the address of the
   first parcel of the instruction**, for `d(PC)` operands, `LEA`, and branches. Branch displacements count 2-byte parcels (the §4.4 reach table needs
   that). Reason: the displacement then does not depend on the length chosen for the instruction itself, so branch relaxation is monotone and
   needs no feedback loop. `LEA 0(PC),Rn` yields the address of the `LEA`.
4. **DECISION: `PUSHM`/`POPM`.** §4.7 puts them in `MBLK` with "parcel 1 is a 16-bit mask", but an `MBLK` parcel 1 carries an 8-bit sub-opcode, so the
   mask has no room at `len=01` (and §4.14 forbids deciding the parcel-0 interpretation per sub-opcode). Spec: they take the two free `SOP` slots,
   `PUSHM #mask` (slot `A`) and `POPM #mask` (slot `B`), with the mask as a 16-bit immediate (mode `0100`): four bytes, using only mechanisms that exist.
   Cost: `SOP` has no free slots left. Mask bit *n* is `Rn`; `PUSHM` stores the lowest register at the highest address.
5. **DECISION: `SYS` sub-opcode.** §4.11 says `reg` and `rm` combine into eight bits of sub-opcode, §4.3.1 says `SYS` takes its sub-opcode from the
   `reg` field, and `TRAP #n` is to be two bytes with 256 vectors, which all three cannot be. Spec: `reg` is a 4-bit **class**; `rm` is a register
   operand, a 4-bit immediate (`SPL`, `TRAP` 0-15) or, in class 0, the minor opcode of the no-operand instructions. `TRAPL #n` (class 15, `len>=1`,
   n in parcel 1) reaches vectors 0-255. `PROBER`/`PROBEW Rd,Rs` use parcel 1 for the second register.
6. **DECISION: `JTAG`.** `JTAG Rn, table` has no field for `Rn` (`reg` is the sub-opcode, `rm`/mode give the table). Spec: `JTAG` is legal only
   with mode `0001`: `JTAG d(Rb)(Rx*s)` with the scale ignored, `PC <- [Rb + d + 8*(Rx & 7)]`. `Rx` is the tagged register.
7. **DECISION: mode `0001` (scaled index) at `len>=2`.** The document gives only `len=01` (`rm4 Rx4 s2 d6`, §12 item 1 calls the 6-bit
   displacement thin). Spec: each extra parcel adds 16 displacement bits: 6 / 22 / 38. Mode `0011` (`d(DP)`) keeps the 4 bits of parcel 1 as
   padding at `len=01` (12-bit displacement as stated) and uses the same widths as base mode.
8. **DECISION: `SOB`.** `Rn` takes the `reg` field, which is the displacement byte at `len=00`, so `SOB` exists from `len=01` (consistent with
   §3.1.1, which lists it with the two-parcel forms). Displacement 16/32/48 bits, in parcels.
9. **DECISION: sub-opcode numbers.** The document names sub-opcodes and never numbers them. The numbers in the spec group related operations
   (`BESC`: counts 0x00-0x08, rotate 0x10, field 0x20, polynomial 0x30, UTF-8 0x40; `FESC` by width and kind; `ATOM` plain/`_DB` pairs).
10. **DECISION: `BESC` rotate by immediate.** `RORI Rd,Rs,#n` is a four-byte form with the count in the low six bits of parcel 1; `ROL Rd,Rs,#n`
    assembles to `RORI` with `64-n` (§4.15). `DSHR`, `EXTB`, `EXTZB`, `INSB` need the third parcel (position, width) and exist at `len>=2` only.
11. **DECISION: `MESC` parcel 1.** `sub4 rm4 disp8` at `len=01` (§4.13); displacement widens 8 / 24 / 40. Only modes whose payload fits that
    way are legal (`base`, `dp`, `postinc`, `predec`, `pcrel`, `abs`); the scaled-index mode is not available in `MESC`.
12. **The machine's name** is pdp64. The documents say 11/64W; they are kept unmodified under `docs/`.
13. **DECISION: `CMP` operand order.** PDP-11 `CMP a,b` computes `a-b`; here the destination must be a register, so a literal `src,dst` meaning
    would force every compare of a register against an immediate or a memory operand to reverse its branch condition. Spec: the encoding computes
    **`reg - rm`**, and the assembler writes it `CMP Rd, rm` (register first). `CMP a,b` followed by `BLT` still reads "branch if a < b", as on the
    PDP-11; only which side must be a register differs. `BIT` is symmetric.
14. **DECISION: flags.** Subtraction and `CMP` set `C` on a borrow (PDP-11 sense, so `BHIS`/`BCC` mean "no borrow", unsigned `>=`). `ADD` sets `C` on a
    carry out. `V` is signed overflow. `FCMPD` sets `N` if a<b, `Z` if a==b, `V` if unordered, `C` cleared (open item 7 in the ISA: the branch
    conditions do not yet separate "unordered" cleanly).
15. **DECISION: pre-decrement syntax.** §5 writes mode `0111` as `-d(Rn)`, which is also how a negative base displacement is written. Spec:
    `-(Rn)` pre-decrements by the operand size, `(Rn)-d` by an explicit stride (the mirror of `(Rn)+d`); `-d(Rn)` is always base+displacement.
16. **DECISION: `FRECPED` and `FRSQRTED` dropped.** ISA 4.10 lists reciprocal and reciprocal-square-root estimates "for Newton-Raphson". The machine has
    `FDIVD`, `FSQRTD` and the fused multiply-add family, so a software Newton loop buys speed only if the hardware divide is slow, which this
    design does not chase; each estimate needs a table in hardware and an exact specification (ARM and RISC-V pin down the algorithm and table, the ISA
    says only "not correctly rounded", so two implementations would return different bits; *fact-check 2026-10-22: in RISC-V the pinned-down estimate exists only in the vector extension, `vfrec7`/`vfrsqrt7`, see `target/riscv/tcg/vector_helper.c` in QEMU, not in the scalar F/D extensions*); and no document names a user (ISA 1.2 rule 3). Sub-opcodes
    `0x40` and `0x41` of `FESC` are free again. The ISA text in `docs/11-64W/` is left as written.
17. **DECISION (added at the user's request): floating-point exception flags.** The ISA never mentions them (a search for flags, `fenv`, "inexact", "sticky" finds
    nothing), and IEEE 754 requires five status flags (invalid operation, division by zero, overflow, underflow, inexact) and C99 `<fenv.h>` needs software
    access to them. Added: a sticky **FPSR**, five bits laid out as RISC-V's `fflags` (bit 0 NX inexact, 1 UF underflow, 2 OF overflow, 3 DZ divide by zero,
    4 NV invalid). Every floating-point operation that can raise one ORs it into FPSR and **never traps**. One operation reads and writes it,
    `FSFLAGS rs, rd` (`FESC` sub-opcode `0x40`): `rd <- FPSR; FPSR <- rs & 31`, so clear is a swap with zero, test-and-clear a swap with zero, set a swap
    with the bits. FPSR is part of the thread context (saved with the PSW). It is not a mode: it never changes what an instruction does, only records
    what it did (ISA 1.1 "no mode bits" is untouched). Quiet-NaN comparisons raise NV only for signaling NaNs (`FCMP*`, `FMIND`, `FMAXD`). Tininess is detected after
    rounding. **Still open, found while doing this: IEEE 754 also requires directed rounding (toward +inf, -inf, zero) for the arithmetic operations, and §9.8 puts
    the mode "in the opcode wherever it is not round-to-nearest-even" but the opcode list has no such variants except `FRINT*`. See CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md section 12.**
18. **Unsigned 64-bit conversions (added).** `FCVTDQU` (double to unsigned quad, truncating; out of range or NaN raises NV and saturates to 0 or 2^64-1) and `FCVTQUD`
    (unsigned quad to double, rounding to nearest even). C needs both; without them each is a compare, a branch and several instructions.
19. **Round half away from zero (added).** `FRINTDA` joins `FRINTD` (nearest even), `FRINTDZ`, `FRINTDM`, `FRINTDP`, for C's `round()`. A qualifier of the round
    operation, so no new operation.
20. **CRC instructions: considered, added, then withdrawn (the user's decision).** The user first asked for `CRC32` and `CRC32C` instructions (four widths each) in
    addition to carry-less multiply, then withdrew them: "we have carryless mul, no need for either crc". The ISA's own choice in 4.6.3 therefore stands:
    `CLMULL`/`CLMULH` are the primitive beneath every CRC polynomial (a CRC is a carry-less multiply plus a Barrett reduction), GF(2^k) erasure codes and
    GCM, with no polynomial committed to the architecture. `BESC` sub-opcodes `0x34`-`0x3B` are free.
21. **`FCMPD`/`FCMPS` are register-first like `CMP`.** The ISA does not say which operand is the register for the floating-point compares. They follow errata 13:
    `FCMPD rd, rm` computes `rd - rm`. (The assembler had treated `FCMPD` like the other ALU operations until now.)
22. **`FESC` operand roles.** `reg` = destination, `low` = first source, `t` = second source, `x` = third source (the fused multiply-add family has four registers;
    ISA 4.10). `fmadd rs, rt, ru, rd` = `rs*rt + ru`; `fmsub` = `rs*rt - ru`; `fnmadd` = `-(rs*rt) - ru`; `fnmsub` = `-(rs*rt) + ru`, each rounded once.
    `fdivd rs, rd` is `rd <- rd / rs` and the `s` forms likewise; `fcopysign rs, rt, rd` takes the magnitude of `rs` and the sign of `rt`.
23. **Integer `ABS`, `MIN`, `MAX`, `MINU`, `MAXU` (added at the user's request; superseded by errata 98: they are assembler aliases).** `AESC` sub-opcodes `0x08` (`abs rs, rd`) and `0x10`-`0x13` (`min`, `minu`, `max`, `maxu`
    `ra, rb, rd`; the `u` forms compare unsigned). They are the integer counterparts of `FABS`, `FMIND` and `FMAXD`, so by the folding rule (integer vs float and signed vs
    unsigned are qualifiers) they add no operation. Flags: `abs` sets N and Z from the result and V when the operand is the most negative number (whose absolute value is
    itself, the result wrapping), C unchanged; `min` and `max` set N and Z from the result, clear V, leave C. Hardware: reuse the negate path and the compare of
    `SUB`/`CMP` with a 64-bit select (est. 150-250 LUTs). Teaching the compiler the idioms (`x<0?-x:x`, `a<b?a:b`) is a shared front-end change that helps every target.
24. **DECISION: floating-point spelling and the alias rule (the user's).** Floating-point operations are the integer operation with an `f` in front (`fadd`, `fsub`,
    `fmul`, `fdiv`, `fsqrt`, `fmadd`, `fabs`, `fmin`, `fmax`, `fcmp`, `fclass`, `frint`...), and a width other than the default is a **decimal suffix in bits**:
    `fadd` is binary64 (the machine's native float), `fadd32` binary32, `fadd16` binary16 (half precision). The rounding variants are one letter:
    `frint` (nearest even), `frintz`, `frintm`, `frintp`, `frinta`. The old spellings (`faddd`, `fadds`, `fmind`, `frintdz`...) stay as aliases. **Aliases are now
    one rule**: a word numbered 0x100 above another word of its group stands for that word (one mnemonic, one instruction, ISA 4.15); `rol` also complements its
    count. The same pass moved `bhis`, `blo`, `mulu` and `wait` to the rule. Conversions (`fcvtdq`...) and the integer widths (`movq`, `movl`, `movw`, `movb`) keep
    their spellings until the user chooses how to write a width on an integer operation.
25. **`SET cc, rd` (added; the first instruction written with a condition word).** `set eq, r1` writes 1 to `r1` if the condition holds and 0 if not. One `AESC`
    sub-opcode (`0x20`); the condition is the `x` field of parcel 1, numbered like the low nibble of the branch opcodes (`al` 0, `ne` 1, `eq` 2, `pl` 3, `mi` 4,
    `vc` 5, `vs` 6, `cc`/`hs` 7, `cs`/`lo` 8, `ge` 9, `lt` 10, `gt` 11, `le` 12, `hi` 13, `los` 14; the drift test checks that numbering against the branches). Flags
    are not changed. The condition words are reserved only in that position: a label may still be called `eq` or `lt`.
26. **NaN results are canonical (decided in `sim64`).** The ISA is silent on the bits of a NaN that an operation creates. Every arithmetic or conversion
    result that is a NaN is the canonical quiet NaN (`0x7FF8000000000000`, binary32 `0x7FC00000`), whatever the operands carried: no payload propagation
    (the RISC-V rule). `FNEG`, `FABS` and `FCOPYSIGN` work on the bits and do not canonicalize. Without the rule the result would depend on the host that ran
    the emulator (x86 makes a negative NaN for 0/0). Hardware must do the same for results to match `sim64`.
28. **DECISION (the user's): integer arithmetic stays 64-bit; there are no sized ALU operations.** The question was whether a descendant of the PDP-11 should
    have native 16-bit (and 32-bit, 8-bit) integer arithmetic. The answer is no: registers are always written in full (ISA 4.2: narrow loads sign- or zero-extend),
    `movb movw movl` sign-extend and `movzb movzw movzl` zero-extend, from a register, memory or an immediate, and every arithmetic and compare instruction works on
    all 64 bits. A narrow value is held extended, and the compiler extends again where a result can leave the range (a C `int` is kept sign-extended).
    *Measured cost of this choice* (pdp64 pcc, 841-file fair corpus, 405,744 instructions): a re-extension `movl r,r` after an operation that can leave the range,
    17,580 (4.3% of instructions; 49% follow `lea` used as an add, 16% `add`, 14% `shl`, 8% `mul`); a narrow load immediately consumed by an ALU or compare
    instruction, 8,451 pairs (2.1%), of which only 146 have a register as the other operand and a dead loaded value, that is, would fuse into a sized memory
    operand (the large group, 4,356 `movzb`+`cmp reg,#imm`, cannot fuse under the one-memory-operand rule either). *Why not:* a width needs bits the encoding does not
    have (the opcode space has 8 reserved slots and every ALU slot but one is assigned; the mode and register fields are full); a prefix costs two bytes per use and
    makes decode restartable in two parts; and the gain is bounded by the numbers above. Signed `int` overflow is undefined in C, so the re-extensions after signed
    operations are the compiler's conservative default and can be dropped (`-mnowrap`: 388,919 instructions, 0.99 times amd64); unsigned 32-bit wrap needs the
    extension. Tests: `tests/sim/extend.s` (every width, source and boundary, partial stores, flags) and `tests/c/extend.c` (the same in C; it returns 1 under host gcc too).
29. **DECISION (the author's; superseded by errata 95): devices are eight memory-mapped registers plus DMA.** Ethernet, USB and disk devices are each eight memory-mapped registers (eight
    addresses in the ordinary address space) and move their data by DMA. *Why:* a driver for so small and regular an interface is trivial to write, which removes driver
    code, and the device carries its own data so the processor only sets up a transfer and has no multitasking burden from it: more of the processor is left for the
    user and the programs that matter (the third layer of the optimization described in the README: gate count, code count, the system around the processor).
    *Where it sits:* ISA section 16 already makes every device a bus device that does not touch the ISA, and already owes devices what DMA needs from the processor
    side (a memory type per page, `FENCE` before a doorbell write, atomics for shared queues); this adds a convention for the device side and changes no instruction.
    *Open:* the register layouts, the DMA descriptor format, the interrupt and doorbell conventions, and whether other devices follow the same shape; none are written yet.
30. **DECISION (the author's plan): the first operating system is a port of Unix/32V; an OpenBSD port follows if an LLVM back end is easy enough.** *Why 32V:* it is V7 already
    ported to a 32-bit paged machine (the VAX-11/780) with the portable C compiler, so it is closer to the pdp64 than V7 on the PDP-11. (The author's belief was that 32V already demand-pages; the headers show
    that it swaps whole processes and uses the VAX page tables for relocation, and that demand paging came with 3BSD. The pdp64's MMU supports demand paging, so it can be added afterwards.) *Why it matters
    for the design:* the first system sets a short list of what the system architecture must define first: the exception entry sequence, a way to write `PTBR`, interrupt priorities, a clock, a console and a disk
    ([`pdp64-system-architecture.md`](pdp64-system-architecture.md) sections 12 to 14). *Open:* the kernel sources are not in the repositories; the LP64 and a.out-versus-ELF decisions; the LLVM estimate; everything
    in the system architecture document's open list.
    *Fact-check 2026-10-22:* the 32V claim is confirmed by Wikipedia (*UNIX/32V*: "released without virtual memory paging, retaining only the swapping architecture of Seventh Edition"; paging added at Berkeley by Bill Joy and Ozalp Babaoglu, released as 3BSD in 1979). The 2002 Caldera licence covers early Unix up to and including 32V (Wikipedia, *Ancient UNIX*); it is not shown to cover 3BSD. See `fact-check-2026-10-22.md`.
31. **DECISION (the author's): the fourth parcel is removed, and `len = 11` becomes a second page of one-parcel instructions; two further instructions are added.** The full map is in
    [`CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md`](CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md) section 12 ("Decided design: the second page"). In short: instructions are 1, 2 or 3 parcels (2, 4 or 6 bytes); `len = 11` is one parcel in the layout
    `11 opc:6 reg:4 low:4` with its own 64-entry opcode map, holding register-deferred loads and stores, `lea` and quad/long loads and stores at small offsets from `sp`, quad and long loads and stores at small
    offsets from a register, frame adjustment and register-save instructions, and four-bit-immediate forms of the common operations. A fused compare-and-branch (`cbCC`, in the reserved top-level
    opcode `0x28`) and a 32-bit rotate by a constant (`rori32`, a `BESC` sub-opcode) are added. *Why:* only 49 of 373,635 compiled instructions (0.013%) needed the fourth parcel; the one-parcel
    forms are estimated at about 118 KB (7%) and the two instructions at 22 KB (1.3%) of a 1.67 MB image (static counts on the 827-file corpus; section 12). *Rule kept:* every page-2 form is exactly the operation of
    its long form (the same flags, the same result), so an assembler that chooses the shortest encoding never changes what a program does. *Costs:* the immediate, absolute, PC-relative and branch fields shrink from 48
    to 32 bits and the base-register displacement from 44 to 28 bits. *Alternatives considered:* keep four parcels; reserve `len = 11`; the register-deferred form of every memory-operand opcode (rejected in favour of the explicit map).
32. **Added: system-register moves `MFSR` and `MTSR` (the missing instructions of a paging operating system).** The ISA names the registers `PTBR`, `EPC`, `EVA` and `ECAUSE` (§2.2) but had no
    instruction to write the first or read the others, and no `MFDMW`; a kernel could not install a page table or learn why it was entered. Two instructions, **`mfsr rd,name`** and **`mtsr name,rs`**, in the
    `SYS` group as class-0 minors **13** and **14** (the last free minors were 13 to 15), four bytes: parcel 0 `01 SYS 0 minor`, parcel 1 `sr:8 r:4 pad:4` (`sr` the system-register number, `r` the general register, `pad` zero). The
    registers they reach, by name (an enumeration in `spec/pdp64-mnemonics.hbnf`, like the condition words of `set`): **`ptbr` 0** (read/write), **`epc` 1** (read/write), **`eva` 2** (read only), **`ecause` 3** (read
    only), **`dmw0` 4** and **`dmw1` 5** (read/write), **`usp` 6** and **`ssp` 7** (the user and supervisor stack pointers, read/write; added because any kernel must read and write the user stack pointer to build a
    signal frame, copy a process or switch context, and `R14` only names the current mode's copy). Writing a read-only register, or an unassigned number, is an illegal-instruction abort. Both instructions are **privileged**: executed outside kernel mode
    they abort with the privileged-instruction cause (§7.1). *Why one general pair and not more `MTxx`/`MFxx` words:* all sixteen `SYS` classes are in use; a general move costs two minors and leaves 8-bit register numbers
    for future registers (a vector base, a timer compare, an interrupt mask) without touching the `SYS` layout again. *Open, unchanged:* what `epc`, `eva` and `ecause` contain (the exception entry sequence is not yet designed; the system
    architecture document §4), the semantics of `ptbr` beyond "the base of the current page table", and which of `MTDMW`'s two windows an operand selects (`dmw0` and `dmw1` through `mtsr` are the unambiguous way; `MTDMW` is kept).
    The emulator models only a kernel/user flag for these instructions (`sim64 -k` runs in kernel mode), not the full privileged state.
33. **DECISION (the author's): wide constants are loaded from a literal pool, written with an explicit `=`; nothing is pooled silently.** An immediate wider than the signed 32-bit field (the fourth parcel is gone, errata 31)
    cannot be an operand. Two ways to supply it were considered: the compiler builds its own pool, or the assembler offers sugar. The author chose sugar, **provided it is obviously sugar**: `movq =K, rd` (ARM's `ldr =value`)
    assembles to one PC-relative load of a pooled 8-byte literal, in `.text`, flushed at the end of the section or at `.pool`; a bare too-wide `#K` is an error that points to `=K`. The first implementation of errata 31 pooled
    silently, which contradicted "one mnemonic assembles to one instruction" (ISA §4.14); the explicit `=` keeps the instruction single and the sugar visible. Allowed on the quad memory-source forms (`movq`, `push`, the ALU group,
    the floating-point ALU ops); refused for sized moves, shifts, stores, `lea` and relocatable symbols. The compiler emits `=K` for constants that do not fit.
34. **DECISION (the author's): `rori32` sign-extends, and `cbCC` gets a wide-immediate form.** (1) **`rori32 rs,#n,rd`** now gives `rd <- sign-extend( ror32( rs[31:0], n mod 32 ) )`, like `movl` and every other
    32-bit result: errata 31 had made it zero-extend, which forced the compiler (that keeps unsigned 32-bit values sign-extended, errata 28) to add a `movl` after each rotate that is not followed by code reading only the low 32 bits.
    (2) **A wide-immediate `cbCC`**: the same mnemonics, a new encoding in the reserved top-level opcode **`0x29`**, six bytes only (`len = 10`): parcel 0 `10 0x29 rd cond`, parcel 1 `imm:16` (signed, -32768 to 32767),
    parcel 2 `disp:16` (signed, in parcels from the first parcel of the instruction, +-32,768 parcels = +-64 KB). It compares `rd` with the sign-extended immediate exactly as `cmp rd,#imm` does and branches on the condition. The
    assembler chooses, for one `cbCC`, the shortest of three encodings: the four-byte form (`imm5`, `disp11`, opcode `0x28`), the six-byte wide form (`imm16`, `disp16`, opcode `0x29`), and the six-byte long form (`imm5`, `disp27`, opcode `0x28`
    at `len = 10`): the long form has the longest reach, the wide form the widest immediate. An immediate outside 16 bits, or a wide immediate whose target is beyond 64 KB, is an error ("use cmp and bCC"); the compiler therefore emits the
    wide form only for functions whose size is bounded below 64 KB. *Why:* 11,821 `cmp reg,imm` (immediates such as 255 and 47) were left unfused after the first `cbCC` (CHANGES §12). *What it will and will not do, stated before
    building:* a `cmp reg,imm16` (4 bytes) and its branch (2 bytes) are 6 bytes, as is the wide `cbCC`, so **it saves an instruction, not bytes** (about 11,800 instructions, 3.4%), except where the branch was four bytes. *Cost:* one of the seven
    reserved top-level opcodes (six remain: `0x2A` to `0x2F`).
35. **DECISION (the author's): a register-register compare-and-branch.** `cbCC ra,rb,label` (the same mnemonics as the immediate forms; the operand kind chooses the encoding, as for `add rs,rd` and `add #imm,rd`) compares
    `ra - rb` exactly as `cmp ra,rb` does (the same flags are written) and branches if the condition holds. New top-level opcode **`0x2A`** (five reserved remain: `0x2B` to `0x2F`). Format: parcel 0 `len 0x2A ra rb` (`reg` = `ra`, `low` = `rb`);
    at `len = 01` (4 bytes) parcel 1 is `cond:4 disp:12` (signed, in parcels from the first parcel of the instruction: +-2,048 parcels, +-4 KB); at `len = 10` (6 bytes) parcel 1 is `cond:4 disp-high:12` and parcel 2 `disp-low:16`, a 28-bit
    displacement (+-128 M parcels), so a compiler needs no function-size limit. The condition numbers are those of `bCC`, `set` and the other `cbCC` forms. The assembler chooses the shorter length that reaches; a target in another section is refused, as
    for the immediate forms. *Why:* the compiled corpus has 4,714 `cmp reg,reg ; bCC` pairs (1.4% of instructions) left after the immediate forms (CHANGES section 12). *What it will do, stated before building:* the pair is `cmp` (2 bytes) plus `bCC`
    (2 bytes, or 4 when far), the fused form is 4 bytes (6 when the target is farther than 4 KB), so it saves **one instruction per pair, and bytes only where the branch was four bytes** (at most about 9 KB, probably a few KB). The `cmp reg,mem ; bCC` pairs cannot use it (the memory operand has no room); the count of 5,356 first written here was wrong, the real count is 611.
36. **ACCEPTED 2026-10-22 (the author chose: register-based exception entry with `EPC` and `EPSW`; software-managed accessed and dirty bits; the whole of System v1 in the first QEMU cut): System v1, the design the QEMU memory-management unit and privileged mode, and a first kernel, are built against.** Written in
    [`pdp64-system-architecture.md`](pdp64-system-architecture.md) section 15: the PSW layout (flags `C V Z N` in bits 0 to 3, `T`, a 3-bit priority, current and previous mode with the PDP-11 encoding), the exception entry sequence (registers only: `EPC`, a new `EPSW`,
    `EVA`, `ECAUSE`; the new PC from the table at `VBR + 8*vector`; `RTI` returns), the vector numbering (`ECAUSE` = vector; traps at 256 + n), the page-table entry (flags in the low bits, the physical page number in place at bits 12 to 39), permissions, software-managed accessed and dirty bits,
    an `MMUCTL` enable bit, `TLBI` by address, three new system registers for `mfsr`/`mtsr` (`epsw` 8, `vbr` 9, `mmuctl` 10, plus `asid` 11 and `cpuid` 12), the list of privileged instructions, eight interrupt levels, a timer and a console device in the virt machine. *Status:* accepted as the design to implement; the author may still change any part of it after seeing it work. *Not in v1:* the direct mapping windows `DMW0` and `DMW1` have no defined register format yet, so in v1 they are stored by `mfsr`/`mtsr` and have no
    translation effect (kernels use ordinary page tables, for instance 1 GB leaf pages); the supervisor mode exists in the PSW and the banked registers but nothing uses it.

30a. **REVISED 2026-10-22 (the author): the first operating system is a port of 3BSD, not Unix/32V.** The reason: the 32V headers show that it swaps whole processes, and 3BSD (Unix/32V plus Berkeley's demand-paged virtual memory) already pages, so the port starts from the system that has the paging. The
    plan otherwise stands (errata 30, [`pdp64-system-architecture.md`](pdp64-system-architecture.md) section 12). *Unverified, to check when the sources are fetched:* that 3BSD was built with the portable C compiler, that its sources are available to the project and under what licence, and the details of its software emulation of reference bits.
37. **DECISION (the author's): `pushx` and `popx`, saving and restoring the exception state in one instruction each.** A handler that can be interrupted must save `EPC` and `EPSW` before it lowers the priority (System v1,
    errata 36); with only `mfsr` that costs two moves and two stores (four instructions, sixteen bytes) on every such kernel entry, and the same to restore. **`pushx`**: `SP <- SP - 16; M[SP] <- EPSW (zero-extended); M[SP + 8] <- EPC`, using the stack pointer of the
    current mode (in a kernel handler, the kernel stack). **`popx`**: `EPSW <- M[SP] (low 16 bits); EPC <- M[SP + 8]; SP <- SP + 16`. Both are privileged (vector 3 outside kernel mode) and write no flags. *Restart:* an abort leaves no architectural effect: the instruction checks both words
    (writable for `pushx`, readable for `popx`) before it stores or loads either, because a 16-byte aligned-to-8 push can straddle a page boundary, and updates `SP` only after both accesses; a misaligned `SP` is the misaligned-access abort. *Encoding:* the long form is a new escape in the `SYS` group, class 0 minor **15** (the last free minor),
    called **`SYSX`**, four bytes: parcel 1 is `sub:8 pad:8`, `sub` 0 = `pushx`, 1 = `popx`, any other `sub` or a non-zero `pad` is an illegal instruction; the two-byte forms are **page-2 opcodes `0x2A` (`pushx`) and `0x2B` (`popx`)**, exact abbreviations of the long forms with `reg` and `low` zero (a non-zero field is an illegal
    instruction), so the assembler writes two bytes. `SYSX` also gives the `SYS` group room for further system instructions without taking another class. Page 2 now has 43 of its 64 opcodes defined and 21 reserved. *Why:* about 12 bytes and 3 instructions saved on every nested kernel entry and exit (the cost identified in
    [`pdp64-system-design.md`](pdp64-system-design.md) D1); entry stays register-only, so it still cannot fault.
38. **DECISION (the author's): the FPGA and ASIC implementations are in-order; the architecture is to be kept ready for a large-scale out-of-order design.** The first implementations are the small in-order, non-speculative cores of the implementation plan. The ISA must not
    contain anything that would make a later wide out-of-order implementation (rename, reorder buffer, speculation, a sequentially consistent memory system with replay) impossible or needlessly slow, and decisions that cost an in-order core nothing but would cost an out-of-order core a great deal are to be made now. The
    readiness review is in [`pdp64-ooo-readiness.md`](pdp64-ooo-readiness.md); the design point, costs and gains are in [`pdp64-in-order-and-out-of-order.md`](pdp64-in-order-and-out-of-order.md). Concrete first candidate: the partial flag updates (a shift or rotate by a register count of zero leaves every flag
    unchanged, ISA 4.2.1; `inc`, `dec`, logic operations and moves leave `C` unchanged).
39. **DECISION (the author's): flag cleanup for out-of-order readiness, `ISYNC` and `HINT`.** From the readiness review ([`pdp64-ooo-readiness.md`](pdp64-ooo-readiness.md); the author adopted four of its five proposals on 2026-10-22). *The invariant:* the flags are **two groups, `C` and `{N, Z, V}`**;
    no instruction writes part of `{N, Z, V}` or writes it conditionally. *Changes:* (1) **`cbCC` (all three forms and the register form) writes no flags**: it computes `rd - operand` for its own decision and leaves `N Z V C` as they were. (2) **Moves, loads and stores write no flags**: `movq movl movw movb`, `movzl movzw movzb`, and the store forms (and their page-2
    abbreviations); `lea`, `set` already write none. (3) **`shl`, `shr`, `sar` write the same flags for every count**, including a count of zero (which gives `N` and `Z` from the unchanged operand, `V` = 0, `C` = 0), so there is no data-dependent flag write; this replaces "a count of zero changes no flag" (ISA 4.2.1). (4) **`mscn` and `mscnn` write all four flags**: `Z` as now (set when the byte was found), `N`, `V`
    and `C` cleared. (5) **`ISYNC`**: an instruction that makes earlier writes to `PTBR`, `ASID`, `MMUCTL`, `VBR` and earlier `ICINV`s take effect for the instructions that follow it; the architecture now promises the effect only after the next `ISYNC` (an in-order implementation may make it immediate; software must not rely on that);
    long form `SYSX` sub-opcode 2 (four bytes), page-2 abbreviation **`0x2C`** (two bytes). (6) **`HINT #n`** (`n` 0 to 255, no architectural effect): `SYSX` sub-opcode 3 with `n` in the `pad` byte (four bytes), page-2 abbreviation **`0x2D`** with `n` = `reg:low`; defined codes `0` plain no-op hint, `1` pause (spin-wait), `2` speculation barrier; every other code is a reserved
    hint that executes as a no-op (forward compatible; software shall not use it). (7) The **architecture-text items** of the review (section 2 of `pdp64-ooo-readiness.md`): rules for `LL`/`SC`/`ATOM` (reservation, forward progress, operands, what `_DB` adds under sequential consistency), no speculative loads from device pages and no side-effecting instruction fetch from them, `mcpy`/`mmov`/`mset`
    internal accesses unordered with respect to each other, `pushm` with `r14` in the mask and auto-modify with data register equal to base reserved, page-table entry changes visible only after `TLBI`; to be written into the ISA text. *Decided afterwards (the author, 2026-10-22):* **rotates (`ror rori rori32`) and `dshr` write no flags; `asl` keeps the PDP-11 rule `V = N xor C`** (all four flags written, every time). The core already does this; the ISA text (4.2.1) is corrected to match. *Costs, stated before building:* moves, loads and stores no longer set the flags that the compiler's `tst`-elision relied on: at most about 0.77% in code size (2 to 4 bytes at roughly 2,900 sites; no extra instructions); `cbCC` no longer provides flags to a following branch, so the compiler can fuse only where the flags are dead afterwards; tests that read flags after moves, `cbCC` and shifts by zero need rewriting.
40. **DECISION (the author's): exception and interrupt entry sets the priority to 7.** Found by the System v1 implementation review (2026-10-22): section 15 kept the old priority on exception entry, so a trap or fault from user mode at priority 0 could be interrupted by the timer before the handler's first `spl #7`, and the interrupt's entry would overwrite `EPC` and `EPSW`. A window of one instruction. *Alternative considered and
    rejected:* defer interrupts for one instruction after entry (an interrupt shadow): it needs hidden state, depends on the handler's first instruction being `spl #7`, and complicates a pipeline that tracks instructions out of order. *Decision:* the new `PSW` priority is **7 on every entry**, exceptions and interrupts alike (the review found the same hole for an interrupt: a higher-level interrupt could preempt a
    lower-level handler before it saved `EPC` and `EPSW`; the author's decision named exceptions, and I extended it to interrupts for that reason; narrowing it back is a one-line change). The handler lowers the priority with `SPL` when it is ready; the previous priority is in `EPSW` and `RTI` restores it. Cost: one `SPL` in a handler that wants to be interruptible, and one less in a handler that began with `spl #7` (all of them). *Not covered:* an exception in the handler's own first instructions; the entry code and the kernel stack must be pinned (section 15). *Throwback:* the PDP-11 loaded the new `PSW` from the vector, with priority 7 conventional for traps (from memory, unverified). Core, QEMU and the `sys-*` tests change accordingly.
41. **DECISION (the author's): the operating system order is OpenBSD first, Unix/32V as the fallback; 3BSD is dropped.** Supersedes errata 30 and 30a (their text stays as the history). The author's words: "forget 3bsd, we'll do 32v port if openbsd proves too hard, but i suspect it is doable." *Consequences:* (1) the LLVM back end is now the critical path to the first operating system (milestone 1, the machine-code layer, is done; selection, the calling convention, tied operands and clang are next, and need the larger machine; section 14.7 of the system architecture document);
    (2) the 3BSD licence question is no longer needed (the 32V fallback is covered by the 2002 Caldera licence, "up to and including Version 7 Unix (UNIX/32V)", per the fact-check); (3) items that OpenBSD's needs bring forward, to be decided one at a time and not yet decided: the `long double` representation before clang, position-independent code and `lld` relocations (OpenBSD defaults to PIE and enforces W^X, per the earlier check; whether it needs SMP or SMAP was not checked), a user-memory guard, an unprivileged time counter, and bus atomics only when going SMP.
    *Not checked:* how hard an OpenBSD bring-up is on a new architecture; my estimate (system architecture section 14.10) stays three to six months for the back end, and is an estimate.
42. **DECISION (the author's): every design rule is a soft rule; none is absolute.** The author's words: "the no mode bits rule is a very soft rule; there were benefits to it, as long as we keep those benefits, a mode bit is fine, and where mode bits outweigh the costs, we take them," and then "all the rules are soft rules." A rule is a default that records a benefit worth protecting and the failure it prevents; it yields when the benefit of breaking it outweighs the cost, with the cost written down (the test of ISA 1.1: the benefit is named and concrete, the cost is bounded and written down, and the author judges the balance). *Consequences:* (1) ISA 1.1 no longer lists any rule as having no exceptions: the two it listed, **no mode bits** (what it protected: state saved and restored at every context switch, and a bit that changes the meaning of unrelated instructions) and **natural alignment of quad accesses** (what it protected: a trapped bug instead of silent corruption in tagged runtimes), are now the strongest defaults, and each is reopened by its cost-benefit and not by its status. (2) A mode bit that keeps the protected benefits (a machine-wide bit set once at boot, a bit in the `PSW` that is saved anyway) is accepted without further argument, and one whose benefit outweighs its cost is accepted with the cost written down. (3) Earlier choices made by a rule ("conversion instructions, never mode bits", no rounding-mode or precision bits in floating point, the one-memory-operand rule) stand but may be reopened one at a time, and a decision document should weigh them as costs and benefits, not cite the rule as a reason.
43. **DECISION (the author's picks, 2026-10-22; decisions-pending P2): a user-memory guard, `UA`, with two instructions.** Closes the weak point D11 of the system design document. *Spec:* `PSW` bit 12 is **`UA`** ("user access allowed"). With `UA` = 0 a kernel-mode data access (load, store, atomic, block-instruction operand) to a page whose `U` bit is set raises the page permission abort, reported as such in `ECAUSE`; with `UA` = 1 it behaves as today. Instruction fetch from user pages by the kernel stays forbidden (the existing rule). `PROBER` and `PROBEW` are not affected by `UA`. User-mode code is not affected (the bit is ignored outside kernel mode). Two `SYSX` words (class 0 minor 15), four bytes, privileged, no flags, `pad` = 0 else illegal instruction: **`stua`** (sub-opcode 4) sets `UA`, **`clua`** (sub-opcode 5) clears it. Exception and interrupt entry **clear `UA`** in the new `PSW`; `EPSW` keeps the old value and `RTI` restores it; `MTPS` writes it like the other `PSW` bits. Reset value 0 (guard on).
    *Why a mode bit is acceptable here (errata 42):* the bit is in the `PSW`, which is saved and restored anyway, so there is no new state to switch, and its only effect is to turn a class of kernel bugs into faults. *Cost:* a copy routine must open and close the window, and every error path out of it must close it (entry clearing it bounds the damage). *Consequences to implement:* the core, grammar (`SYSX` subs 4 and 5, `PSW` bit), QEMU, the simulator, the LLVM generator and tests; the existing test `sys-pushx` uses `SYSX` sub 4 as its "reserved word" and must move to a different reserved sub-opcode (6 or higher). Page-2 forms are not allocated.
44. **DECISION (the author's, from the principle of errata 42; decisions-pending P10): `MMUCTL` stays; the `DMW` format is deferred.** `MMUCTL` bit 0 (translation enable, reset 0) is accepted as a deliberate mode bit: it is machine-wide, set once at boot, not part of any thread's state and not saved at a context switch, and it changes the meaning of no instruction a program executes (it only decides whether addresses are translated), so the benefits the rule protected are kept. It follows the PDP-11's `SR0` enable bit and the VAX's `MAPEN`. The weak point D9 of the system design document is closed as accepted. `DMW0`/`DMW1` stay stored-only registers (System v1, errata 36); their format is defined when a kernel needs a window, not before. ISA 8.3's sentence that the machine comes up with `DMW0` covering physical memory is superseded for System v1 by "`MMUCTL` = 0: every address is physical". *My earlier recommendation to remove `MMUCTL` rested on the absolute reading of the rule and is withdrawn.*
45. **DECISION (the author's picks, 2026-10-22; decisions-pending P11): atomics are defined on a monitor (reservation), not a bus lock.** *Spec:* `LL` establishes a reservation on a naturally aligned granule of the implementation's choice (not smaller than 8 bytes); a write to the granule by **any initiator** (another core, a device, an atomic) clears it; `SC` succeeds exactly when the reservation is intact and otherwise writes nothing. The read-modify-write atomics (`CAS`, `SWAP`, `XADD` and the others) are single-copy atomic with respect to every initiator; the implementation may build them from an exclusive access with hardware retry. A constrained `LL`/`SC` sequence (short, no intervening memory access or exception) must eventually succeed when no other initiator keeps writing the granule (the guarantee RISC-V gives, per the verified RISC-V manual). `DATIP`/`DATO` are no longer an architectural primitive; a bus may still implement the atomics with them or with AXI exclusive access, which is decided separately (decisions-pending R4). The `_DB` variants keep their meaning (the atomic plus a barrier) and, under sequential consistency, are a convenience. *Still to write:* the ISA text (open item 4, 17.6), granule discovery in the feature registers (decisions-pending P4), the reservation tests in the emulators. *Cost:* a reservation rule for every initiator, and forward-progress rules; a device that writes shared words takes part by clearing reservations.
46. **DECISION (the author's pick, 2026-10-22; decisions-pending P13): `long double` stays the same as `double`; the ABI version is the door.** ABI version 1 (`abi.md`) keeps `long double` = `double`. A change to a 128-bit `long double` (binary128, in software) is an incompatible ABI change and so ABI **version 2**; the library, compiler and runtime support for it come with that version, not before. The ABI version is to be carried in the object format (ELF `e_flags`, low byte, value 1 for all objects built now): `as64` writes it, `ld64` refuses to link objects of different versions; the fields are defined when this is implemented. Matches the Apple and Windows ARM64 position and costs nothing now; the per-port variation of OpenBSD (powerpc64 64-bit, arm64 and riscv64 binary128, per the verification pass) is noted for the port.
47. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the boot software is open source and part of the specification.** The author's words: "we'll have an open source boot sector boot loader as part of the spec; that is part of the spec that the boot loader/bios is open source." The boot ROM and its monitor (the pdp64's "BIOS"), the first-block program, the second-stage loader and the boot tools (`installboot` and the like) are published with the project, built from source by the project's own toolchain, and form part of what the specification defines; no closed firmware sits in the boot path. The licence is chosen by errata 48. *Recorded with the boot review:* [`boot.md`](boot.md); the disk-layout question (B6) is open there.
48. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): BSD licence for everything.** The author's words: "bsd license for everything." The whole project (the toolchain, the simulator, the QEMU and LLVM target sources kept here, the grammars, the tests, the documents, and the boot software of errata 47) is under the **BSD 2-clause licence**, in the file `COPYING`, copyright 2026 David Walther. *The variant (2-clause) and the copyright line are my choice of the common default, not stated by the author; changing them is one file.* *What was done with it:* `COPYING` added; the 11 QEMU target files that carried QEMU's conventional `SPDX-License-Identifier: GPL-2.0-or-later` now say `BSD-2-Clause` (BSD is compatible with the GPL, so the files can still be built into QEMU; the resulting QEMU binary as a whole stays under QEMU's own licence). *Notes, not decisions:* the pcc back end lives in the pcc repository under pcc's own BSD licence; if the LLVM target is ever offered to LLVM it would have to be relicensed under LLVM's Apache-2.0 with LLVM exceptions, which the author may do as the copyright holder; per-file licence headers are not yet added to the other source files.
49. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22; the eight registers superseded by errata 95): the bus is a device; it enumerates, maps and alerts.** The author's words: "since drivers are driving devices accessed through 8 mmapped registers, they should be trivially small, so stage2 should be fine; and the bus itself is another device with its own set of mmap registers so we can detect what is on it and request it to map a device to a particular address range; we do need some way for the bus to alert us when a device enters or leaves the bus." *Decided:* (1) drivers are trivially small because devices are eight registers, so the second-stage loader carries its own drivers (this settles the cost noted in `boot.md`, B1); (2) **the bus controller is a device with eight memory-mapped registers** through which the processor detects what is on the bus and asks it to map a device to a given address range; (3) **the bus alerts the processor when a device enters or leaves.** *Open (proposals in `pdp64-q64-bus.md` section 13, decisions N1 to N3):* how addresses are assigned, the alert mechanism, and what happens to accesses to a departed device.
50. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the disk uses a GPT wrapper around the BSD disk label.** The author's words: "we will have the gpt wrapper for the disk label." The disk carries a **GUID partition table with a protective MBR**; the OS's own partition (the OpenBSD type) holds the **BSD disk label** (OpenBSD's, errata 48 licence applies to the project's tools), which names the root, swap and other partitions. This is option 2 of `boot.md` decision B6. *Consequences:* (1) block 0 is the protective MBR, so the first-stage bootstrap lives in its 446 boot-code bytes (decision B8, proposal: a block list there, the second stage in a small boot partition, no GPT parsing in the ROM or the first stage); (2) sector counts are 64-bit throughout (GPT's LBAs and the label's high fields); (3) the second stage and the kernel need a GPT reader (OpenBSD has one in `kern/subr_disk.c`); (4) the disk is recognised by other operating systems and partitioning tools, which was the point. *Open:* B8 (how the first stage finds the second stage), the partition type identifier for the boot partition, and the exact layout; the GPT details are not confirmed against the UEFI specification.
51. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): follow the majority for the boot-block and label offsets.** The author's words: "follow the majority as far as boot block offset." *Decided:* inside the BSD partition the **label is at sector 1, offset 0** (seven of the nine architecture headers in OpenBSD's tree, against Alpha and SPARC64 at sector 0), the **boot area is 8192 bytes at the start of the partition** (the BSD `BBSIZE`: sector 0 the primary bootstrap, sectors 1 to 15 the label and a secondary bootstrap), and the **file system starts after it with its superblock at offset 8192**. The port's header is `sys/arch/pdp64/include/disklabel.h` with `LABELSECTOR` 1 and `LABELOFFSET` 0. *Source of the counts:* [`boot.md`](boot.md), "Disk layout and partitions". This does not decide where the disk-level first stage lives (errata 50, decision B8).
52. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the goal before silicon.** The author's words: "this pdp64 project final goal (before we put it on actual silicon) is assembler, linker, compiler backend (llvm), emulator and port of openbsd we can ssh into." *Recorded as the project's finish line:* an assembler, a linker, an LLVM compiler back end, an emulator, and a port of OpenBSD that can be logged into with `ssh`. The roadmap, the state of each piece, the order of work and the decisions that gate each step are in [`roadmap.md`](roadmap.md). *A consequence noted there:* the Unix/32V fallback of errata 41 would not reach this goal (it has no network stack), so the LLVM back end is on the critical path with no fallback to the ssh goal.
53. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): a comprehensive manual is a deliverable.** The author's words: "a deliverable is a comprehensive manual showing every opcode, explaining what it does, how it works, rationale for it being included, the whole story behind it; and the stories for clusters of instructions and decisions, and the bus and bios/boot stuff, and the default bus/disk/usb/nic drivers (8 register) dma/mmap design." *Recorded as a deliverable alongside the five of errata 52.* The plan (what it contains, how it is produced so that its numbers cannot drift from the grammar, the order of work, and the parts that need design before they can be documented) is in [`roadmap.md`](roadmap.md), section "The manual".
54. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the architecture manual is the primary deliverable.** The author's words: "right now the important part is the design and docs is most important, the as/ld/cc/emu parts are advanced as needed to do the docs", then: "you could say the manual for the arch (not only the isa) is the primary deliverable, but the as/ld/cc/emu are also important." *Recorded:* the **manual for the architecture, not only the instruction set**, is the project's primary deliverable (errata 53); the assembler, linker, compiler back ends and emulator are also important and are advanced as needed to write and check it, and the finish line of errata 52 stands. The work order in [`roadmap.md`](roadmap.md) puts design and documentation first.
55. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the published form of the manual.** The author's words: "it will be a directory of chapters on the site (open source, remember?) and also a book, compiled into typst using my buildabook repo", and "perhaps the book might be in multiple volumes; we'll see how the word count comes out." *Decided:* (1) the manual is **a directory of chapters on the project's site**, open source under the project licence (errata 48); (2) it is **also a book**, built to Typst with the author's `buildabook` tool (`../buildabook`, github.com/moebiusV/buildabook); (3) the book **may be several volumes**, decided when the word count is known. *How it fits:* [`roadmap.md`](roadmap.md), section "The manual", describes the layout (chapter sources in Markdown with `buildabook`'s cross-reference tokens, one outline, the resolved Markdown published as the site's chapters and the same sources compiled to a PDF through Typst).
56. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the manual's style is aphorism and commentary.** The author's words: "the style will be like the old sanskrit books, an aphorism followed by commentary, or mishnah and gemarah. so the main book will be an easy read of lots of straightforward things; the commentary in each subsection will describe the historical precedents, the tradeoffs, the improvements, the benefits, and the modern comparables, and what the instruction is mainly used for (if it is a rare instruction; things like mov and add and mul are pretty obvious)." *Recorded as the form of the manual and of the manual's chapters (then called the streamlined set; renamed by errata 70)* ([`manual/`](manual/00-index.md)): each section states its **aphorisms**, short numbered statements that can be read straight through as the main book, and then a **commentary** with fixed labels: *Precedent*, *Trade-offs*, *Improvements*, *Benefits*, *Modern comparables*, and, for an instruction that is not obvious, *Use*. The reference entry of every instruction (errata 53) takes the same form.
57. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the reference part of the manual follows the classic CPU manuals; the PowerPC 601 manual is the model.** The author's words: "also the style will be like those old cpu manuals, the powerpc 601 manual was an excellent example of a cpu manual." *Recorded as the form of the reference entries* (to be added to errata 56): every instruction gets an entry in a fixed layout (the layout of such manuals, as I recall the 601 User's Manual: the mnemonic and full name, the assembler syntax, an **encoding diagram with the bit fields**, the **operation in pseudocode**, a description, the **registers and flags altered**, the **exceptions**), every register and format a diagram with bit positions, and the appendices carry **summaries of the whole instruction set by mnemonic and by opcode**, a register summary, a glossary and an index; the aphorism-and-commentary form of errata 56 sits on top of that layout. *Not checked:* I have not opened the 601 manual here; the layout described is from memory and should be confirmed against a copy before the entry template is fixed.
58. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): every decision is explained by its impact on the kernel, the compiler and the hardware layout.** The author's words: "the manual is written with him in mind (a CPU designer who thinks all ISAs are equivalent; this is to show him how wrong he is), the sections explain every decision in light of how it impacts efficient kernel, efficient compilation, and efficient hardware layout." *Recorded as a standing requirement of the manual* (added to errata 56 and 57): the commentary of every section that carries a decision ends with an **Impact** line giving the effect on an efficient *kernel*, on efficient *compilation* and on an efficient *hardware layout*, and the introduction states the case that the instruction set is not interchangeable, with the measurements ([`manual/01-introduction.md`](manual/01-introduction.md), section 1.6).
59. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): vector, graphics and AI work goes the memory-mapped device route.** The author's words: "i agree we should go the mmap device arm style route for that, so it is about graphics and ai/llm stuff... also mention apl/k as making vector languages easier to support and more efficient." *Decided* (this closes decision S1 of `decisions-pending.md` in favour of its option 1, and with it S2, S3, S4 and S6 for the processor): the processor has **no vector register file and no SIMD instruction set** (no SSE, AVX2 or NEON equivalent); wide data-parallel work is done by **memory-mapped devices** driven by descriptors, as ARM-based systems attach their neural-network and graphics engines; the workloads are graphics, AI and large-language-model inference, and the **array languages APL and K**, whose whole-array primitives map one-to-one onto descriptors and which are easier to support and more efficient on such a device. *Open:* the formats and operation set of the vector engine (S5), the reconciliation of the accelerators' descriptors with the standard descriptor, whether `BLT64` conforms to the eight-register convention, and whether packed integer lanes are added (appendix A.10). *Where:* [`manual/08-vectors.md`](manual/08-vectors.md). *Interpretation noted:* "arm style" is read as the way ARM-based systems attach accelerators as memory-mapped devices.
60. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22; decision P19; began as the author's idea; superseded by errata 97): the top physical-address bit selects device space.** The author asked: "perhaps that bit 40 of the bus could be for mmio if set, if not set, regular dram. what do you think?" and, on hearing of the VAX's arrangement, asked that it be noted in the book. **Decided on 2026-10-22: the author confirmed "bit 39 is right"** (P19 closed): bit 39 of the physical address selects device space when set and memory when clear. *Still to do:* the system-architecture text and the QEMU memory map. *Also recorded from the same message:* the graphics terminal is a modernised Commodore 64 with a SID-style sound device, 48-bit pixels and 48-bit audio, and HDMI (like SATA and USB) is standard kit behind a small set of memory-mapped registers; a standard graphics card is therefore an open question, not a refusal. *Book order set by the author:* introduction, bus, graphics, boot, device drivers, kernel, SMP, vectors, instruction set, conclusion. The register-level C64 documentation was not found and is a stub in [`manual/03-graphics.md`](manual/03-graphics.md) section 3.2.
61. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22; superseded by errata 95): storage interfaces follow the one device pattern, and eight registers is a minimum, not a limit.** The author's words: "scsi/ata/sata/nvme all follow the same device pattern, and not limited to 8 reg mmio, mmio allows larger windows as needed." *Decided:* SCSI, ATA, SATA and NVMe are all the ring-and-descriptor device of chapter 5 with their own command encodings (NVMe's queues being the closest existing form); a device maps as many pages of register window as it needs, with the common first eight registers (ID, CONTROL, STATUS, RING_BASE, RING_SIZE, DOORBELL, IRQ, DEVICE) in the same place in every device. This refines errata 29 and the eight-register wording elsewhere (read "eight" as "at least eight"). *Where:* [`manual/05-drivers.md`](manual/05-drivers.md) sections 5.1.1 and 5.2.1.
62. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the manual includes the full instruction-set dictionary, and the atomic, system and fault material lives in the multiprocessor and kernel chapters.** The author's words: "no no this is the full reference manual, don't skip on the instruction set dictionary!", "atomics belongs in the smp chapter i think, and system instructions, faults and memory management belong in the kernel chapter", "but the instructions chapter can refer to them, especially in the sections for each relevant instruction, put a cf chapter...". *Done:* (1) the dictionary is [`manual/dictionary/`](manual/dictionary/index.md), one entry per instruction (syntax, encoding diagram, operation, flags, exceptions, status, *Cf.* pointer), the second page, the aliases, and appendices by mnemonic and by opcode; it is **generated** by `tools/mkmanual.py` from `spec/pdp64-mnemonics.hbnf` (the numbers) and `tools/manual/isa_*.py` (the words, checked against `common/pdp64exec.c`), and `tools/check-all.sh` fails if a mnemonic has no entry, an entry names a mnemonic that does not exist or a generated file is stale. (2) The atomic instructions are book section 7.12, the system instructions 6.7 and faults, restart and memory management 6.8; chapter 9 keeps short sections 9.13 to 9.15 that point to them, and its other instruction sections carry *Cf.* lines. *Found while writing it:* the reference executor does not implement the atomic group (`ATOM`), the memory escape (`MESC`), the UTF-8 instructions or `MVAL`, `FCLASS32`; the dictionary marks each entry as implemented or not by reading the executor; the operand placement of the atomics is not fixed in the instruction-set text; `STUA` and `CLUA` (errata 43) are not in the grammar.
63. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-22): the instruction-set chapter contains all the instructions, and bit layouts are drawings.** The author's words: "the instruction set chapter is missing all the instructions! also, the bit layout diagram shouldn't be ascii art, you can draw tables, use proper line drawings". *Supersedes the layout of errata 62:* the entries are no longer separate group pages; they are generated **into the sections of the chapters**: moves 9.6, integer arithmetic 9.7, logic and shifts 9.8, bits and text 9.9, branches and transfers 9.10, stack and block operations 9.11, floating point 9.12, the atomic instructions 7.12 and the system instructions 6.7 (between `<!-- entries:N.N -->` markers; the chapters stay readable as plain Markdown). `docs/manual/dictionary/` keeps the reference tables (second page, aliases, constants, the mnemonics and the opcodes). Every encoding and the processor status word are drawn by `tools/mkmanual.py` as SVG line drawings (`docs/manual/figures/`, one box per field, widths proportional to the bits, bit numbers above, numeric fixed values shaded); the three ASCII diagrams of chapter 9 are replaced by figures. `tools/check-all.sh` fails if an entry, a table, a figure or a chapter block is stale.
64. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-23 UTC): every instruction that is not common carries the story of why it is in the instruction set.** The author's words: "every instruction (except for the common ones) should have the story of why it was chosen to be in the isa, based on the the archive of this chat". *Done:* each instruction entry (book chapters 6, 7 and 9) that is not common (the PDP-11 core and the operations every architecture has carry none) is followed by **Why it is here**, written from the record and nothing else: the author's questions in the original design chat (`chat01.txt`), the author's messages in the archive of the working session (the transcript; dates are its own, in UTC), the revision changelogs of the instruction-set text, the errata, and the measurements of `CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md` section 14. Where the record gives no reason the paragraph says so; where the record is the assistant's own answer from memory it says that too. The stories are `tools/manual/isa_stories.py`; the story of the second page is in the reference tables.
65. **PROPOSAL (not yet confirmed by the author; implemented 2026-10-23 as part of decision P19, errata 60): the memory type of a leaf must agree with the space bit.** Done: (1) the device pages of the virt machine moved into the upper half of the 40-bit physical space: `MAP_DEV` and `MAP_TIMER` are `0x8000000000` (1 << 39), `MAP_CONSOLE` is `0x8000001000`, `MAP_DEV_END` is `0x8010000000`; RAM stays at 0 (`spec/pdp64-system.hbnf`, rule `map`, which also names the space bit, `MAP_SPACE_BIT` = 39); the constants are no longer written with a `ULL` suffix, so the assembler can read them. (2) The immediate and absolute operand fields are 32 bits sign-extended, so a test loads these addresses with the literal pool, `movq =MAP_TIMER, r13`. `MAP_DEV_END` in `tests/sim/sys-exc.S` and `sys-pushx.S` means "the first address that no memory or device answers" (a bus fault), and keeps that meaning. (3) **The proposed rule:** a leaf whose physical page has bit 39 set must have memory type device (2) or device-weak (3); one whose bit 39 is clear must have cached (0) or uncached (1); any other combination is a protection fault of the access type (`IPROT`, `LPROT`, `SPROT`), checked after the format checks (valid, reserved bits, `W` without `R`, superpage alignment) and before the permission and the accessed/dirty bits. It is one inline function, `pdp64_pte_space_ok`, in `common/pdp64exec.h`, used by QEMU's walker (`qemu/target-pdp64/mmu.c`); tests `tests/qemu/mmu-space.S` and the memory-type pages of the permission matrices. With translation off (`MMUCTL` bit 0 clear) a physical address with bit 39 set simply reaches the devices. A virtual address cannot name device space (a canonical virtual address has bit 39 equal to bit 38), so the kernel maps devices through a device-typed leaf; `tests/qemu/mini-kernel.S` does so with a 1 GB leaf. *If the author rejects the rule*, remove the call in `mmu.c` and the matrix model line `mt >= 2`; nothing else depends on it.
66. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-23): the terminal is based on the Blit as well as the C64.** The author's words, noticing that the graphics chapter named only one: "i notice the c64 didn't mention we are basing the design not only on the c64, but also on the blit terminal". *Recorded:* the graphics terminal is based on two machines, the Commodore 64 (video and sound as chips with registers: `VIC-64X`, `SID-64X`) and the Blit terminal (a framebuffer in the address space with a bit-block-transfer engine: `BLT64`), both modernised and extended. The chapter ([`manual/03-graphics.md`](manual/03-graphics.md), statement 3.1.1, the opening, the precedent commentary and the stub of section 3.2), the index, the introduction and the glossary say so. Its Blit side remains as unspecified as the C64 side: the bit-block-transfer operations and the windowing model are to be written down from the author's sources. *Addendum (the author, the same day):* "the blit was an m68k that received code and pixels over a serial link; precursor to X11 protocol and NeWS window system"; recorded in the chapter's precedent commentary and the glossary as the author's account. *Research addendum (round 6, 2026-10-23, with the author's agreement to apply the edits):* the primary papers confirm the 68000 and the serial link and the downloaded code, say the terminal drew every pixel itself, give the Blit no hardware blit engine, and do not state the X11/NeWS lineage; chapter 3 (3.1.6 to 3.1.8, the commentary) and the glossary were corrected to say so, and the claim is kept as the author's. See `kernel-pain-points.md` 14I.
67. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-23): Q64 connects arbitrary buses, which are enumerated through it.** The author's words: "just as usb is enumerated over a pcie bus, our q64 bus will allow arbitrary buses to be connected to it and enumerated." *Decided:* a device on Q64 may itself be a bus (a bridge), presenting the devices behind it as slots with the registers of the bus controller, to any depth; the kernel's enumeration is one recursive walk. *Not yet specified:* how a slot's type says it is a bus, how arrivals and departures behind a bridge are reported, and how the windows behind a bridge map into device space (stub statement 2.6.8). *Where:* [`manual/02-bus.md`](manual/02-bus.md) section 2.6 (statements 2.6.7 and 2.6.8 and their commentary, with the VAX Unibus adapter as precedent, read in the 4.3BSD sources `vaxuba/ubareg.h` and `vax/autoconf.c`).
68. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-23): the architecture is designed to be efficient with in-order and out-of-order implementations; instructions are judged by hot-loop weight.** The author's words: "although the first implementations will be in-order, we have to make the decision safe for out of order implementations"; "a rarely used instruction can still pull its weight if its used inside a critical tight hot loop"; and, on the official documents: "designed to be efficient with in-order and out-of-order execution implementations". *Decided:* manual statements 1.4.4 and 1.4.5 and the README design paragraph. *Consequence, proposed and not yet decided:* [`architecture-recommendations.md`](architecture-recommendations.md) section 3 (a split translation root so the kernel is not walked in user mode, and twelve rules that make today's in-order safeties hold for an out-of-order core) and section 4 (keep rarely used instructions unless a profile-weighted measurement shows no hot loop uses them).
69. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-23): every instruction's official entry lives in the instruction chapter (chapter 9), complete and with its diagram.** The author's words: "every instruction should be fully documented with diagrams in the instructions sections, the other sections can describe their instructions in as much as their rationale, history, provenance, etc, but the official entry must live in the instructions section of the manual"; and "an entry in the instructions section may point back to other chapters for fuller explanation of the instruction." *Done:* the atomic and ordering entries (formerly in section 7.12) and the system-instruction entries (formerly in section 6.7) are now generated into sections 9.13 and 9.14; sections 6.7 and 7.12 keep their numbered statements and now list the entries and link to them. The dictionary links point to the new places. An entry may keep its *Cf.* line to another chapter, but it is complete without it. *Not done, stated plainly:* the atomic entries still say that the operand placement of each form is not fixed (an open item from before this change, not a new gap); the diagrams are of the encoding, one per entry, and a group's entry shares one diagram among its mnemonics (the sub-opcode table beside it lists each mnemonic). *Where:* `tools/mkmanual.py` (`HOME`), [`manual/09-isa.md`](manual/09-isa.md) sections 9.13 and 9.14.
70. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-23): the manual is the official manual, so `docs/streamlined/` is renamed `docs/manual/`.** The author's words: "move the manual out of the \"streamlined/\" dir to the \"manual/\" dir because it is the full official manual, not \"streamlined\"". *Done:* `git mv docs/streamlined docs/manual` (history follows); every path in the README, the handoff, the tools (`mkmanual.py`, `mkchapters.py`), the book sources, outline, template and README, and the research documents was rewritten; the manual's title and index no longer say "streamlined". *Not changed:* the data records under `docs/codegen-data/` (including the round-6 ledger) keep the old path where they cite it, because they record what was read then; earlier errata that say "streamlined" are history. The online address is now `https://moebiusv.github.io/pdp64/docs/manual/00-index.html`; a one-page stub at the old address, `docs/streamlined/00-index.md`, points to it. The PDF in git was not rebuilt (the author builds it).
71. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-23): the eight manual edits proposed by research round 7 are accepted.** The author's words: "i'll take all 8" (of the list in `kernel-pain-points.md` 14M.8). *Applied as numbered statements, each tagged to this erratum:* 3.1.9 (frame-boundary registers shadowed, one `ARM`, missed-arm status, underrun bit and counter, vertical blank at a fixed line; 3.1.8 now defers to it) and 3.3.4 (the audio clock relation N and CTS); 2.4.4 and 5.7.4 (the interrupt write cannot overtake the data writes; a moved interrupt target is masked and read back or written atomically); 7.2.6 (sequential consistency per byte, single-copy atomicity, a load overlapping a buffered store of a different size) and 9.13.2 (`CAS` is 4 or 8 bytes and may span two 4-byte fields; the `CAS` entry says so); 6.4.9 (split translation: bit 38 selects `PTBR` or the new `KTBR`, the upper half is never walked in user mode) and 9.3.7 (the `DIT` bit with the contract of `architecture-recommendations.md` section 6.2); 4.4.4 (the handoff record of the video device); 2.6.9 (a bus reset produces no slot events); 7.8.4 (per-core class and capacity register, stop-and-drain register). *Not done, stated plainly:* none of this is yet in the specification files (`spec/`), the reference executor, `sim64`, QEMU, the assembler or the LLVM back end; the encodings are open (the `CAS` size forms, the position of `DIT`, the number of `KTBR`, the registers of items 1, 7 and 8), and the existing tests and the mini-kernel map the kernel in the lower half, which `KTBR` will change when it is implemented. The statements say "not yet in the specification files" where that is so. Implementation is queued.
72. **RECORD (2026-10-24): the author's device chat of 2026-08-29 was read; conflicts with the repository are listed, not decided.** `c64stuff.txt` (the author's exported chats) was compared with the documents. *Recorded:* (1) the chat's device decisions are extracted in `docs/c64-devices-from-chat.md`; (2) `decisions-pending.md` Part E lists eleven conflicts (E1 to E11), each with the author's words and the line in `c64stuff.txt`; the manual statements involved (3.1.2 to 3.1.5, 10.1.2) are re-tagged *(P)* until the author rules, and nothing was decided; (3) **little-endian is the author's own decision** ("we go full little endian, none of this middle endian stuff, that gets fixed right now"), not a gap-fill of ours (`CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md` 2.2 corrected); (4) **errata 27 does not exist**: the numbering runs 26, 28. It is cited for `binary16` ("added at the user's request") in `CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md`, `decisions-pending.md` and `fact-check-2026-10-22.md`, but no entry or quoted request was found; the author is asked to confirm that request (the text of the entry cannot be reconstructed from the repository); (5) errata 31 (the fourth parcel) records the author's decision but quotes none of the author's words; (6) stale lines annotated: `pdp64-q64-bus.md` (`DATIP`/`DATO`, errata 45), `pdp64-isa.md` (bit 39, in-order claim), `manual/02-bus.md` 2.1.6 (decided by errata 60), `pdp64-fpga-plan.md` (instruction length distribution predates errata 31). *Still open:* ISA section 1 still ranks transistor count first and code density last (E9); data alignment (E2) was never answered.
73. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-24): the VIC-64X pixel is 48 bits, RGBA, 12 bits per channel; the device chat is to be read as written for the earlier, smaller machine.** The author's words: "c64 stuff was written before the decision to have a 39 bit address space; we were still thinking of a pdp-11 emulation with very conservative extensions. the decisions there should be read in that light. and the 48 bit is a new decision of mine, rgba format, 12 bits per color/channel". *Recorded:* (1) the pixel is four 12-bit channels (red, green, blue, alpha); this settles the source of the 48 bits that errata 60 recorded without a quotation, and 3.1.2 is *(D)*; (2) the 3.4.2 frame size and the FPGA plan table now also give the 48-bit figure (12.4 MB at 1920 by 1080); (3) `decisions-pending.md` E1 is closed, and Part E and `c64-devices-from-chat.md` carry the note that the chat predates the 39-bit address space and the 64-bit ISA. *Still open:* the SID-64X sample width (errata 60 says 48 bits; the statement above covers the pixel), and whether indexed and 565 formats and the text cell's colour width remain.
74. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-10-24): sound is 48-bit, and is not only the SID.** The author's words: "yes, the sid/c64 is a device on the bus, but also i want 48 bit sound as well, not just the sid." *Recorded:* (1) the C64-derived parts (the VIC and the SID, and any 8502 compatibility island) are devices on the bus, not part of the ISA; (2) the sound device offers a general sound path with **48-bit samples** in addition to SID-style voices and filters, so it is not limited to what a SID can do; 3.1.3 is *(D)*, which settles the sample width left open in errata 60 and 73. *Still open:* what a 48-bit sample is (a single 48-bit integer, floating point, or a stereo frame of two 24-bit halves), the sample rates, and how SID voices mix into the 48-bit path.
75. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03; the NVMe disk superseded by errata 101): Q64 is built on PCI Express, NVMe and USB; the C64-derived devices are iteration two.** The author's words: "radical change; we're going with pcie and nvme and usb for our q64 bus; if there are any improvements or niceties we can make with overlays or convenience registers/functions we will. iommu will allow us to map in c64/c128 and i think we should, but that is extraneous; whatever is the most standard, secure, easy to support nic and gpu and audio interfaces will be standardized for iteration one; our 12 register nic and sound card and graphics card are planned for the future; i like the blit model." and, the same day: "so, 48 bit pixels, 48 bit audio samples.... for the future, same time as the 12 register devices". *Recorded:* (1) the peripheral bus is PCI Express, with NVMe storage and USB; (2) overlays and convenience registers are welcome where they help; (3) an IOMMU is part of the design, and later maps a C64/C128 window (extraneous, so deferred); (4) iteration one uses the most standard, secure, easy-to-support network, graphics and audio interfaces; (5) the 12-register network, sound and graphics cards, the 48-bit RGBA pixel (errata 73), the 48-bit audio sample (errata 74) and the Blit model are **one bundle, planned for the future** (iteration two); chapter 3 carries a note to that effect. *Superseded in part, pending the answers in `decisions-pending.md` Part F:* errata 29 (eight-register devices), the slot enumeration and slot-derived vectors of chapters 2 and 5, errata 67 in its present form, decisions R1, R4, R5, R6 and Q10, and Part E entries E4, E5, E7 and E8. *Not done yet:* the rewrite of the bus, drivers, boot and FPGA chapters, which waits for those answers.
76. **PROPOSAL (the author's recommendation, 2026-11-03): dedicated cores for graphics, the network and audio, then general-purpose cores.** The author's words: "we recommend one core dedicated to the gpu/graphics device, one core dedicated to the nic, running the full tcp/ip stack, then a bunch of other cores for general processing." and, a message later: "and also one core for audio processing, doing midi, dsp, etc." *Recorded as a proposal:* (1) one core runs the graphics device (the Blit model, where the terminal has a processor of its own); (2) one core owns the network interface and runs the whole TCP/IP stack; (3) one core does audio: MIDI, digital signal processing and the like; (4) the remaining cores do general processing. *Read with:* the cores are the same architecture (the core-class register of manual 7.8.4 already allows cores to differ in class and capacity); the network core drives a standard PCI Express network card (errata 75), so no network hardware beyond that is implied; this partly restores the idea of the device chat's KE64 (a network card running the TCP stack), now as a core and not as fixed hardware (`decisions-pending.md` E4). *Open:* `decisions-pending.md` F16 to F21 (does iteration one have it, how the dedicated cores share memory with the others, what runs the stack, how many cores, the IOMMU domains).
77. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): the C128 is just another device on the bus, not part of the pdp64 standard.** The author's words: "the c128 will just be another device on the bus; not part of the pdp64 standard". *Recorded:* the C128 (its 8502 core, 8722 memory management, VDC and the compatibility island of `c64-devices-from-chat.md` section 4) is an optional PCI Express device, reached through the IOMMU window of errata 75; the pdp64 standard (the ISA, the memory model and the iteration-one devices) neither contains nor depends on it. No instruction, register or PSW bit of the processor exists for it. *Open:* whether `VIC-64X`, `SID-64X` and `BLT64` of chapter 3 are part of the standard in iteration two or optional devices like the C128 (they are the author's own extended designs, not the C128, so this is not settled by this decision).
