78. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): the VIC-64X, SID-64X and BLT64 are deferred; the standard is one PCI Express audio/video set, chosen for simplicity and efficiency.** The author's words: "the vic-64x, sid-64x, blt64 are deferred for now, for now we will have a standard pcie set of audio/video devices, but pick the ones that are the simplest to program while still being efficient; i want a standard audio/video interface so there is no bullshit with supporting a bunch of device drivers, although as a pcie bus any device MAY be attached and supported." *Recorded:* (1) the three C64-derived devices of chapter 3 are deferred (closes `decisions-pending.md` F22: they are not in the standard for now); (2) the standard names **one** interface per class (network, graphics, audio), so the system needs one driver per class and no board-specific drivers; (3) any other PCI Express device may be attached and supported, but the standard does not require it. *Open:* the choice of the three interfaces (`decisions-pending.md` F6 to F8, now proposed as virtio) and the hot-plug scope (F23).
79. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): virtio is the standard interface for network, graphics and audio.** The author's words: "yeah, virtio is fine". *Decided* (closes `decisions-pending.md` F6, F7 and F8 as proposed there, and settles Q10's "virtio only if reusing OpenBSD's drivers saves more than it costs" in favour of virtio): the standard devices are **virtio-net**, **virtio-gpu** (2D mode) and **virtio-snd**, all over the virtio PCI Express transport, so one transport driver and three thin class drivers serve the system, and the same devices run in QEMU, `sim64` and the FPGA. Any other PCI Express device may be attached with its own driver (errata 78). *Open:* whether OpenBSD has a virtio-snd driver (if not, one is written); the exact feature subset of each device (`F28`); the platform ordering the virtio ordering feature must declare (`F14`).
80. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): Q64 is PCI Express, backward compatible, with extra convenience and security circuitry.** The author's words: "our q64 bus will be pcie with the painpoints all solved or mitigated. so, backward compatible with extra convenience/security circuitry added." *Decided* (settles `decisions-pending.md` F1 and the retirement of the eight-register device rule in F2): (1) Q64 is not a separate bus beside PCI Express; it is PCI Express, so an ordinary PCI Express device works on it unchanged; (2) the pdp64 adds circuitry on top, for convenience (overlays, helper registers, errata 75) and security (the IOMMU hardening of F25, error and reset rules of F27); (3) the pain-points document (`kernel-pain-points.md`) is the list the added circuitry is measured against: each pain is to be solved or mitigated, and the re-marked rows (F24 to F28) are the work list. *Open:* how a device announces the extensions (`decisions-pending.md` F29).
81. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): an extension is announced only if a card must take part in it.** The author's words, answering `decisions-pending.md` F29: "depends on the mitigations; if they don't affect the cards, the cards don't need to know." *Decided:* (1) a mitigation that lives entirely on the processor side (the root complex, the IOMMU, the interrupt controller, the fault rules) is invisible to cards and needs no discovery; (2) only an extension that needs a card's cooperation (a device-side helper register, say) is announced, through a PCI Express extended capability, and only pdp64-aware devices implement it; (3) the pain-points work list (F24 to F28) is therefore processor-side, apart from any convenience register that a device must offer.
82. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): data is naturally aligned; byte access stays; wide transfers are fine.** The author's words, answering the question left open in the device chat (`decisions-pending.md` E2): "ok, yes, align 64 bit values the natural way; as long as we have circuitry to access individual bytes i'm ok with loading in 64 bits (or more) at a time." *Decided:* (1) word, long and quad data accesses are naturally aligned (ISA 7.4 stands; the earlier chat message "only 2 byte memory alignment" is read as the rule for instructions, which stay on 2-byte boundaries); (2) individual bytes remain addressable by the byte forms of the move instructions (`MOVB`, `MOVZB` and the others), at any address; (3) an implementation may move 64 bits or more at a time (caches, `MBLK`, DMA) without this being visible to software; (4) the tag bits of ISA 6.2 and the atomicity of aligned accesses are unaffected.
83. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): the definitive priority list; the "no mode bits" rule is dropped.** The author annotated the collected lists (`docs/priority-lists.md`) and confirmed the draft. *Decided, in order:* (1) **security and safety** (an error that can never arise; failing that, one that is easy to detect; failing that, one that is cheap to recover from; restart and recovery go together; no strange side effects; "as if we were the NSA"); (2) **opcode choice** (fewer instructions executed, so fewer memory fetches, the slowest part of a modern processor; coalescing opcodes is good; this is not fewer opcodes); (3) **opcode encoding** (dense; rare instructions longer); (4) **speed and power**, which low gate count serves (the author: "4 and 5 are essentially the same thing"); (5) **good compiler target**; (6) **pleasant in assembler**. *Standing constraints, not priorities:* out-of-order readiness (authoritative; it supersedes the original in-order design; "anything that can fit our error prevention, detection, and recovery priorities in out of order execution is something to look at extremely seriously"); the soft-rule test (errata 42); hot-loop weight; Occam ("a principle that applies no matter what it is orthogonal to the hierarchy"). *Dropped:* the rule "compatibility is conversion instructions, never mode bits". The author: "mode bits are case by case, but in general should be visible, not hidden state. putting modes in bits that are saved anyway sounds like a freebie." *Changed in the text:* ISA section 1 (priorities replace the r20 ranking, which put transistor count first; the rule list is three rules; 1.1 and 1.2 follow the new list), sections 9.8 and 15.2 (the founding-rule wording), section 17 (the in-order claim), manual 1.4, 9.2.5 and 10.3.3, the README design principles, and `decisions-pending.md` Part 0. This closes E9 of `decisions-pending.md` Part E; E10 (the compare-and-branch opcodes) is judged by priority 2 and stands. *Not changed:* statements that the current floating-point design has no rounding-mode bits (ISA 9.8, manual 1.3.7 and 9.12.5): they describe the design, which may now be reopened one case at a time.
84. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): the nineteen retire-stage candidates.** The author annotated `docs/retire-stage-candidates.md`. *Approved, to be specified:* 1 granule colouring ("do it, no delay"); 2 the reserved colours for uninitialised and freed memory; 4 the stack limit register; 5 landing pads gated by a page bit, with the fault attributed to the branch; 8 the `SYS` page bit; 13 predictor and cache-way isolation by ASID, both parts ("smp is immediate, so don't defer", which overrides the draft's deferral of cache ways); 14 the residue check of ALU results; 15 precise ECC and parity reporting; 16 precise hardware watchpoints; 17 the restart contract. *To be studied, with the author's direction:* 6 the return predictor as the shadow stack ("minimize recovery code; make the setjmp path one instruction"); 9 overflow-trapping arithmetic (leaning yes); 10 the poison bit per register (leaning yes, since the register file is small and the reason for 16 registers is opcode layout and not gate cost); 11 secret taint (must have no false positives and be precise and deterministic); 12 retire-gated visibility (leaning yes); 18 the self-closing `UA` window (leaning yes; a solid proposal wanted); 3 the capability bit (asked whether it can be done with 64-bit pointers and flags outside the 64 usable bits). *Deferred:* 7 return-address authentication. *Rejected:* 19 the user-visible transactional region. *Also decided:* the QEMU back end is to be brought to parity with `sim64`, and once the OpenBSD port runs each adopted choice is measured individually for performance (`roadmap.md`). *Open:* items 1 and 2 spend pointer bits that the canonical-address rule of ISA 6.1.1 refuses to software; the rule must be restated, since the hardware, not software, would define and check those bits (see the study of item 3).
85. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): replies to the second round of security proposals.** *Random numbers (P9):* the pool is one device shared by all cores; no per-process pool; no version register and no updatable firmware ("the algo is simple enough"); it is an assistant to the operating system's own generator, which absorbs into it and squeezes from it. The author's point that a read with a known and wanted effect is not a strange side effect is accepted. *Select and secure core (P17):* the branch-free select (`MASKEQZ`) is adopted; at least one **secure core** has every timing channel removed (all instructions and all cache fetches take fixed time), keeps its timer ticks, has private cache lines, and gets constant-time AES and SHA instructions "or whichever opcodes would speed up and enable aes and sha"; any of these protections that cost no performance on the other cores are enabled there too (the proposal is `docs/crypto-secure-core.md`). *Literal (P18):* `MOVQL #imm64, Rd` is accepted; breaking the three-parcel rule for this instruction is acceptable because a 64-bit literal is not counted as part of the instruction; "we can do the same trick for 32 bit immediates too". *IOMMU (F25):* the five recommendations of the synthesis are accepted; bits 40 to 63 of the leaf are confirmed free (the CPU page-table entry reserves them as zero, and the IOMMU leaf is a separate format that shares bits 0 to 39). *Studies:* item 6, the **mirror** shadow stack is approved with its recommendations; item 9, `TRAPCC` with the fused `ADDV`/`SUBV`/`MULV` forms and no compare-and-trap form, as a trap and not an abort; item 11, the proposal's leanings are followed; item 12, both selectors (per domain and per page) and the kernel strict by default; items 10 and 18 await answers to the compressed choices in `docs/study-round2.md`; item 3 awaits the answer on full CHERI (`study-round2.md` section 1).
86. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-03): the second review of the study proposals and the crypto proposal.** *Study proposals:* item 3, full 128-bit CHERI is rejected and stage 1 (a valid-pointer tag per register and per 8-byte word) is kept, with bounds deferred (the author: "i accept your recommendation"); `TRAPCC` is a trap and not an abort; item 10, the three leanings are accepted (fault at the instruction that writes the flags from a poisoned source; the kernel decides a deferred load's fault; the kernel dispatcher, not the hardware, tests the argument registers for poison); `NS` and `SEC` are independent page bits; item 18, the five leanings are accepted (fixup table, `.U` spelling, keep `PSW.UA`/`STUA`/`CLUA` until the OpenBSD port confirms, `ECAUSE` bit 15, atomics take the user form once `ATOM`'s operand placement is fixed). *Secure core and select (`docs/crypto-secure-core.md`):* the design of section 1 is approved ("i like your design. approved.") and the select instructions of section 2 ("looks good"). *Ticks:* the author's rule is that if an instruction can take a time independent of its operands without ticks, timing interrupts or hertz, then none are introduced; this reverses the earlier "ticks, not tickless", so the secure core takes no periodic timer tick and is stopped and preempted by an inter-processor interrupt (section 1.5 is rewritten). *Timing:* confirmed that `ADD` and `DIV` need not take the same time as each other; each takes a time independent of its data. *Awaiting:* sections 3 to 5 of the crypto document, and the answers to the questions the author asked: 32 registers and 128-bit registers (`docs/register-file-options.md`, recommending neither).
87. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-04; the two members superseded by errata 94) and what was assumed: the prefix-coded encodings, phase 1 (specification, shared codec, `as64`, `ld64`).** *Decided by the author:* the encoding of `docs/encoding-proposal.md` replaces parcel 0 = `len opc reg low` for new work, as **two grammars**; the architecture is **pdp64** and its two members are **pdp64/16** (16 registers, 4-bit register fields; scheme A16, corpus-trained, not the profile-tuned B16/C16) and **pdp64/32** (32 registers, 5-bit fields; scheme A32r, eight register-register mnemonics with a 5-bit one-parcel form); `r16`-`r31` exist only in pdp64/32; the four fused pairs `movcall`, `popmret`, `popret`, `mov2` are in both and **fusing is left to the compiler** (the assembler never fuses); the mnemonics and the assembly language stay, plus the new words (`MASKEQZ MASKNEZ BSEL AES* SHA* MSHA* MAESCTR TRAPCC ADDV SUBV MULV MOVQL`...); `as64` must emit every instruction of the ISA in both; the original encoding stays, named **pdp64/r20**, selected by `as64 -m`, recorded in `e_flags` (0, 1, 2), and no existing test changes; the 22 meaningless shapes (`clr #imm`, `jmp #imm`, a store to an immediate, `lea #imm`...) are refused; the hot-register tables (`r0 r1 r8 r2 sp r3 r9 r10`; pdp64/32 also the 4-bit `... r11 r4 r15 r5 r24 r25 r26 r6`) are part of the ABI (`docs/abi.md`); linker relaxation of external `call`/`lea` is a follow-up (the encoding is ready: a 4-byte `call` reaches +-2 MB). *Assumed by the implementation (to be confirmed):* (1) the form tables are data (`spec/pdp64-16/forms.json`, `spec/pdp64-32/forms.json`, written by the optimiser) and not hbnf rules, because the prefix is variable and the fields are split (`spec/README.md`); (2) the optimiser was rerun with the fused pairs forced in, so the tables differ from the report's A16/A32r (pdp64/16: 219 forms, 281 leaves, 5.0% free; pdp64/32: 196 forms, 255 leaves, because `RR5` and `mov2` take 18.75% of the code space; corpus size 1,016,300 bytes for pdp64/16 and 1,052,526 for pdp64/32, compiled for the old encoding, with no fused instruction in it); (3) `SETF` (`set cc,rd`, 4 bytes) and `CBDI`/`CBDR` (narrow forms for `cbpl cbmi cbvc cbvs`, which the optimiser never selected) were added, and the placeholder `mfsr_` of the report's R3 list was dropped (`mfsr` has its own form); (4) ATOM operands are three registers (`cas`, `casdb`: four), the address in a register, in the order written; `TRAPCC` is `trapcc.<cc>`, one parcel, no operand, and `TRAPL` is `trap` with an 8-bit vector; (5) `(r)+` and `-(r)` without a stride write stride 0, meaning the size of the access; (6) a `cbCC` that no form reaches is assembled as `cmp` + `bCC` (**the flags are written**), a `movq #imm` that fits no `movq` form as `movql`; (7) `pushm`/`popm` masks are the low 32 bits of the number; (8) the MESC words and the `.u`/`.d` words have only the wide memory forms, and `extb`/`extzb`/`insb` cannot encode a width of 64; (9) the pc-relative field of a branch or call counts parcels (unit 2), of a memory operand bytes (unit 1), and in `movcall` the target is measured from the first parcel of the fused instruction. *Found in the report:* its sizes assumed greedy pairing of fusion candidates (which decides by position and not by gain), a pseudo-distance of 32,000 bytes for literal-pool loads, and an R2 entry `set` that carried no condition; none changes a conclusion. *Note (2026-11-04, integration): superseded in part. The reach of a 4-byte `call` is +-1 MiB, not +-2 MB (20 bits counted in parcels; entry 91); the tables are derived from the hbnf grammars, not from `forms.json` (entry 89); the encodings were re-optimised on the new compiler's output (entry 90); linker relaxation was done and is the default (entry 91). The numbers of the final integrated tree are in `docs/encoding-work/integration.md`.*
88. **Phase 2 of the prefix-coded encodings: `sim64` runs pdp64/r20, pdp64/16 and pdp64/32 (found, decided by the implementer, not by the author).** The executor (`common/pdp64exec.c`, `pdp64exec_enc.inc`) decodes the new encodings through the table codec and runs the groups of the original ISA through the same code; the encoding comes from `e_flags` (`sim64 -m` overrides it); `sim64 -P` writes a profile (executed instructions, bytes, categories, per pc). *Found:* (1) **a bug of the original executor**: `extb`, `extzb`, `insb` and `dshr` read their position and width from the wrong field and ignored both (they returned the whole register); fixed, test `tests/sim/bitfield.s`; the cross-encoding differential test found it. (2) `addv`, `subv` and `mulv` are in the two-register form `rs, rd` in the tables (the optimiser put them in `R2`), while `docs/study-proposals.md` item 9 writes `ra, rb, rd`; the executor takes the table: `rd <- rd op rs` (like `add`, `sub`, `mul`), signed overflow is an abort at the instruction (vector 16, nothing written), no flags. (3) The tables have no store to `d(dp)` (only loads, `DPF.D28`): `movq r3, 24(dp)` has no form in pdp64/16 or pdp64/32. (4) Assumed: `trapcc` that fires is a trap to vector 16 (`CHECK`) with EPC the next instruction and EVA the address of the `trapcc` (errata 86 decides trap, not abort; the vector number is the proposal's), outside the system mode it ends the program; `.u` moves are plain moves without an MMU. (5) **Not in the reference executor, trapping as `unimplemented instruction`**: the atomic group (operand placement and size encoding not fixed), AES, SHA, `msha256`, `msha512`, `maesctr`, `utf8dec` (the length field of the status word is not defined), `utf8enc`, `mval`, the deferred loads `.d`/`.ud`, `syspg0`-`syspg5`. Implemented from the manual's text: `movub..movuq`, `addm..bism` (flags of the ALU operation), `utf8len` (0 for a byte that cannot begin a sequence), `fclass32`, `maskeqz`, `masknez`, `bsel`, `movql`. *Verified:* the six large programs (lz4, zlib, Lua, three BearSSL kernels) give the output of the host build in all three encodings, 206 c-testsuite programs and 147 gcc-torture programs give identical results in all three encodings, every test of `tests/sim` runs in all three, a differential test of 1551 cases, the fused instructions restart whole after a fault (tested with `-K`, and the test fails when the restore is switched off).
89. **The grammars are the source of truth of pdp64/16 and pdp64/32 (the author's correction, 2026-11-04; what went wrong, what was done).** *The author's words:* "i said grammar is the source of truth; undo your pivot to python. use latest hbnf." *What went wrong:* in phase 1 (entry 87) the two encodings were defined by `spec/pdp64-NN/forms.json`, a data file the Python optimiser wrote, and `tools/mkenc.py` generated the codec tables, the assembly grammar and `tables.md` from it; `spec/README.md` justified this with "hbnf can express the prefix code only as one alternative per leaf, and I did not build that route". That broke the principle of the project (`docs/CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md` section 8, README: one source of truth, a set of hbnf grammars, so that the assembler, the linker, the emulator, the compiler back end and the QEMU target cannot drift apart): a table beside the grammars is exactly the second source the principle forbids, and the route that was declared too hard was the route to build. It was not hard. *What was done:* (1) the project now builds hbnf `dd34982` (origin/main, 2026-11-03) with no local patch (`tools/build-hbnf.sh`; `tools/hbnf-numbers.patch` was already dropped, upstream has the numbers since c99c7cc); the notation of upstream's change of 2026-11-03 (`0x41..0x5A`, `i"..."`) is applied to `spec/ascii.hbnf`, `common.hbnf`, `pdp64-asm.hbnf`, `pdp64.hbnf.in` by upstream's `tools/hbnf_modernize.py`, and since upstream's C parsers now default to compact, overlaid nodes, `as64`'s and the syntax test's parsers are generated with `--no-compact --no-overlay` (the node layout `as64/asmp.c` is written against); (2) each encoding is an hbnf grammar, `spec/pdp64-enc.hbnf` (shared) and `spec/pdp64-NN/pdp64-NN.hbnf`: the prefix as a constant, the sub-opcode, the pieces of every operand with their widths, the operand shapes (widths, signedness, scale), the mnemonic numbering, the register file, the hot tables, the fused instructions, the catalogue of forms, the illegal patterns (a check on the unused sub-opcodes, a prefix that no leaf has); they were converted from `forms.json` by `docs/codegen-data/encoding/write_hbnf.py`, run once, and read back by `tools/encgram.py` into exactly the dict `forms.json` held (forms, leaves, hot tables, fused list, all equal); (3) `forms.json` is deleted; `tools/mkenc.py`, the checks and the vector generators read the grammar through `encgram.py`, and the optimiser's `export_forms.py` now writes grammars (it stays as the oracle, `check-enc.py` still lays the forms out with the Python model, and as the design tool); (4) the generated tables are the ones the old tree generated, byte for byte but the first comment line (`pdp64-16-tab.c`, `pdp64-32-tab.c`, the assembly grammar of as64, `pdp64-groups.h`, `pdp64-extnames.h`, `pdp64-mnnames.h`), and `tests/enc/generated.sha256` records their digests; (5) `tools/check-grammar-enc.sh` (in `check-all.sh`): hbnf accepts the grammars, everything is regenerated and compared, and the decoder hbnf generates from the grammar (`parse_bits`) is run against the codec on 262,144 parcel sequences per encoding, with equal legal/illegal, length, leaf and raw value of every field. *Where hbnf stops short (nothing blocks; the grammar works around each, and each is an upstream proposal):* a bit field cannot be typed by an enum, so `sub:4` and the list of mnemonics `sub-FORM-K` are two rules joined by name and a check `?(sub < N)`; a rule name cannot hold `.` or `/`, so the optimiser's form names live in the catalogue as literals and the rules use `-`; a bit rule must hold a field to be one (`insn = leaf:( ... )`, and `insn` must come first, or the grammar is read as text); no template generates a table of leaves, so `tools/mkenc.py` is a generator that reads the grammar. The workflow for the next change of an encoding is in `spec/README.md` ("Changing an encoding").
90. **The compare-and-branch reach of pdp64/16 and pdp64/32, and the encodings re-optimised on the new compiler's output (found and decided by the implementer; the task of 2026-11-04, the grammars staying the source of truth).** *The question:* phase 2 (entry 88) measured pdp64/32 at +0.01% executed instructions against pdp64/16 but -5.21% if each `cmp` + `bCC` that `as64` substitutes for a `cbCC` no form reaches counts as one; Lua's +12.4% was the substitution. *Diagnosis* (`docs/encoding-proposal.md` section 12.1; the 841 files compiled by `pcc-enc` abacdf17, `-m pdp64/16` and `-m pdp64/32`): the substitution is 328 of 34,387 `cbCC` sites in pdp64/16 and 866 of 34,524 in pdp64/32; 284 and 682 of them are immediates of more than 12 bits (the widest `cbCC` immediate was 12 bits in pdp64/32, 16 bits for `cbeq`/`cbne` only in pdp64/16), not distance (58% of the 866 are within 256 bytes); the cause is the allotment of code space (the `cbCC` leaves took 29.5% of the first parcels in pdp64/16, 9.4% in pdp64/32, the 95% budget being the same); the executed pairs are 0.61% and 1.02% of the instructions of the six programs, almost all in Lua's opcode dispatch (the 38.8 M of entry 88 belonged to the old compiler's chain of compares and are gone). *Done:* (1) the optimiser (`docs/codegen-data/encoding/`) trains on the compiled assembly of each encoding (`PDP64_CORPUS_DIR_<TAG>`; `m2.py`, the assembly reader it needed, was outside the repository and is now in it), fits the distances of the previous round's layout instead of the original encoding's lengths, prices a `cbCC` that no form reaches as the `cmp` and `bCC` the assembler emits plus a price per extra instruction (before: 99 bytes, which credited each `cbCC` form with a saving that was not there), offers three-parcel `cbCC` forms (a 16-bit immediate with a +-4 KB pc field, an 8-bit one with +-2 MB, two registers with +-32 MB) and runs a local search after the greedy selection; the hot tables are kept (the ABI). (2) `spec/pdp64-16/pdp64-16.hbnf` and `spec/pdp64-32/pdp64-32.hbnf` (and the shapes in `spec/pdp64-enc.hbnf`) were written by `export_forms.py`, not edited; `tables.md`, the generated-file digests, the vectors and the assembler/linker expectations regenerated by the workflow of `spec/README.md`: 208 forms and 270 leaves (pdp64/16), 207 and 268 (pdp64/32). (3) *The workflow was incomplete in one place and is fixed:* `tools/check-drift-enc.py` and `tests/exectest-enc.c` each recorded the numbers of forms, leaves and unassigned first parcels of the grammars (a second copy that failed after every change); they now derive them from the leaves, and only the hot tables, which the ABI pins, stay recorded. *Result* (real `as64`/`ld64`/`sim64`): substituted `cbCC` 328 -> 4 and 866 -> 17; corpus instruction bytes 951,144 -> 944,600 (-0.69%) and 985,082 -> 976,000 (-0.92%); the six programs execute 643.19 M instructions and 1,723.6 M bytes (pdp64/16, -0.6% and -2.1%) and 605.24 M and 1,779.9 M (pdp64/32, -1.0% and -0.3%), output identical to the host's. *Is pdp64/32 still worse than pdp64/16 in fetched bytes and size? Yes:* +3.27% executed bytes (1.40% before), +3.32% corpus text, +3.92% linked text of the six programs, for 5.90% fewer executed instructions and 63.5% fewer frame accesses; the same 16-register code assembled as pdp64/32 fetches +10.75% (static +4.23%), of which the 32-register compile repays about two thirds. *Caveats, stated:* the objective is the static size of the corpus (the dynamic effect is not optimised: pdp64/32's one-parcel `cbCC` with 0 shrank from a +-64 to a +-32 byte reach, and lz4 fetches 1.2% more bytes for it); no profile-tuned variant was tried; the corpus is compiled file by file, so cross-file `call`, `lea` and `movcall` (14,136, 12,787 and 6,556 sites in pdp64/32) could not be measured and still use their 32-bit forms until the linker relaxes them; the compiler (`pcc-enc`) was used as committed, not as left in its working tree by the agent developing it; leave-projects-out on the sources of the six programs keeps about half of the static gain (section 12.6).
91. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-04) and what was assumed: linker relaxation of external `call` and `lea` (`ld64`, with the object-format support in `as64`).** *Decided by the author:* the linker relaxes an external `call` and a data-symbol `lea` (the encoding has the 4-byte forms, the assembler cannot know the distance). *Assumed by the implementer (to be confirmed):* (1) *(first an opt-in; the author then decided, 2026-11-04: "yes do relax by default")* it is the **default** for pdp64/16 and pdp64/32: `as64` marks the object (`e_flags` 0x10, `EF_PDP64_RELAX`) unless `--no-relax`, `ld64` relaxes every marked object unless `--no-relax`, pdp64/r20 is untouched; `as64 --no-relax` makes the plain object (the bytes of phase 1) and `ld64 --no-relax` links a relaxation object as the assembler laid it out, byte for byte as the plain object would link; no tool needs a flag; (2) the site of a relaxation is the existing relocation (INSN_PC, INSN_BR, INSN_ABS), which `as64 --relax` now also records for references to labels of the same section (the value is in the instruction already), plus a new `R_PDP64_ALIGN` (type 6) on every padding of `.text` (`.even`, `.align`, `.pool`), so the paddings are recomputed from the new positions; (3) the linker does not shrink the assembler's lengths but **grows each instruction from its shortest form** to what its distance needs, the lengths only grow and the iteration ends (`docs/relaxation.md` has the argument), and it re-derives each instruction from the codec (`enc_decode`, then `enc_encode_n`), so branches the assembler had resolved itself and `movcall`, pool loads and absolute operands are treated like calls; (4) a `.text` that holds a distance that is not a relocation (a difference of two labels as a number, a `d(pc)` written as a number, an instruction with two address operands) makes the object `NOADJ_TEXT` (0x20) and the linker leaves its `.text` alone; (5) a label immediately before an `.align` that happened to need 0 bytes is moved behind the padding when it grows; (6) a `cbCC` the assembler expanded to `cmp` + `bCC` stays expanded; (7) the reach of the 4-byte `call` is +-1 MiB (20 bits of parcels), not the +-2 MB of errata 87 and of `docs/encoding-proposal.md`; `lea` and pc-relative loads of 4 bytes reach +-32 KiB, `jmp` +-64 KiB; (8) the order of the sections is unchanged, `.data` still starts on the page after `.text`, and the text of a link is smaller by what relaxed, so every address moves. *Verified:* see `docs/relaxation.md` section 6 and 7 (a generated program of 126,000 instructions, every `tests/sim` program relaxed, a decoder-based check of every relaxed image, seven mutants of `ld64`, the six large programs and the c-testsuite with and without relaxation: same output).
92. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-04 and 2026-11-05): core counts and mixed cores.** The author's words: "yeah let's have 4 cores minimum, 16 or more preferred."; "its ok to have in order and out of order cores on the same die as long as we can tell which is which." (written 'same day'); "for initial device 4 cores is enough hopefully that fits on the fpga". *Decided:* (1) the design supports at least 4 cores, and 16 or more is the preferred ceiling that no decision may preclude; (2) the initial device has 4 cores, hoped to fit the FPGA (the paper estimates are in `smp-decisions.md`; no core has been synthesised); (3) in-order and out-of-order cores may share a die if software can tell them apart, which the core-class register of manual 7.8.4 must report; the architectural memory model and the ISA semantics are identical on every core. *Numbering note (2026-11-04, integration):* written on main as entry 91 while 87 to 90 were on the unmerged encoding branches; in the integrated tree the encoding branches keep 87 to 90, the linker relaxation entry of `enc-ld` (written as 89 there) is 91, and this entry is 92. *Open:* the SMP decisions of `docs/smp-decisions.md`. *Read with errata 130 and 132:* errata 130 (a minimum of two cores) is withdrawn by errata 132; this entry stands.
93. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-04; the three manuals superseded by errata 94): there is one manual for each CPU of the family, written once and generated three times; every bit-layout chart is generated from the grammar.** *The author's words:* "manual" (the whole instruction when the family was integrated); then "the manuals for /16 /32 and /r20 are separate manuals because they are different cpus"; "all the bit layout charts for each opcode should be generated from the grammar, for the dictionary section of the manual"; and, of the later tool, "hbnf itself should have a grammar that 'transforms' the opcodes ... a custom layout, so the one grammar matches the opcodes and the bit layouts, but what is emitted can be either the assembler/linker or the svg charts". *What was assumed first and replaced:* the plan of `docs/manual-update-plan.md` recommended one chapter 9 for the family with an availability line on every instruction; the author's words above replace it, so there are no availability lines. *Done:* (1) the hand-written text is once, in `docs/manual-src/` (README there), with member blocks (`<!-- member: 16 32 -->`, `[[16: words]]`, whole files); `tools/mkmanual.py` assembles `docs/manual/pdp64-r20/`, `pdp64-16/` and `pdp64-32/`, which are committed and checked by `tools/check-all.sh`; `docs/manual/` is a landing page; the numbers of chapters, sections and statements stay literal and each generated manual is checked for contiguity, for links and for section references; there is one book for each (`book/pdp64-r20.pdf`, `-16`, `-32`). (2) The charts are generated: `spec/pdp64.hbnf.in` gives the fields of pdp64/r20 (the guards of its alternatives are evaluated for each opcode), `spec/pdp64-mnemonics.hbnf` the numbers and the new `spec/pdp64-layout.hbnf` what a field is called; the leaves of the prefix-coded grammars come through `tools/encgram.py`; a chart for every layout (pdp64/r20 67, pdp64/16 270, pdp64/32 268, and a chart for each form of each word, in the entries) and a check that no mnemonic is without a chart and no chart without a mnemonic. (3) The 70 words of `mn-ext` have 19 entries in the manuals of pdp64/16 and pdp64/32 only (`tools/manual/isa_part4.py`, `isa_stories_ext.py`), each saying how firm its text is. *Assumed (to be confirmed):* a chart for each class of instruction, length and body in pdp64/r20, where the opcode is a white box and the table under the chart gives the number of each mnemonic, and a chart for each leaf in the two others (the sub-opcode of each mnemonic in the legend), and not one for each mnemonic; the sentences of the entries that describe a layout of the original encoding are left out of the manuals of pdp64/16 and pdp64/32, with a few replaced (`tools/manual/memberprose.py`, `isa_enc.py`) until the author of the member text rewrites them; `docs/encoding-work/manual-generated.md` lists what is generated, what remains for the hand-written text, and the statements of the new entries that the repository does not settle.
94. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-09 and 2026-11-10): pdp64/32 is the pdp64; pdp64/r20 and pdp64/16 are tested alternatives, no longer part of it.** *The author's words:* "only the 32 register pdp64 is official, the other two are cancelled." (2026-11-09); then "pdp64/32 is the decided upon pdp64", "r20 and pdp64/16 are gone, there is no family" and "it is good to document the testing against the original r20 and pdp64/16 variants, /32 chosen for performance reasons. follows our principles of check, don't guess." (2026-11-10). *Decided:* (1) the pdp64 is the 32-register, prefix-coded architecture, pdp64/32 (`r0`-`r31`, the grammar `spec/pdp64-32/pdp64-32.hbnf`); (2) pdp64/r20 (the original encoding) and pdp64/16 (the 16-register prefix-coded encoding) are not members of an architecture: they were built and measured against pdp64/32, which was chosen for performance, and the testing against them is documented (`docs/encoding-work/`, above all `integration.md` section 4 and `timing.md`); (3) what the repository carries of them (their grammars, manuals, books, test runs and the `-m` options of the tools) stays as that documented comparison; their removal is not done, and no file or tool has been removed. *Supersedes* the three-manuals decision of errata 93 (one manual for each CPU of the family) and the two-members wording of errata 87 ("the architecture is pdp64 and its two members are pdp64/16 and pdp64/32"); their text stays as the history. The manual of the pdp64 is `docs/manual/pdp64-32/`; the manuals of pdp64/r20 and pdp64/16 are generated from the same source and stay as the comparison. The chart generation, the grammar as the source of truth (errata 89) and the form of the hand-written source of errata 93 stand. *Still to do:* the removal of the two alternatives from the tools and the repository, if the author asks for it.
95. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-09): the bus moves to virtio across the board; the native eight-register device model is gone.** *The author's words:* "forget the native 8 register devices. gone. virtio across the board."; and, of the recommendations made for the bus chapter, "i like all your recommendations, go ahead and update the bus chapter." *Decided:* devices are virtio devices (on the PCI Express, NVMe and USB base of errata 75); the model of eight memory-mapped registers plus DMA is dropped, and with it the "eight is a minimum" refinement. *Supersedes* errata 29 (devices are eight memory-mapped registers plus DMA), the eight-register bus controller of errata 49 (that the bus enumerates, maps and alerts is not changed by this entry; how it is done under virtio is part of the rewrite) and errata 61 (storage interfaces follow the one device pattern of at least eight registers); their text stays as the history. *Still to do:* the rewrite of chapters 2 and 5 of the manual (`docs/manual-src/02-bus.md`, `05-drivers.md`) and of `docs/pdp64-q64-bus.md`, which still describe the eight-register devices, and the question of the syntactic sugar that the 12-register-plus-DMA idea raised.
96. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-09): page tables of 3, 4 or 5 levels, with the number of levels discoverable at runtime.** *The author's words:* "3 4 and 5 is good, as long as this info is discoverable easily at runtime."; the virtual addresses of 39, 48 and 57 bits that go with them are "all fine"; and of the direct-mapping windows, "good thing we already had dmw". The author also asked why the chapter said that a three-level table is not supported: it is supported. *Decided:* three levels (39 bits, the System v1 form), four (48 bits) and five (57 bits) are all allowed; the level count is readable by software at runtime, so a kernel adapts to the implementation without a build option. *Supersedes* the reading of the page-table question in `decisions-pending.md` that makes three levels the only choice and four a rejected option (the question stays there as the record of the options). *Still to do:* the register or field that reports the level count, the walk of four and five levels in `docs/manual-src/06-kernel.md` (which describes three levels only), and the removal of the sentence that said a three-level table is not supported.

97. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-09): no physical-address bit selects device space.** *The author's words:* the whole "bit 39 decides if memory or dma" should go away, "if we are going to mmap petabytes, we need those bits"; with, in the same message, "how about we allow 4 levels" (of page table, errata 96) and "i like all your recommendations, go ahead and update the bus chapter, about address width". *Decided:* the rule of errata 60 (bit 39 of the physical address selects device space when set and memory when clear) goes, and the physical address is free to be wider than 40 bits so that mappings of petabytes are possible. How devices are located in the physical address space instead (the PCI Express configuration and base address registers of errata 75), and the physical address width, are part of the rewrite of the bus chapter and are not decided here. *Supersedes* errata 60 and decision P19 of `decisions-pending.md`; their text stays as the history. *Still to do:* the bus chapter (`docs/manual-src/02-bus.md`, section 2.1.6 and the address map), `docs/pdp64-system-architecture.md`, `docs/pdp64-q64-bus.md` and the QEMU memory map, which use the bit; the choice of the physical address width.
98. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): `abs`, `min`, `minu`, `max` and `maxu` are assembler aliases, not native instructions.** *The author's words:* "abs/min/max are aliases, and their entries in the manual show the alias." (2026-11-10, answering whether `ABS`, `MIN`, `MINU`, `MAX` and `MAXU` stay native opcodes); and, on 2026-10-21, of the conditional select (`cmov`, `csel`, `fsel`) he had asked to know more about, "am happy for abs/min/max to be aliases on top of it in the assembler". *Decided:* the five words are assembler aliases over the conditional select, and the manual's entry for each shows the alias. *Supersedes* the native `AESC` sub-opcodes of errata 23 (`0x08` `abs`; `0x10` `min`, `0x11` `minu`, `0x12` `max`, `0x13` `maxu`); the text of errata 23 stays as the history. *Still to do, stated plainly:* the conditional select the aliases sit on is **not yet defined** (`docs/pdp64-isa.md` section 12 item 15 keeps it on hold until a speed model justifies it), so the expansion of each alias is not decided. The only select the records define is the branch-free `MASKEQZ`/`MASKNEZ` of errata 85 (it reads a register, no flag); a candidate expansion over it (compare, `SET` into a scratch register, the two masks, `BIS`) needs a scratch register and changes `C`, and whether it is the select the author meant is his question. Until it is, the grammars (`spec/pdp64-mnemonics.hbnf` and the encoding grammars), `as64`, `sim64`, pcc, QEMU and LLVM still treat the five words as native `AESC` sub-opcodes; the manual says so. The flags of the native words are the behaviour an alias must keep, or the decision that defines the expansion must say that it changes them: `abs` sets `N` and `Z` from the result, sets `V` when the operand is the most negative number (the result wraps) and leaves `C`; `min`, `minu`, `max` and `maxu` set `N` and `Z` from the result, clear `V` and leave `C`. A select that writes no flags would change this.
99. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the FPGA clock target is 200 to 300 MHz.** *The author's words:* "200-300MHz is fine; still much faster than pdp-11 no?" (2026-08-29) and "the FPGA clock is 200-300 MHz" (2026-11-10). *Decided:* the clock the design aims at on the FPGA is 200 to 300 MHz. *Supersedes,* as the target, the frequency estimates of `docs/pdp64-fpga-plan.md` section 3 (50 to 80 MHz on the Cyclone V, 90 to 130 MHz on a Zynq 7020, 180 to 250 MHz on an UltraScale+) and its advice not to chase frequency in phase 1; the estimates stay as estimates (E), and the plan says plainly that every one of them is below the target, the best of them (250 MHz, UltraScale+) being the only one inside it. *Still to do:* a closure plan for the board that is chosen (pipeline-stage boundaries in the RTL from phase 1, in the plan's risk 2); no estimate of Fmax has been measured, because no RTL exists.
100. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): `R7` is an ordinary general register and is not a view of the program counter.** *The author's words:* "r7 is not a view of the pc" (2026-11-10, answering the open question about `R7`). *Decided:* `R7` is a general register like any other; the program counter is not a register and is read with `LEA 0(PC), Rn` (mode `0010`); the PDP-11 spelling (`MOV #5, R0`, `MOV foo(PC), R0`, `MOV @#addr, R0`) is kept as assembler syntax. *Supersedes nothing:* the records held no earlier ruling that `R7` might be the program counter; `docs/pdp64-isa.md` section 9.3 argued the point (a read-only view of the PC rejected for the definitional commitment about which PC it would return) and the manual's statement 9.3.2 already said the PC is not a general register; the author's words close the question those texts had left as the assistant's own argument. `docs/decisions-pending.md` held no open question about it.
101. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the disk is virtio-blk.** *The author's words:* "disk is virtio-blk" (2026-11-10, answering whether the disk is NVMe alone or also virtio-blk). *Decided:* the standard disk is a virtio-blk device on the virtio PCI Express transport, in line with virtio across the board (errata 95); the boot ROM and the drivers of the manual are written for it. *Supersedes,* as the standard disk interface, the NVMe disk of errata 75 ("we're going with pcie and nvme and usb for our q64 bus"). *Not removed:* the bus remains PCI Express on which, in the author's words of 2026-11-04, "any device MAY be attached and supported", so an NVMe device may still be attached as any PCI Express device may, with its own driver; it is not the standard disk, and the manual does not require its driver. *Still to do:* the feature subset of virtio-blk (the platform-ordering feature, flush, the block size) and the read of block 0 by the boot ROM through a virtqueue, which are part of the drivers chapter's open items; the boot-media order of decision F10 of `docs/decisions-pending.md`.
102. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the physical address is 40 to 64 bits, and device windows can be mapped anywhere except the first few pages.** *The author's words:* "physical address: minimum 24 bit, up to full 64 bit. mapping can be anywhere except the first few pages where the boot stuff is loaded." (2026-11-10, answering the width and the placement that errata 97 left open), and minutes later "fine, minimum 40 bits." The minimum of 24 bits of the first message was replaced the same day by 40. *Decided:* (1) the physical address is at least 40 bits and at most 64; an implementation chooses its width in that range; (2) the window of a device (and the configuration space) can be mapped at any physical address except the first few pages, where the boot code and boot data are loaded. *Supersedes* the fixed 40-bit physical address of the earlier text (the ISA's section 6.1.1 and the table of section 15, the system-architecture document, the bus document and the manual's glossary and chapters 2, 6 and 9, which stated it as the design) and completes errata 97, which freed the width and the placement without fixing them. *Open, and tagged (P) or (S) in the texts:* (a) how many pages "the first few" are (not stated; the boot ROM's load address of `docs/boot.md` is still "to be chosen"); (b) how software discovers the implemented physical width (no register or field is defined; the level count of errata 96 is in the same position); (c) **the page-table entry**: it is 64 bits with the flags in bits 0 to 9 and the physical page number in place in bits 12 to 39. A physical address of up to 64 bits needs a page number of 52 bits, bits 12 to 63, so the present layout extended in place would fit a 64-bit address exactly, with every bit above bit 11 taken by the page number and no spare bit for a later flag (a no-execute-style or memory-type extension would have nowhere to go) or for a wider memory type; whether that is the format, or the entry changes (a wider entry, flags elsewhere, or a smaller maximum for the page number), is the author's question and no format is chosen here. The simulator, QEMU and `spec/pdp64-system.hbnf` map 40 bits, which is the minimum, so the present implementation meets the rule (*(S)*, implementation); they still put the devices in the upper half from `0x8000000000` (errata 97).
103. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the manual is written for two readers who know nothing that happened in computer architecture after the 1970s; the bus chapter is rewritten first.** *The author's words:* "the manual should be written as if a first year compsci student OR an experienced pdp11 assembly programmer were reading it; both don't know about anything that happened in computer architecture since the 1970's. so, the bus chapter... write from the point of view of that pdp11 programmer, what would he want to know about pcie and virtio. include pseudocode and those nice memory layout diagrams that they use to show link lists, etc". *Decided:* (1) the manual is written for two readers, a first-year computer-science student and an experienced PDP-11 assembly programmer; (2) neither is assumed to know anything of computer architecture that came after the 1970s, so every later idea is explained where it first appears; (3) the bus chapter (chapter 2) is the first chapter rewritten for them: it starts from the Unibus and explains PCI Express and virtio by contrast with it, with pseudocode and box-and-arrow memory-layout diagrams. *To follow:* the other chapters; they are not decided here, and nothing in this entry says how or in what order they are rewritten.
104. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): a read of a configuration position that never held a function returns all ones.** *The author's words:* "i'd assume all ones, bus faults are spendy." and, asked where a miss could be recorded: "yes, all ones; don't we already have a status register and mode flags, can't you use a bit in one of those or add a bit?" *Decided:* the read completes normally and returns all ones in every byte (vendor ID `0xFFFF` means nothing is there); it is not a bus fault, though a read of other non-existent memory stays a precise `BUSFAULT` (6.8.3). *Decided (2026-11-10, "agree root complex bit"):* the root complex records the miss in a sticky, write-one-to-clear bit of its own status register (the register and its place stay unspecified). The processor status word was not used, as the assistant explained, because it belongs to the running thread and is saved and restored on every exception and context switch, so a flag set by a bridge would be lost or attributed to the wrong thread; a system register (13 and up are reserved) was the alternative and was not chosen. Manual 2.4.9 and 2.4.10.
105. **REQUEST AND AGREEMENT (the author's, 2026-11-10): the graphics chapter is rewritten for the two readers of errata 103, as a history leading to the terminal; the output is HDMI or another standard graphics output (agreed); anti-tearing and the removal of graphics boilerplate are wanted (goals, not designs); the form of the terminal device is open.** *The author's words (the request):* "now do the graphics chapter from the same point of view; pdp11 had a tty that did one line at a time, then there was the vt55 terminal with escape codes, but then came the blit and c64 which allowed graphics and text mode at the same time; the c64 did games, so it had sound and color and sprites as well; then the aweful days where vga/svga took over, no sprites, then vesa, and the split between pixel graphics and text mode got uncomfortable, then we had gpu. our virtio-console device is an attempt to bring the nice properties of the blit terminal and c64 for doing simple and easy peek poke style display of text and also doing graphics, but with modern color depths and screen sizes, and with glyph maps we can even have a ttf engine generate glyphs and they will go into their memory slots for use. then talk about the progression to gpus and shaders and vertices and how that changed graphics; why do all modern terminal emulators use hte gpu? how does it speed things up? why is 3d graphics needed to draw a font?" *Then, the same day:* "conclude the graphics chapter: we're still figuring it out, what would a mashup of gpu, vram, c64 and blit look like." and "oh, it is agreed that output is hdmi or some other standard graphics output, and we want to put in nice architectural sugar to avoid 'tearing' and all those other issues graphics programmers had to use double buffering to fix." and "basically, anything that made graphics programming annoying or cumbersome or boiler platey, we want to sugar away to almost nothing." *Done:* [`manual/03-graphics.md`](manual/03-graphics.md) is rewritten history-first: the teletype (3.1), the glass teletype and its escape codes (3.2), the Blit (3.3), the Commodore 64 with text, graphics, sprites and sound together (3.4), VGA, SVGA and VESA and the uncomfortable split (3.5), the GPU with its pipeline, vertices, rasterization, textures, shaders and command buffers (3.6), why terminal emulators use the GPU and why a font needs the three-dimensional machinery (3.7), scanout, tearing and double buffering (3.8), what made graphics programming annoying with worked before-and-after pairs and a table (3.9), then the pdp64's terminal: the earlier specification (3.10), the terminal as the author describes it with glyph slots (3.11), a peek/poke program (3.12), the stub (3.13), HDMI (3.14), the framebuffer and memory type (3.15), virtio-gpu (3.16), a standard card (3.17), a closing section of four sketches of a mashup, all open (3.19), what was checked (3.20), what is open (3.21); with ten figures (`tools/manual/gfxfigs.py`). Every old numbered statement and tag is kept; the numbering moved (old 3.1, 3.2, 3.3, 3.4 and 3.5 are now 3.10, 3.13, 3.14, 3.15 and 3.17, so the statement "3.1.9" of errata 71 is 3.10.9, and "3.1.1" of errata 66 is 3.10.1; the earlier errata keep the numbers they were written with). *Recorded, and not decided:* (1) **AGREED (the author's words above): the output is HDMI or some other standard graphics output.** This matches the repository and adds nothing to it: statement 3.14.1 (old 3.3.1, *(D)*) says that the display connector is standard kit, HDMI (and DVI and DisplayPort) a transmitter chip, on the first board the DE10-Nano's ADV7513, and 3.14.2 and 3.14.3 (*(P)*) say that the video device's output is a pixel stream with sync, clock and data-enable that any such transmitter takes, and that other ports (a VGA converter, DisplayPort, an LVDS panel) are the same shape. No text contradicts it; "or some other standard graphics output" is what 3.14.3 says. (2) **WANTED, not designed *(P)* (statement 3.8.1): architectural conveniences that avoid tearing, flicker and the other faults that forced double buffering.** The form is open. What the repository already decides is 3.10.9 (errata 71: every register that takes effect at a frame boundary is shadowed, one `ARM` write latches them at the start of the next vertical blank, a status bit says whether the arm was taken or missed, an underrun bit and counter, the vertical-blank interrupt at a fixed line); the chapter lists the further candidates with their costs and decides none: a latching scanout base register with a completion interrupt or fence, per-layer latching, an atomic commit, a raster-position register and interrupt, variable refresh (general knowledge, unverified), damage rectangles, a hardware cursor and sprites, versioned buffers, a fence counter. virtio-gpu itself defines no vertical blank, flip or refresh timing in `virtio_gpu.h` or in QEMU's `virtio-gpu.c` (checked); its only handles are the flush rectangle, the fence flag and the cursor queue. (3) **GOAL, not design *(P)* (statement 3.9.1): what made graphics programming annoying, cumbersome or boilerplate is to be reduced by the architecture to almost nothing.** The chapter lists nineteen such pain points with illustrative (not measured) sizes of the boilerplate, worked pairs for getting a picture on the screen, the cache flush and drawing text, and the sugar each could have; of those, only the coherence rule (uncached framebuffer, no cache-maintenance instruction: 2.5.2, 3.15.1), the vertical-blank latch (3.10.9) and little-endian (errata 72) are decided. (4) **The form of "our virtio-console device" is open *(S)* (statement 3.11.2), and the author's description differs from the repository.** The repository's chapter 3 specified the native devices `VIC-64X`, `SID-64X` and `BLT64` (each statement *(D)*, *(P)* or *(S)*), deferred by errata 78 (2026-11-03) and obsolete as a model since errata 95 ("forget the native 8 register devices. gone. virtio across the board.", 2026-11-09); the standard video device is virtio-gpu in 2D mode and the standard audio device virtio-snd (errata 79). The virtio-console of the virtio specification, as `virtio_console.h` and `virtio_ids.h` on this machine show it, is a serial text device: device type 3, configuration `cols`, `rows`, `max_nr_ports`, `emerg_wr`, features `SIZE`, `MULTIPORT`, `EMERG_WRITE`, control messages for ports, byte streams on queues; it carries no cells, colours, glyphs or pixels. The repository mentions virtio-console only as a candidate for the console of the boot ROM and kernel (statement 5.5.5 *(S)*, and the open question Q6 of the decision records), never as a display. So the device the author describes (cells, colours, glyph slots and graphics, peek/poke) is none of the three: it is not the specification's virtio-console, not virtio-gpu 2D, and not yet any native device. The chapter does not resolve this: four readings are listed (a new virtio device type of the pdp64's own; virtio-gpu extended with a text layer and glyph slots; the real virtio-console as the text stream beside a separate display device; the native family renamed), tagged *(S)*. The terminal's properties he asks for, peek/poke text and graphics, modern colour depth and screen size and a glyph map that a TrueType engine fills, are recorded as *(P)* (3.11.1); the layout of a glyph slot and of a cell is *(S)* (3.11.3); sprites in the new device are *(S)* (3.11.4). (5) **The author's "vt55":** the repository names the VT52 (the `ESC Y` code, in the chats of `c64stuff.txt`) and the VT100/ANSI family, and nowhere a VT55; the chapter says that the name is the author's, describes the VT52 and ANSI codes by what the records say, and marks the details of the DEC terminal of that number unverified. (6) The sketches of 3.19 (layered scanout; layers plus a blitter and a glyph atlas; a command-queue device with a peek/poke window; virtio-gpu 2D with the conveniences in a library) are the answer to "what would a mashup look like"; they are the assistant's drawings of options, tagged *(P)*, with a leaning labelled as the assistant's; **nothing in them is decided.** *Open, the author's questions:* (a) what the "virtio-console" device is (the four readings above); (b) which sketch, or what combination; who composes the layers, where the glyphs live, whether sprites exist, whether there is a blitter, whether commands or pokes are the main interface; (c) whether a cell holds a character code (the author's c64x chat of 2026-06-18: "each cell holds its actual data, not a reference") or a slot number (implied by "glyph maps ... memory slots"); (d) the 48-bit pixel of errata 73 against the 8-bit-per-channel formats of virtio-gpu 2D; (e) the anti-tearing and boilerplate sugar, which candidates; (f) a write-combining memory type for the framebuffer; (g) VRAM or system memory on the first board and later. *Cited as a referent (2026-11-10):* the author, on why the history and the pain points should be told plainly: "still, is ok to mention the problems graphics coders faced, which is why we had 800 page michael abrash books". Recorded as a feature liked or cited: the depth of knowledge the graphics programmer was forced to carry (VGA register programming, planar memory, Mode X, bank switching, palette tricks, retrace timing, hand-written inner loops, per-chip quirks); the aim is the opposite, that the pdp64 needs none of it. The chapter names the books only as "Michael Abrash's books on PC graphics programming"; the titles, dates and page counts are unverified (no mention of him is in the repository, and no copy is on this machine), and "800 pages" is the author's figure, not checked. *Also asked (2026-11-10):* "with modern hdmi is tearing even a concern?"; answered in 3.8 ("Is this still a problem?") with the checked and unchecked parts separated, and the question whether tear-free flipping is worth hardware if the display side is variable-refresh is added to the open list (3.21). *GOAL (the author's, 2026-11-10; *(P)*, statement 3.9.3):* "graphics programming should be as simple and pleasurable as the c64 was."; the chapter states it as the measure the designs are held to. *PROPOSED DESIGN IDEA, not designed (the author's, 2026-11-10; *(P)*, statement 3.18.1):* "we will have a c64 mode, where a c128 is built onto the die with sid etc, so a bit of code loaded into its address space by pcie dma will let it act as a terminal the way the blit and vt55 did". *Clarified the same day (*(P)*, statement 3.18.2):* "the mode is just enabling/disabling the c128 as a pcie device so it takes over the graphics and audio output... or sends them to a compositor. not sure what that would look like. is there virtio-compositor?" Recorded: C64 mode is the on-die C128 appearing or disappearing as a PCI Express device; when enabled it takes over the graphics and audio output or sends them to a compositor; the form of the compositor path is open. Written up in 3.18 (what the Blit did, a terminal with its own small computer, the mechanics of loading a program by DMA with pseudocode and a figure, the questions, the options: hard core, soft core in the FPGA phase, software emulation on a pdp64 core, or no core and a virtio device served by a host process) and as sketch 5 of 3.19. Checked for the answer to "is there virtio-compositor?": **there is none**: no virtio device type of that name in `virtio_ids.h` or the other `virtio_*.h` headers on this machine; what comes near it is virtio-gpu's up to 16 scanouts, `SET_SCANOUT_BLOB`, the separate cursor queue, the capability sets VIRGL, VIRGL2 and GFXSTREAM_VULKAN with `context_init`, and, in QEMU's `virtio-gpu.rst`, the `virtio-gpu-rutabaga` variant's "Wayland display passthrough" with a `cross-domain` capset (the documentation says no more); virtio-snd and virtio-input exist. Five paths are drawn and none is decided: exclusive takeover, a second virtio-gpu head and sound stream, a surface for the host's compositor, layers at scanout, a new virtio device type (the process for a new type is unverified). *Open, the author's, from this idea:* which core ("c128" as written: the C128's 8502, Z80, VIC-IIe, 80-column VDC and SID are general knowledge, unverified; the repository records only the 8502 island of the chat-era design, `c64-devices-from-chat.md` section 4); a real core, an emulation, or a pdp64 core with the C64's registers; whether the mode also means unmodified C64 software; how video memory is shared with the host and virtio-gpu; the compositor path; how the core and the host talk; how the loaded image is authenticated and contained (the IOMMU of errata 75 and 85; code signing is his call and only a question here); **the tension with errata 78 and 95** (native registers deferred, "virtio across the board") which is stated and not resolved; the cost (a 6502-class core a few thousand gates: general knowledge, unverified; the estimate is the assistant's); the sound path to HDMI audio; the hand-over without tear or pop. *Not done, stated plainly:* no register, no specification file, no simulator, emulator or driver change; the figures and the pseudocode use placeholder names; the facts about the C64, the IBM PC adapters, the VT52 and VT100, GPU internals and Vulkan are general knowledge and marked unverified, since no manual of those machines is on this computer.

106. **GOAL, PREFERENCE AND QUESTION (the author's, 2026-11-10): bring over the simplicity of rio and NeWS; of the compositor paths the author prefers the c128 drawing into a buffer; and what is the audio equivalent of a compositor?** *The author's words:* (1) "it should mention blit terminal was inspiration for the rio and NeWS windowing systems, and we'd like to bring over what we can of that simplicity also"; (2) of the five compositor paths of errata 105, "c128 drawing into a buffer makes most sense"; (3) "what is the audio equivalent, what do those mixers do to combine multiple programs audio?" *Recorded, and not decided:* (1) **GOAL *(P)*, statement 3.19.1:** carry over what can be carried of the simplicity of the Blit's descendants; the means are open and listed with their trade-offs in 3.19 (a window as a plain buffer and a rectangle, drawing as writes, a composing server, downloadable server-side code with the c128 core as display server, no toolkit in the base system, windows as files or virtio queues). The chapter says what the sources confirm: the lineage Blit, `mux`, 8½, rio is in Pike's own papers as recorded in `kernel-pain-points.md` section 14I (8½: "the general model and appearance of 8½ are similar to those of mux"; rio replaced 8½); that the Blit inspired NeWS is the author's statement and **no source read makes the claim**; that NeWS was an extensible server to which clients downloaded programs is from the Handbook as recorded; that it was Sun's, in PostScript, by James Gosling, is general knowledge, unverified. (2) **PREFERENCE *(P)*, not a decision, statement 3.18.4:** path (c), the c128 renders into a buffer that the host compositor treats as a surface. The chapter says it is the author's leaning; the details under it (how the compositor learns the buffer is the core's, who owns the memory, frame timing) are open. (3) **The audio path** (3.20): what a sound card, a ring buffer, an underrun (audio's tearing), the SID's synthesis, a mixer, sample-rate conversion, latency against underrun and clock drift are; who the mixer is in real systems (general knowledge, unverified; `pulseaudio` and the ALSA library are on this machine, PipeWire is not; QEMU has a mixing engine, `audio/mixeng.c`, with ALSA, PulseAudio, PipeWire, CoreAudio, DirectSound, JACK and SDL backends, checked); what virtio-snd defines (`virtio_snd.h`, checked: four queues, PCM streams with a format bitmap from ADPCM to `FLOAT64`, a rate bitmap from 5,512 to 384,000 Hz, `PCM_SET_PARAMS` with `buffer_bytes` and `period_bytes`, TX/RX buffers with `stream_id`, a status with the current latency, period-elapsed and xrun events, optional controls) and that it says nothing about who mixes several programs (the host does in QEMU; the pdp64 has no host, so its mixer is its own open question, *(S)*, 3.20.2); and four options for the core's audio (exclusive takeover; the SID as a virtio-snd stream the host mixes; a fixed-gain mix in the device; a fade at hand-over), none decided, with no preference stated by the author for audio. *Numbering:* the chapter's sections after 3.18 moved (the windows section is 3.19 and the audio section 3.20, so the mashup sketches are 3.21, what was checked 3.22 and the open list 3.23); entry 105 was written with the earlier numbers (its 3.19, 3.20 and 3.21 are now 3.21, 3.22 and 3.23). *Open, the author's:* the window model; where the mixer lives; the form and rate of the SID's output; the compositor details; and everything listed in errata 105.

107. **REQUEST (the author's, 2026-11-10): the kernel chapter is rewritten from the point of view of errata 103.** *The author's words:* "do the kernel chapter next from the same point of view" (2026-11-10, after the bus chapter of errata 103 and the graphics chapter of errata 105). *Recorded, not decided:* (1) the kernel chapter (chapter 6) is rewritten for the two readers of errata 103, a first-year computer-science student and an experienced PDP-11 assembly programmer, neither knowing anything of computer architecture since the 1970s; (2) it is written, as the bus chapter was, from the point of view of the PDP-11 systems programmer who has written or read the V6 or V7 kernel, and explains each later idea where it first appears, with pseudocode and box-and-arrow figures; (3) the chapters not yet rewritten (boot, drivers, SMP, vectors, the instruction set and the rest) are **to follow** and are not decided here: nothing in this entry says whether, how or in what order they are rewritten. *Done:* [`manual/06-kernel.md`](manual/06-kernel.md) now begins with what a V7 and 2.11BSD kernel did (the status word at 0177776 with its current and previous mode, the vector pairs `trap; br7+n` at low memory, `trap()`, the KT11 with eight page registers per mode and `sureg()` and `estabur()`, `mfpi` and `mtpi`, whole-image swapping), checked against the V7M and 2.11BSD sources at `<work>/facetlang/unixtree`, and explains by contrast the status word and modes (6.2), the three kinds of entry and the registers that exception entry writes (6.3), precise faults and why they matter on an out-of-order core (6.4), the system registers (6.5), the step from segments to pages with the walk in pseudocode (6.6), the page-table entry bit by bit and the memory types (6.7), software-managed accessed and dirty bits, the translation buffer, the invalidate and its broadcast, and address-space numbers (6.8), the split of the space, the direct-mapping windows and the layout of an address space (6.9), the user-memory guard and system calls (6.10), a system call, a page fault, a timer interrupt, a context switch and the idle loop in pseudocode (6.11), the reset state, the first pages of physical memory and the first kernel (6.12), the system instructions (6.13), what was checked (6.14) and what is open (6.15); with eight figures (`tools/manual/kernfigs.py`, hooked in `tools/mkmanual.py`: the KT11 beside the pdp64 walk, the tree of tables, the page-table entry, the translation buffer, exception entry and the vector table, a context switch, the layout of an address space, the first pages of physical memory). The pseudocode of 6.11 and 6.12 is the assistant's illustration; no pdp64 kernel exists. *Every old numbered statement and tag is kept; the numbering moved.* *Old to new (the earlier errata keep the numbers they were written with):* 6.1.1-6 to 6.2.1-6; 6.2.1-7 to 6.3.1-7; 6.3.1-2 to 6.5.1-2; 6.4.1 to 6.6.1; 6.4.1a to 6.6.1a; 6.4.2 to 6.7.1; 6.4.3 to 6.7.2; 6.4.4 to 6.7.3; 6.4.5 to 6.8.1; 6.4.6 to 6.8.2; 6.4.7 to 6.8.3; 6.4.8 to 6.7.4; 6.4.9 to 6.9.1; 6.5.1-4 to 6.10.1-4; 6.6.1-3 to 6.12.1-3; 6.7.1-8 to 6.13.1-8 (the instruction entries of the generator are now between the markers `entries:6.13`); 6.8.1-3 to 6.4.1-3; 6.8.4 to 6.6.2; 6.8.5 to 6.7.5; 6.8.6 to 6.9.2; 6.8.7 to 6.4.4; 6.9 (still open) to 6.15. By section: old 6.1 is new 6.2, 6.2 is 6.3, 6.3 is 6.5, 6.4 is 6.6 to 6.9 (6.6 the levels, 6.7 the entry, 6.8 the bits, the buffer and the tags, 6.9 the split and the windows), 6.5 is 6.10, 6.6 is 6.12, 6.7 is 6.13, 6.8 is 6.4 (faults) with its page-table statements in 6.6, 6.7 and 6.9, 6.9 is 6.15; new are 6.1, 6.11 and 6.14. The citations in chapter 9 and its generator (`tools/manual/isa_part1.py` to `isa_part4.py`, `isa_entries.py`), chapter 2 (2.4.9: 6.8.3 is now 6.4.3), the appendices (A.8.1: 6.5.4 is now 6.10.4; the glossary rows), the index, `docs/architecture-recommendations.md`, `docs/manual-update-plan.md` and `handoff.md` were updated in context; `docs/encoding-work/manual-handwritten.md` records edits made under the old numbers and keeps them. *Found while writing, listed in the chapter (6.15) and not decided:* (a) the instruction after the write that turns translation on is fetched through the new tables, and the repository does not say what covers it (an identity mapping of the boot code for that moment, or a direct-mapping window, whose format is open); the older text said that no trampoline is needed, which holds only with a window; (b) the architecture document says the machine comes up with `DMW0` covering physical memory, which disagrees with translation being off at reset (6.8.2); the manual follows the second; (c) the system-register table has no number for `KTBR` (6.9.1 was accepted by errata 71 and is not implemented in the simulator or the QEMU model, which have one `PTBR`); (d) nothing says how the kernel saves and restores a thread's user `DP` when it switches threads; (e) the system-call register convention is "by the ABI" and appendix D does not give it; (g) the entry of `MFSR` in chapter 9 and the reference executor flush the whole translation buffer on a write to `PTBR`, `ASID` or `MMUCTL`, which defeats the address-space numbers; (f) the simulator and the QEMU model still check the memory type of a page against bit 39 (errata 65, removed by errata 97). *Open, the author's:* everything of 6.15, above all the level and width discovery (errata 96, 102(b); 6.6.1a), the page-table entry for wide physical addresses (errata 102(c); 6.7.1), `KTBR` and the window format, how many pages "the first few" are, and the questions (a) to (e) above.

108. **REQUEST (the author's, 2026-11-10): the boot chapter is rewritten from the point of view of errata 103.** *The author's words:* "do the boot chapter next from the same point of view" (2026-11-10, after the bus chapter of errata 103, the graphics chapter of errata 105 and the kernel chapter of errata 107). *Recorded, not decided:* (1) the boot chapter (chapter 4) is rewritten for the two readers of errata 103, a first-year computer-science student and an experienced PDP-11 assembly programmer, neither knowing anything of computer architecture since the 1970s; (2) it is written from the point of view of the PDP-11 programmer who has keyed a bootstrap in on the front panel, used a boot ROM and the console `ODT`, or typed `boot` at the `:` prompt of V7 or 2.11BSD, and explains each later idea by contrast, with pseudocode and box-and-arrow figures; (3) the drivers chapter (5) and the remaining chapters (the instruction set and the rest; the SMP, vector and conclusion chapters) are **to follow** and are not decided here: nothing in this entry says whether, how or in what order they are rewritten. *Done:* [`manual/04-boot.md`](manual/04-boot.md) now begins with what a PDP-11 did (4.1: the front panel, the boot ROM, block 0, the two-stage bootstrap and its `#` and `:` prompts, the 512-byte limit and the registers of 2.11BSD, `ODT`, what the PDP-11 lacked), checked against the V7M and 2.11BSD sources at `<work>/facetlang/unixtree` and the OpenBSD sources at `<work>/kernel-src/openbsd`; then what the PC added (4.2: the BIOS and its four jobs, the boot sector and its 446 bytes, the GPT, UEFI), what takes the place of a BIOS and why there are stages (4.3), the reset state, the ROM in pseudocode and the first pages of memory (4.4), the disk with the first stage and its block list (4.5), the second stage and the boot-information block (4.6), starting the other cores (4.7), starting the devices (4.8), why UEFI is not used and what a shim would be (4.9, errata 109), recovery (4.10), what was checked (4.11) and what is open (4.12); with seven figures (`tools/manual/bootfigs.py`, hooked in `tools/mkmanual.py`: the boot stages as a chain, memory when block 0 is loaded, the protective MBR and GPT with the disk label, the boot-information block, the start of the other cores as a time line, the PC's BIOS and UEFI paths beside the pdp64's, the place of a UEFI shim). The pseudocode of 4.4 to 4.10 is the assistant's illustration; no pdp64 boot ROM, first stage, second stage or shim exists. *Every old numbered statement and tag is kept; the numbering moved.* *Old to new (the earlier errata keep the numbers they were written with):* 4.1.1-3 to 4.3.1-3; 4.2.1-4 to 4.4.1-4; 4.3.1-4 to 4.5.1-4; 4.4.1-4 to 4.6.1-4; 4.5.1-2 to 4.7.1-2; 4.5.3 to 4.8.1; 4.6.1-2 to 4.9.1-2; 4.7.1-2 to 4.10.1-2. By section: old 4.1 is new 4.3, 4.2 is 4.4, 4.3 is 4.5, 4.4 is 4.6, 4.5 is 4.7 (the cores) and 4.8 (the devices), 4.6 is 4.9, 4.7 is 4.10 and 4.8 (still open) is 4.12; new sections are 4.1 (the PDP-11), 4.2 (the PC) and 4.11 (what was checked). So errata 47 and 50 speak of the statements now 4.3.2, 4.5.1 and 4.5.2, and "section 4.4.2" of the earlier text is 4.6.2. *Found while writing, not decided:* (a) the boot review took `hpuboot.s` for the second stage; it is the primary bootstrap of block 0 (its own header says "block 0 bootstrap" and the V7M `boot(8)` page says so), and the second stage is `boot.c`; (b) the first stage has 446 bytes and no firmware to call (4.3.1), yet must read the blocks of its list through a virtio queue: how is not specified (4.5, 4.12 (b)); (c) the ROM must find and map the boot device before any software has assigned windows (4.4); (d) 4.8.1 speaks of an `INIT` bit and `READY`, words of the eight-register devices that errata 95 withdrew, and virtio has no firmware operation (4.8); (e) the SMP chapter's 7.8.1 loads `r0` only where 4.7.1 loads `r1` too. *Open, the author's:* the items of 4.12.

109. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the pdp64 boots by the block-0 route; UEFI firmware is not part of the specification and is not built; the hand-off and the second stage are kept simple enough that a UEFI shim could be written by someone who wanted one.** *The record.* On 2026-10-22 23:08 (R256) the author asked of boot: "look into uefi; is it worth it at all? can't we do what pdp11 used to do, load 512 byte block 0 then jmp 0? (not precisely true, but directionally true)"; at 23:15 (R257) he asked how UEFI plays into partitions and the GPT and said "we'll have an open source boot sector boot loader as part of the spec; that is part of the spec that the boot loader/bios is open source" (errata 47); errata 50 (the GPT wrapper with the protective MBR) followed. The sections 4.6.1 and 4.6.2 of the earlier text of chapter 4 ("the pdp64 does not use UEFI"; "a UEFI shim could be written by someone who wanted one") were the assistant's proposals, tagged *(P)*, and never the author's decision. On 2026-11-10 the author asked "i thought we did decide to allow uefi? check all the threads where we discussed it" (the threads were checked: no decision to allow UEFI was found in any recorded message), then "or we kept the originnal simple pdp11 boot block method?", and, given three options, chose: "option 2, block 0 with a shim possible". *Decided:* (1) the pdp64 boots by the block-0 route, the PDP-11 method of manual 4.4 and 4.5; (2) UEFI firmware is not part of the specification and is not built; (3) the hand-off (the boot-information block) and the second stage are kept simple enough that a UEFI shim could be written by someone who wanted one. Manual 4.9.1 and 4.9.2 are now *(D)*. *The shim's scope (the author's questions, and the answer).* He asked "not sure why firmware and coff are needed for uefi, with virtio native that shuld be minimal, no?" and then "yes, state the shim's real scope". Virtio removes most of the driver mass; what stays for a shim is (i) **PE/COFF**: the UEFI specification requires UEFI applications to be PE32+ images, a property of the loader programs and not of the firmware, so running an existing UEFI loader means building it as a PE/COFF image for the pdp64, which needs the toolchain to produce one and a machine-type code for the pdp64 to be assigned by the owners of the UEFI specification (**both unverified general knowledge**: the specification is not on this machine); and (ii) **a small services layer** that provides the few boot-services calls a loader uses (memory allocation and the memory map, reading a file from the boot partition, the console and graphics output protocols, exit-boot-services, a runtime clock and reset) over virtio, plus a **hand-off of a device description** to the kernel (a device tree or tables). *Measured on this machine (manual 4.9, 4.11):* OpenBSD's three `efiboot` loaders (`<work>/kernel-src/openbsd/sys/arch/{amd64,arm64,riscv64}/stand/efiboot`) call about fifteen to seventeen distinct boot and runtime services (`HandleProtocol`, `AllocatePages`, `LocateHandle`, `LocateProtocol`, `GetMemoryMap`, `FreePages`, `ExitBootServices`, `ResetSystem`, `GetVariable`, `SetVariable`, `GetTime` and the rest listed there) and the console, disk, file, network and graphics protocols; the arm64 loader is linked as ELF and wrapped as PE32+ by a 176-line header in assembler (`start.S`, machine type `0xaa64`) and a 124-line self-relocator, so a PE/COFF output need not be a linker feature; the loaders are 4,593 (amd64), 7,195 (arm64) and 4,513 (riscv64) lines. *The cost figure replaced.* The boot review's "hundreds of thousands to millions of lines" for UEFI firmware (`docs/boot.md`, "Is UEFI worth it?") was unconfirmed and describes a full firmware stack such as EDK II; a shim is a different and much smaller thing, and the figure is no longer used for it. **No line count of a shim is given: none can be measured here** (no implementation of the UEFI services is on this machine: the `edk2` and `u-boot` directories under `qemu-src/roms` are empty; only EDK II's built images are present, the x86-64 code image being 3,653,632 bytes).
