The other costs stay as the review had them (a loader to port to the pdp64, a services layer to write and keep, more code to trust before the kernel, a platform description still needed), and the failure modes of firmware (variable store, drivers, boot-order state, a setup screen, resident code) are not incurred. *What was done with it:* manual 4.9.1 and 4.9.2 tagged *(D)* with the wording above; manual 4.9 written for the two readers with a services-table sketch (an illustration, no such code exists); `docs/boot.md` ("Is UEFI worth it?" and decision B5) says it is decided, with the shim's scope; appendix B (B.2.13.1) and the system architecture (section 9) say so. *Not decided:* the loader's path, which services a shim offers (a given loader needs its own set), the machine-type number, and whether a shim is ever built.

110. **CONFIRMATION AND REQUEST (the author's, 2026-11-10): UEFI is an open specification and the door-open choice is good; chapter 4 compares five boots.** *The author's words:* (1) "if uefi isn't an open spec how are all the open source os supporting it? it requires binary firmware blobs? I guess leaving the door open to uefe support but having the classic mbr fallback, gpt wrapper is a good choice then."; (2) "also boot would compare pdp11 boot, vax boot, c64 boot, the x86 bios boot with mbr, then finally the gpt/uefi". *Recorded, not a new decision:* (1) is a **confirmation of option 2 of errata 109** (block 0 with a shim possible): he thinks it a good choice; nothing is added to or changed in 109. The question is answered in manual 4.9 by what could be checked: the UEFI specification is published by the UEFI Forum and readable without charge (general knowledge, **unverified**: not on this machine, and the terms of implementing it are not known); an open-source implementation, EDK II, is under BSD-2-Clause-Patent (**read** in `qemu-src/pc-bios/edk2-licenses.txt` and the Debian OVMF copyright file; built images are on this machine, its source is not); open-source operating systems write the loader side that runs on top of firmware already on the machine (OpenBSD's `efiboot` read) and ship no firmware; the proprietary blobs are in the firmware shipped on real PCs, the vendors' builds and the CPU vendor's initialisation code (unverified); Secure Boot adds a signed-loader chain (unverified); and block 0 of the pdp64 disk already has the protective MBR and GPT layout (errata 50), so the classic route and a future UEFI route share one disk. Text that called the firmware closed was reworded (4.3: it is the shipped firmware, not the specification) and `docs/boot.md` says the specification is published. *The request (2)* the author named PDP-11, VAX, C64, x86 BIOS with MBR and GPT with UEFI as his referents; manual 4.11 (new) compares them, each as a numbered step list, a figure of the five chains with the pdp64's, and a side-by-side table of nine properties whose last column says which of the five the pdp64 resembles. Checked on this machine: V7M, 2.11BSD, 4.3BSD-Tahoe (`raboot.c`, `vaxstand/boot.c`, `rpb.h`) and OpenBSD sources; **everything about the Commodore 64 boot, the VAX console and VMB, the x86 reset vector and `0x7C00`, UEFI's boot manager and NVRAM variables, and Secure Boot is unverified**. *Found:* in the 4.3BSD source `rpb.h` is the restart block of a crash dump, not a boot hand-off; the VAX hand-off of the 4.3BSD boot is `r10` and `r11`, and what DEC's boot program passed is unverified (4.6 and `docs/boot.md` reworded). *Old to new (the earlier errata keep the numbers they were written with):* the new section is 4.11; old 4.11 (what was checked) is 4.12 and old 4.12 (still open) is 4.13, so errata 108 and 109 speak of 4.11 and 4.12 where 4.12 and 4.13 are meant now; no statement number moved.

111. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): the kernel chapter revised after errata 107 for flow, plain wording and verification.** *The author's words:* "the 3 chapters you just updated, go back, remove all ai slop, verify everything, and structure it nicely with good flow the way i expressed it to you"; "make sure each chapter flows from pdp11 to pdp64 with relevant precedents in between"; "where the structure and flow is already good, keep it of course". *Done:* [`manual/06-kernel.md`](manual/06-kernel.md) keeps its section order, its figures and every numbered statement with its tag; no statement number moved, so no citation changed (old to new map: none). The commentary blocks (Precedent, Trade-offs, Improvements, Benefits, Modern comparables, Use, Impact) are replaced by one *Lineage* paragraph per section that gives the PDP-11 way, the intermediate machines with what each contributed (each marked *unverified* where the handbook or source is not on this machine) and the pdp64 way with the reason; filler openers and closers were removed. *Corrections to the earlier text:* (1) the V7 vector listing had a line `. = ZERO+4` that is not in `conf/l.s`, and the system-call dispatch is `fuiword((caddr_t)(a-1))`, not `pc-1`; (2) the V7 `idle` clears the priority bits and waits, and only 2.11BSD uses `SPLLOW`; (3) the vector-table paragraph omitted the reserved vectors 64 to 255; (4) the context-switch pseudocode and figure did not save the outgoing process's user stack pointer (`MFSR USP`) and `return_to_user` did not restore the user registers from the trap frame; (5) the figure of the first pages said the PDP-11 vectors filled octal 0 to 0376, which is not verified (V7's table goes past 0224); (6) the processor-status address 0177776 and the KT11 enable write (`mov $1,SSR0`) are now checked in the Unix sources and moved out of the unverified list of 6.14; (7) the claim that the shootdown is a source of deadlocks in the BSDs is reduced to what `docs/kernel-pain-points.md` says (IPI deadlock cases counted among the costs); (8) the sentence that supervisor mode is kept because adding a mode would change the status word has no source and was removed; (9) 6.14 now says that the simulator's status word has no `UA` bit (errata 43 decided it; not implemented) and the same is listed in 6.15. Findings (a) to (g) of errata 107 stand. `tools/manual/kernfigs.py` changed in two strings (the context-switch figure and the first-pages note).

112. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): the boot chapter revised after errata 108 to 110 for flow, plain wording and verification, at the author's instruction "remove all ai slop, verify everything, and structure it nicely with good flow" and "flows from pdp11 to pdp64 with relevant precedents in between".** *Structure:* section 4.2 now holds the precedents in the author's order (the VAX, the Commodore 64, the PC with a BIOS and an MBR, the GPT with UEFI), each as steps with the one thing it added, moved from the old 4.11; section 4.11 keeps only the figure, the pdp64 paragraph and the side-by-side table; each of 4.3 to 4.10 has a "Chain" line from the PDP-11 through the precedents to the pdp64. **No section or statement number moved**, so no citation changes; the numbered statements and tags are unchanged. *Errors found and fixed:* (1) `hp_sizes[8]` and "cyl 44 thru 429" are in V7's `usr/sys/dev/hp.c`; the V7M `hp.c` has `hp_sizes[24]` (three drive types); (2) "roughly 512 bytes of code" is in `biosboot(8)` and the `installboot` patching and "its inode may change" are in `biosboot(8)`, not `biosboot.S`; (3) the 2.11BSD check word is declared in `pdpstand/boot.c` ("one's complements of bootopts"); the lines read do not say it travels in `r2`, so that is no longer stated; (4) 4.3BSD-Tahoe `raboot.c` takes the Unibus I/O page address in `r1` (the adapter number is cut from it); (5) the OpenBSD efiboot loaders call 17 distinct boot and runtime services (amd64) and 18 (arm64, riscv64), counted; errata 109 and `boot.md` said "about fifteen to seventeen" (`boot.md` corrected); (6) the stage count of the PC (four programs before the kernel) is now stated against the PDP-11's three and V7's own two; (7) the V7M `htboot.s` comment names the M9312 only as the boot for the TS11, so "one ROM program per device" is marked unverified (`boot.md` too); (8) the 2026-11-10 quotations of the author are not in the decision log, only in errata 109 and 110, and the chapter says so; (9) the figure `boot-stages` said the first stage is 512 bytes, now 446 bytes of code, and two figures' notes were corrected (`bootfigs.py`: the shim count, the 4.3BSD-Tahoe source). *Re-counted and confirmed:* `hpuboot.s` 285, `boot.c` 272, `mbr.S` 521, `biosboot.S` 862, amd64 `boot` 189, `libsa` 6,319, `efiboot` 4,593, 7,195 and 4,513, `start.S` 176, `self_reloc.c` 124, the amd64 stand total 15,440, EDK II image 3,653,632 bytes, Linux `smpboot.c` 1,528, `realmode` 1,258, PSCI 124, spin table 127, SBI 108, spinwait 58; the cross-references to 2.3.6, 2.4.1, 2.6.3, 2.9.4, 5.5.1, 6.12, 7.4.2, 7.6, 7.8.1 and 7.8.2 hold. *Removed:* claims about Open Firmware, U-Boot, OpenSBI and the SRM console that carried no source (the "Precedent" lists), the unsupported "a few hundred bytes of ROM" as fact (now an estimate), and the filler and restatement of the old commentary.

113. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): the bus chapter was edited for flow and checked against the sources.** *The author's words:* "bus chapter too"; "make sure each chapter flows from pdp11 to pdp64 with relevant precedents in between." No statement number moved and no tag changed. *Added:* the route table at the end of 2.1 (PDP-11, VAX, PCI, PCI Express, virtio, Q64) and a "Chain" in each commentary. *Corrected:* "the first 64 bytes are the same for every device" (only the first 16 bytes share a layout; a bridge's header differs); the notification capability is four bytes longer, not "two words"; the `init_device` listing lost the ACKNOWLEDGE bit when it wrote DRIVER; `config_msix_vector` is `msix_config` in `virtio_pci.h`; link speeds are not exact doublings (5 to 8 GT/s); the vendor ID 0x1AF4 is QEMU's `PCI_VENDOR_ID_REDHAT_QUMRANET`, and "Red Hat made the specification" is removed; "the kernel is built with a table" is replaced by what 2.11BSD does (`rkprobe`, the RK11 at `0177400` in `pdpstand/rk.c`). *Now verified, no longer marked unverified:* the RK11 addresses, vector `0220` at BR5, the vector-4 bus error, the map registers at `0170200`, the VAX `NEX780`, `NEXSIZE`, `probenexi`, `unifind`, `UMEM780`, `UBA_PURGE780`. *Marked unverified or removed:* the V7 `nofault` idiom is now cited to `m40.s`; the 11/70 cache, "x86 DMA is coherent", the Q-bus, AXI exclusive access, Thunderbolt, CXL, Amiga AutoConfig and NuBus, lane counts beyond x8, "writing 0 resets", MSI data variation, PCI Express interrupt-pin emulation. The cross-references to 6.4.3 and 6.7.1 were checked against the files as they stood.

114. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): the graphics chapter was reordered where its flow broke, edited for plain wording, and checked against the sources.** *The author's words:* "the 3 chapters you just updated, go back, remove all ai slop, verify everything, and structure it nicely with good flow the way i expressed it to you"; "make sure each chapter flows from pdp11 to pdp64 with relevant precedents in between"; "where the structure and flow is already good, keep it of course". *Done:* [`manual/03-graphics.md`](manual/03-graphics.md) keeps sections 3.1 to 3.7 (the history to the GPU) and 3.18 to 3.23 in place, with their figures; the terminal, its display plumbing and the virtio-gpu route now come before the pain-point and tearing sections, which follow the author's order (the terminal, then what made graphics programming annoying, then tearing, then the c64 mode). A chain table (PDP-11 teletype, VT52 and VT100, Blit, C64, VGA, GPU, pdp64 terminal) opens the chapter and each commentary names its PDP-11 antecedent or says there is none. *Old to new statement map* (every tag unchanged): 3.8.1-2 to 3.17.1-2; 3.9.1-3 to 3.16.1-3; 3.10.1-9 to 3.8.1-9; 3.11.1-4 to 3.9.1-4; 3.13.1-2 to 3.11.1-2; 3.14.1-5 to 3.12.1-5; 3.15.1-4 to 3.13.1-4; 3.16.1-4 to 3.14.1-4; 3.17.1-3 to 3.15.1-3; 3.12 (the peek/poke program, unnumbered) to 3.10; 3.18 to 3.23 unchanged. Citations updated: the glossary (HDMI transmitter 3.12, blitter 3.8, scanout and tearing 3.17, glyph map 3.9), `decisions-pending.md` E3 and E5 (3.8.4, 3.8.7, 3.8.5), `c64-devices-from-chat.md` section 5 (3.8.2) and the figure text in `gfxfigs.py` (3.9.3). Errata 105 and 106 keep the numbers they were written with (3.10.9 is now 3.8.9, and so on, by the map above); older documents that cite the pre-rewrite numbers (3.1.x of `kernel-pain-points.md` and `architecture-recommendations.md`) were stale before and are left. *Corrections to the earlier text:* (1) the virtio-gpu sequence has five commands (create, attach backing, set scanout, transfer, flush), not six, as the figure shows; statement 3.14.1 now says "five of the figure"; (2) "Original Sin" is in section 14G of `kernel-pain-points.md`, not 14I; (3) the quotation attributed to Q6 of the decision records ("virtio-console optional for ordinary use") is not in the records and was replaced by statement 5.5.5; (4) "presumably 16 bits per channel" for the 48-bit pixel contradicted errata 73 (four 12-bit channels); (5) the VT52 code is at `c64stuff.txt` line 4888 and "VT100/ANSI" at lines 218 and 4875, not 4888 for both; (6) the Blit redrew its cursor at 60 Hz (`kernel-pain-points.md`), not "at each clock interrupt"; (7) "flip on vsync unconditionally" is the assistant's accepted proposal (`c64stuff.txt` line 5647), not the author's words; (8) the `put_char` listing passed a slot to a function that looked a slot up, and `put_pixel` used 4 bytes for a pixel the text calls 48 bits; both were rewritten; (9) `DRM_MODE_ATOMIC_*` are in `drm_mode.h` only, and the 2.11BSD console files are `cons.h.gz` and `cons.c.gz` under `sys/pdp`; (10) the claim that rio's size could be read "in an afternoon" confused the paper's "small size" with a conversion of the code to threads and was removed; (11) PipeWire's client library is installed on this machine, its daemon is not. *Marked unverified or removed:* a few thousand lookup tables for the terminal, about 25 lines for a virtio-gpu start-up, "every virtualised PC uses virtio-gpu", "every embedded display controller has a 2-D blitter", the 11/70 cache, the Amiga blitter, the Cyclone V and Zynq 7020 lacking a hard PCI Express block, the toolkit comparison between X and rio, the C128's chips. *Questions for the author:* whether statements 3.8.4 and 3.8.5 are *(P)*, as the chapter tags them, or *(D)*, as `decisions-pending.md` E3 and E5 say; whether the audio section should precede the windows section (the chain of errata 106 asked for compositor and audio together).

115. **REQUEST (the author's, 2026-11-10): the drivers chapter is rewritten from the point of view of errata 103, with the author's rules for all chapters.** *The author's words:* "do the drivers chapter next" (2026-11-10, after the bus, graphics, kernel and boot chapters); "remove all ai slop, verify everything, and structure it nicely with good flow"; "make sure each chapter flows from pdp11 to pdp64 with relevant precedents in between"; "where the structure and flow is already good, keep it of course"; "not everything needs a source, just they are nice to have"; "just because you don't have a source, search for sources before removing things. be thorough; some things, the source is by inference rather than direct statements, and that is ok."; "the whole point of doing the status and decisions document is so we have a source of truth, all docs and manuals must follow it as authoritative". *Recorded, not decided:* (1) [`manual/05-drivers.md`](manual/05-drivers.md) (chapter 5) is rewritten for the two readers of errata 103, a first-year computer-science student and an experienced PDP-11 assembly programmer, neither knowing anything of computer architecture since the 1970s, from the point of view of the programmer who has written or read V6, V7 or 2.11BSD drivers; (2) the chapters not yet rewritten (the SMP chapter, the instruction set, the appendices) are **to follow** and are not decided here. *Done:* the chapter now runs: the PDP-11 driver (5.1: `rk.c`, the CSR, `INIT` and `READY`, `bdevsw` and `cdevsw`, `struct buf`, vectors and BR levels, `spl`, the clist and the KL11 console, autoconfiguration, `physio`, map registers, the Q-bus), what came between (5.2: the VAX, PCI, virtio, OpenBSD, with the author's own referents first), what a driver is on the pdp64 (5.3), the interface it programs (5.4), its life (5.5), the disk (5.6), the console, timer and entropy source (5.7, open, nothing decided), the network, graphics, sound and USB drivers (5.8), interrupts (5.9), DMA and the buffer's memory (5.10), errors (5.11), firmware (5.12), where drivers run and how they are written in C (5.13), testing (5.14), what was checked (5.15) and what is open (5.16); with five figures (`tools/manual/drvfigs.py`, hooked in `tools/mkmanual.py`) and pseudocode for a virtio-blk driver's probe, request and interrupt handler, a polled loop beside the interrupt version, a complete tiny console driver and a timer tick handler, all marked as illustrations because no pdp64 driver exists. *Old to new statement map* (every tag unchanged): 5.1.1-4 to 5.3.2-5; 5.2.1 to 5.3.1; 5.2.2-6 to 5.4.1-5; 5.2.7 to 5.6.1; 5.3.1-5 to 5.5.1-5; 5.4.1-4 to 5.10.1-4; 5.5.1 to 5.6.2; 5.5.2-4 to 5.8.1-3; 5.5.5 to 5.7.1; 5.6.1-4 to 5.11.1-4; 5.7.1-4 to 5.9.1-4; 5.8.1-3 to 5.13.1-3; 5.9 (still open, unnumbered) to 5.16; new: 5.12.1 *(S)* (firmware). *Citations updated:* chapters 2 (5.2.2, 5.2.5, 5.1.3, 5.4.3, 5.4.4, 5.7.1, 5.7.2, 5.4.2, 5.8.2), 3 (5.5.5), 4 (5.5.1, twice) and 6 (section 5.7), the glossary, `handoff.md`, `smp-decisions.md` (5.7.4), `decisions-pending.md` (5.5.2, 5.2.7, 5.3.5) and four rows of `kernel-pain-points.md` (5.4.2, 5.8.2, 5.4.1, 5.1.4, 5.1.1); the other rows of that file cite the pre-rewrite text (the `IRQ` register, slots, the 64-bit counter at 5.5.4) and were stale before; errata 29, 49, 61, 75 and 95 keep their old numbers as history. `docs/decisions-and-status.md` was not touched. *Errors found in the old chapter:* (1) `config_msix_vector` is `msix_config` in `virtio_pci.h`, and the driver's feature fields are `guest_feature_select` and `guest_feature` there; (2) 5.6.2 says a reset device leaves its queues untouched, but OpenBSD's `virtio_init_vq` zeroes the ring and restarts both indices at 0 and its `vioblk_reset` fails the unfinished requests, so the resend of 5.5.4 and 5.6.2 is the pdp64's proposal and not that driver's practice (statements unchanged; commentary says so); (3) the old chapter said the same devices "run in QEMU, `sim64` and the FPGA" as a fact, but no virtio device, root complex, interrupt controller or IOMMU exists in the repository (5.14); (4) the old "OpenBSD has drivers ... by the listing recorded in `security-proposals.md`" is now read in the OpenBSD 8.0 tree at `facetlang/pdp11/openbsd-src`, which has `if_vio.c`, `vioblk.c`, `viocon.c`, `viogpu.c`, `viomb.c`, `viornd.c`, `vioscsi.c` and no virtio-snd driver, which answers the question left open by errata 79 for that tree; (5) the old text did not say that the console and timer of the QEMU machine exist (a KL11-shaped console and a counter and compare timer at fixed addresses behind the withdrawn bit 39); (6) 5.5.5's candidates omitted virtio-console's emergency write, which prints with no queue (found in `virtio_console.h` and QEMU), and the `VIRTIO_ID_CLOCK` and `virtio_rtc.h` that the briefing called unverified; (7) the claim that virtio's queue layout for port 0 of the console is unverified (chapter 3) is now read in QEMU: queues 0 and 1. *Removed or reworded from the old chapter* (nothing else): the lead's "short" in "a short ordinary program" (the transport and class drivers of OpenBSD are 1,075 to 2,184 lines each, counted); the 3-line `descriptor`/`available ring`/`used ring` code block after 5.2.3 (repeats the figures of chapter 2); the statement-level text is otherwise unchanged. The old comparables about rings with a done mark, the 11/70 cache, `swiotlb`, requeueing after a reset and all-ones for a removed device are kept and marked *unverified*. *Agreement with the ledger* (`decisions-and-status.md`): the Decided lines for virtio, the standard GPU, NIC and audio, USB and NVMe, hot-plug and the first operating system agree with the chapter; the ledger lists the entropy and device-initialisation subjects as IN THE AIR (the chapter says so, and keeps the `INIT` and `READY` idea as the antecedent of the `device_status` handshake), shows the virtio-only chapter rewrite as TO DO and says `errata.md` ends at 93, which are out of date. *Open questions for the author:* the form of the console, timer and entropy source (briefing Q6; whether the emergency write changes the console option); the interrupt controller and vector numbering (Q7, R1); how a virtio device is given firmware; which virtio devices "make sense" in silicon and the syntactic-sugar question of R359; whether the Q-bus (11/23 and later) belongs in the chain beyond what drivers see (it is in 5.1 only for its 22-bit memory and the bounce buffer, read in 2.11BSD).

117. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): the kernel chapter after the second pass: what the first edit pass of errata 111 had removed is restored where the repository supports it; implementation remarks are moved out.** *The author's words:* "supervisor mode rationale; don't remove it without telling me what it said"; "search for sources before removing things"; "the manual is about the arch as it is to be, not as it is right now". *Old to new map:* none; no statement number moved and no tag changed. *Restored:* (1) the supervisor-mode reasoning of the old commentary to 6.2.1, now a paragraph at the end of 6.2 labelled the assistant's inference, with what the repository says (`docs/pdp64-system-design.md`: the mode costs a third bank of two registers and is an unresolved question; `docs/pdp64-system-architecture.md` section 1: carried from the PDP-11) and the check against the status word: the mode field is two bits, so three modes with the value 2 reserved fill it at no cost and dropping supervisor mode would free no bit; the old reason, that adding a mode would change the status word, holds only for a fifth mode, since a fourth would take the value 2; (2) the Impact lines that carry information (kernel, compiler, hardware) for 6.2, 6.3, 6.4, 6.5, 6.6, 6.7, 6.8, 6.9, 6.10, 6.12 and 6.13, and the trade-offs they held (two opcodes against one instruction per register; the alignment check of a large page; the half bit and second root of the split; the open and close around every user copy); (3) the 2018 flaw as the reason for the split of 6.9.1 is no longer marked unverified: `docs/kernel-pain-points.md` row 1 and `docs/hardware-vulnerabilities.md` section 3.2 (Meltdown, CVE-2017-5754, KAISER merged January 2018) support it, and so the claim that x86 kernels paid a table switch only after 2018; (4) the remark that other architectures mask interrupts on entry, marked unverified; (5) the reasoning that a read-only walker is preferable to hardware-managed accessed and dirty bits, marked the assistant's and unverified (no repository document names a flaw in the hardware form). The glosses of strongly and weakly ordered, the VAX regions, MIPS `KSEG0`, x86 `PCID` and AArch64 `TTBR0` and `TTBR1` were already restored in the Lineage paragraphs, each marked as checked or unverified. *Removals kept:* the old Impact lines that said "Compiler: None" and the restatements of the Lineage text. *Implementation remarks moved* to `docs/implementation-status.md`, with their exact text: the simulator and QEMU facts of 6.6, 6.7, 6.9.1, 6.10 and 6.13.8, the FPGA buffer size (and the note of the figure `kern-tlb` in `tools/manual/kernfigs.py`), the executor's flush of the whole buffer, the missing `UA` bit and the bit-39 check, and the statement that no kernel exists. Findings (a) to (e) and (g) of errata 107 stand in 6.15 as questions about the specification (for (g), only that the entry of `MFSR` in chapter 9 states a flush that disagrees with 6.8); (f) and the `UA` finding of errata 111 are implementation facts and are in the status file. The questions about the system-call register convention and the number of `KTBR` stay open.

sections 4.1 to 4.13 and every statement and tag are unchanged (4.9 stays before 4.10). *Restored, with sources:* (1) the M9312 contract with block 0, read in V7M `sas/htboot.s` ("leaves the CSR address in r1") and 2.11BSD `mdec/rkuboot.s` (`nop`, `br`, "DEC boot ROMs will refuse to run boot block", `r0` unit, `r1` csr), the per-device ROM program as inference; (2) `ODT`, now read in 2.11BSD `man4/cons.4` (prompts `>>>` and `@`); (3) `r2` as the check word of 2.11BSD, read in the `pdpstand/boot.c` comment; (4) the roles of Open Firmware, U-Boot, OpenSBI, the Alpha console and the VAX console beside the BIOS (4.3), with QEMU's `pc-bios` image names, OpenBSD's `ofwboot` and `sbi_*` calls as sources; (5) the Alpha hardware restart parameter block, read in OpenBSD `alpha/include/rpb.h`; (6) the `quick_memory_check()` line of the ROM sketch (source `boot.md`, job 1), the reason the ROM polls (marked inference), the IPL record and ARM loader remarks (unverified); (10) Linux and NetBSD loaders: NetBSD's `efiboot` calls `ExitBootServices` and Linux's kernel is itself the PE/COFF image (`efi-stub.rst`), both read; (11) VAX microcode and the NVMe firmware-download command, unverified; (12) SMM, trusted firmware and U-Boot and OpenSBI consoles, unverified; (13) the "no trampoline, no firmware call" and "block shrinks to memory, cores and the bus controller" benefits. *Corrected:* the chapter named `firmload(9)` as OpenBSD's; it is NetBSD's, and OpenBSD's is `loadfirmware(9)` (chapter 5, 5.12, still says `firmload(9)` for the PC case: for its owner). 4.8 now states plainly that `INIT` and `READY` are the PDP-11 start of a device, 4.8.1 keeps them and the virtio `device_status` handshake is their descendant, citing 5.1, 5.5 and 2.7.3. *Removed as implementation state (moved to `docs/implementation-status.md`):* "no pdp64 boot ROM, first stage or second stage exists (the QEMU machine loads the kernel directly, 6.12.2), so none of it is tested"; "nothing has been assembled" (twice); "no such code exists"; "A proposal and a decision, no code."; the figure caption "Nothing is built". The "What the statement predates" remark of 4.8 became "How the statement reads under virtio". *Tags:* none changed. `docs/boot.md` updated for the M9312 and `ODT`.

120. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the manual describes the architecture as it is to be; proposed, deferred and open matters leave the chapters for three appendices; the name Q64 and the three native device names are no longer used in the manual.** *The author's words:* "the manual is about the arch as it is to be, not as it is right now." "instead of retagging, blt64 vic-64x and sid-64x they shouldn't be mentioned at all until I decide on them. 'deferred' stuff shouldn't be in the manual, nor should 'proposed' stuff, except maybe in an appendix, one for proposed, one for deferred. the main body of the manual shouldn't have a whiff of them." "an open item can be mentioned in a few words, but an 'open items' appendix is where each open item gets a fair discussion of pros and cons of each option and what the actual issue is." On the name: "you chose q64, not me. i went along with it ... until we have some substantive improvements or sugar over pcie, maybe best to just remove mention of it", and later the same day: "well if we DO add things to pcie, then that is q64 bus." *Decided:* (1) the body of the manual carries no proposed and no deferred material; (2) three new appendices hold it: appendix H Proposed (designs nobody has decided, statements tagged *(P)*), appendix I Deferred (the author said "later"), appendix J Open items (questions the architecture must answer and has not, statements tagged *(S)*); (3) in the body an open item is one short sentence with a pointer to its entry in appendix J, and the entry gives the issue, why it matters, every option with its pros and cons, the precedents, the author's referents and, labelled as the assistant's, a leaning; (4) the tag *(D)* stays in the body, *(P)* and *(S)* are used only in the appendices; (5) the name Q64 means PCI Express plus the pdp64's own additions: the manual does not use it until such additions are decided, and if and when he decides additions the name returns for that bus; (6) BLT64, VIC-64X and SID-64X are not mentioned in the manual until he decides on them. *Mechanism (no decision):* the appendices are assembled by `tools/mkmanual.py` from fragment files `docs/manual-src/appendix-parts/<NN>-proposed.md`, `-deferred.md` and `-open.md` (`<NN>` the chapter number with two digits, or an appendix letter for the ABI); a moved statement keeps its number as a label (`**2.3.3** (moved from chapter 2)`) and `tools/manual/assemble.py` treats a number defined only in an appendix as a valid target; `book/outline.txt` and `book/sources.txt` list the three appendices; the convention and the template of an open-items entry are in `docs/manual-src/README.md`. *Done:* the name Q64 and the three device names are removed from the manual sources (the glossary rows, the index, the generator `tools/declog/evolution.py` of appendix G, which now also filters them) and from `README.md` (the bus is PCI Express). *Links changed:* `docs/pdp64-q64-bus.md` is an internal design document about the additions and keeps its name; the links to it were removed from `README.md` (two: the sentence on the bus, now pointing to chapter 2 of the manual, and the document list), from `manual-src/02-bus.md` (line 7), `manual-src/05-drivers.md` (the list of working documents) and `manual-src/appendix-f-evidence.md` (the row). *Candidate additions* (decisions in the manual that are already additions to PCI Express, not named Q64 until the author decides): 2.4.6 (queued work of a departed device stays in memory), 2.4.7 (a bus reset produces no removal events), 2.3.5 (convenience and security circuitry on the processor side: overlays, helper registers, the IOMMU, error and reset rules), the interrupt-write ordering rule of errata 71 (2.4.4, 5.7.4: an interrupt write cannot overtake the data writes; a moved interrupt target is masked and read back), and the overlays and convenience registers of errata 75. *Not changed:* the records keep the words Q64 and the three names (errata, ledger, internal design documents).

121. **CORRECTIONS AND A RULE (2026-11-10): the chapters renumbered 1 to n after the moves of errata 120; the rule of the author about approval.** *The rule (the author's words, 2026-11-10):* "when I say 'looks good' and 'I like it' that is a decision in favor of." *Corrections (no decision):* after the statements and sections that moved to appendices H, I and J were taken out, the sections of chapters 2 to 8 and 10 and of appendix A and the statements within them were numbered 1 to n again, and every citation in the manual sources, the fragments, the generators, the glossary and the repository documents that cite the manual (decisions-pending.md, smp-decisions.md, c64-devices-from-chat.md) was rewritten from the map in one pass; the open items of appendix J keep their numbers (the open items J.7.4 and J.7.5 are J.7.3 and J.7.4 in the manual of pdp64/r20, where the atomic operand entry J.7.3 does not apply). Earlier errata keep the numbers of the text of their day. A statement that moved to a fragment keeps its old number there as a label; the open items' lines 'Where the body refers to it' were rewritten from the pointers of the chapters. *Statements restored by the rule:* 2.3.6 (the windows of a device start on page boundaries; the former 2.3.3 of the proposed material) and 2.4.9 (a departed device's windows give a precise bus fault; the former 2.4.5) are decided, with details to be determined, on the strength of R262 ("your bus design looks excellent, let's wait for pain points to land before settling it, but it looks good"); both stand at the end of their sections so that no other number moved; MSI-X (2.6.1, 5.9.1) is marked decided with details pending (J.6.3) in both places. The other hits of a search of the author's messages for approval words (R173 the rounding variants, present in the instruction set, and the CRC variants, withdrawn by errata 20; R390 the PUSHX and POPX savings, decided; R360 and R361 the 39, 48 and 57-bit addresses, decided in 6.6.1; R311 the Blit model, in 3.8; R391 the NN(arg) syntax of the assembler) are already in the body; R392 ("excellent list of starting points on bus design") names no statement. *Old to new numbers (changed ones only; every other number is unchanged):* Chapter 2: 2.3.4 to 2.3.3, 2.3.5 to 2.3.4, 2.3.6 to 2.3.5, 2.4.6 to 2.4.5, 2.4.7 to 2.4.6, 2.4.9 to 2.4.7, 2.4.10 to 2.4.8, 2.6.4 to 2.6.3; Chapter 3: 3.8.8 to 3.8.2, 3.12 to 3.10, 3.12.1 to 3.10.1, 3.12.3 to 3.10.2, 3.12.5 to 3.10.3, 3.13 to 3.11, 3.13.1 to 3.11.1, 3.13.2 to 3.11.2, 3.13.3 to 3.11.3, 3.14 to 3.12, 3.14.1 to 3.12.1, 3.15 to 3.13, 3.15.1 to 3.13.1, 3.15.2 to 3.13.2, 3.16 to 3.14, 3.16.1 to 3.14.1, 3.16.3 to 3.14.2, 3.17 to 3.15, 3.17.1 to 3.15.1, 3.18 to 3.16, 3.18.1 to 3.16.1, 3.18.2 to 3.16.2, 3.18.4 to 3.16.3, 3.18.5 to 3.16.4, 3.19 to 3.17, 3.19.1 to 3.17.1, 3.20 to 3.18, 3.20.1 to 3.18.1, 3.22 to 3.19; Chapter 4: 4.4.2 to 4.4.1, 4.6.4 to 4.6.3; Chapter 5: 5.10.3 to 5.10.2, 5.10.4 to 5.10.3, 5.11.3 to 5.11.2, 5.13.3 to 5.13.1, 5.15 to 5.14; Chapter 6: 6.12.3 to 6.12.2; Chapter 7: 7.8.2 to 7.8.1, 7.8.3 to 7.8.2, 7.8.4 to 7.8.3, 7.8.5 to 7.8.4, 7.8.6 to 7.8.5, 7.8.7 to 7.8.6; Chapter 8: 8.1.4 to 8.1.5, 8.1.5 to 8.1.6, 8.1.6 to 8.1.4, 8.3.5 to 8.3.2, 8.3.6 to 8.3.1, 8.4.4 to 8.4.3, 8.4.5 to 8.4.4; Chapter 9: 9.20.5 to 9.20.4; Chapter 10: 10.1.3 to 10.1.2, 10.1.4 to 10.1.3, 10.1.5 to 10.1.4, 10.1.6 to 10.1.5, 10.1.7 to 10.1.6, 10.4.4 to 10.4.3, 10.4.5 to 10.4.4; Appendix A: A.11 to A.9, A.12 to A.10, A.12.1 to A.10.1, A.12.2 to A.10.2, A.12.3 to A.10.3.

122. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): r20 and pdp64/16 are previous iterations, no longer CPUs with manuals; pdp64/32 is the pdp64; there is one manual.** *The author's words:* "r20 and 64/16 are gone as cpus with manuals etc; they are previous iterations that were superseded by 64/32 which is now the official pdp64". *Supersedes* the part of errata 94 that kept the grammars, the manuals, the books and the tool options of pdp64/r20 and pdp64/16 as a documented comparison with manuals of their own; the rest of errata 94 stands (pdp64/32 is the pdp64, chosen for performance; the testing against the other two is documented). *Decided:* (1) pdp64/r20 and pdp64/16 are previous iterations of the design, superseded by pdp64/32; they are no longer CPUs and have no manuals; (2) pdp64/32 is the pdp64: the 32-register, prefix-coded architecture is called simply the pdp64, and there is one manual, one book and no family; (3) the history of how pdp64/32 was chosen stays as history: `docs/encoding-work/` (above all `integration.md` section 4 and `timing.md`), the measurements, appendix G of the manual and chapter 1, section 1.8; (4) the last commit that carries the manuals and the tools of the two iterations is the git tag `pre-single-cpu`. *Done with this entry (documents, manuals and books only):* the manual is `docs/manual/` (it was `docs/manual/pdp64-32/`; `docs/manual/pdp64-r20/` and `docs/manual/pdp64-16/` are removed), the book is `book/pdp64.pdf`, the member machinery of the manual source and of `tools/mkmanual.py`, `tools/mkchapters.py`, `tools/mkbook.sh` and `tools/check-manual-src.py` is removed, and the manual generators read only the grammar of pdp64/32. *Not decided here:* the removal of the r20 and pdp64/16 grammars, assembler and tool options, simulator cases, compiler and QEMU or LLVM code and tests is to follow, and what exactly is removed is not decided by this entry.

123. **DONE (2026-11-10): the code, data and tests of r20 and pdp64/16 are removed; there is one CPU, the pdp64.** *The author's words:* "r20 and 64/16 are gone as cpus with manuals etc; they are previous iterations that were superseded by 64/32 which is now the official pdp64" (errata 122), and then: "remove the code too, tag first". *Tags:* `pre-single-cpu` (the last commit with the manuals and tools of the two iterations) and `pre-code-removal` (the last commit with their code), both pushed. *The single target is called `pdp64`:* the grammar is `spec/pdp64/pdp64.hbnf` (it was `spec/pdp64-32/pdp64-32.hbnf`), the tables `build/pdp64-tab.c` and `spec/pdp64/tables.md`, the encoding name in the tools "pdp64". The `-m` option is gone from `as64`, `ld64`, `sim64`, `tools/timing.py`, the build and test scripts; `as64`, `sim64` and the pcc compiler `ccom` accept `-m pdp64` (and no other value) for the driver convention of pcc and say so in their usage text. The low four bits of `e_flags` hold `EF_PDP64_ISA` (2, the value of the old pdp64/32); `ld64` and `sim64` refuse an object that names another instruction set. *Removed:* the grammars `spec/pdp64-16/`, `spec/pdp64.hbnf.in` (the formats of the original encoding) and `spec/pdp64-layout.hbnf`; the generated parser and format codec (`as64/fmtp.c`, `as64/fmt.h` as a codec header), the field calibration (`common/calib.*`), page 2 (`common/p2.*`), the r20 decode path, branch and compare-and-branch cases and profile classes of the executor, the r20 instruction builder of the assembler (about 500 lines of `as64/as64.c`), `as64 --limits`, `--p2-expand`, `--no-p2`, the r20 relocation patching of `ld64`, the r20 timing classes and the `ldec0` and `ldecp` parameters of the timing model; the encoding 1 and 2 tables of the codec (one table now); in QEMU the generated parser, calibration and page 2 copies (the QEMU target now decodes with the table codec, and `tests/qemu/mmu-*.S`, which held hand-written r20 words, use `ret` and a first parcel of the new encoding); the checks `check-drift.py`, `check-formats.sh`, `check-p2.sh`, `check-llvm-gen.sh`, `tools/gen-llvm-td.py` and the LLVM oracle tests `llvm/check.sh`, `oracle.py`, `forms.py`, `negative.py`, `runsim.py`; the tests `tests/asm` and `tests/asm-err` of the original encoding, `tests/asm-pdp64-16`, `tests/asm-err-pdp64-16`, `tests/ld` (original), `tests/ld-pdp64-16`, `tests/enc/vectors-16.txt`, `tests/formats.c`, `tests/p2diff.*`, and the simulator tests that wrote r20 instruction words (`cbw`, `hint`, `isync*`, `sys-pushx`, `sysreg-pad`, `sysreg-unassigned`, `sysreg-write-*`, `page2`, `p2reserved`; `sys-exc` was ported) and `diff.same`, the comparison with the r20 oracle. *Renamed* (git mv): `spec/pdp64-32/` to `spec/pdp64/`, `pdp64-32.hbnf` to `pdp64.hbnf`, `tests/asm-pdp64-32` to `tests/asm`, `tests/asm-err-pdp64-32` and the r20 refusals that still hold to `tests/asm-err`, `tests/ld-pdp64-32` to `tests/ld`, `tests/enc/vectors-32.txt` to `vectors.txt`, `tests/sim-enc` into `tests/sim`; the chart identifiers of the manual lost their `32-` prefix. *Counts* (`tools/check-all.sh`, before and after; only r20 and pdp64/16 tests are gone): format vectors 63 (original) and 80 (pdp64/16) gone, 95 (pdp64) kept; encoder and codec checks 2 grammars to 1 (drift 680 to 340 checks); simulator runs 231 to 91 (every program ran in five forms and now in two); timing checks 128 to 44; relaxation runs 80 to 40; QEMU machine tests 53 to 54 run (the `bitfield` test is no longer skipped; `fused` is skipped on QEMU); the C programs 963, 602 and 963 runs (pdp64/32, r20, pdp64/16) to 963 runs (107 programs under nine flag sets). *Kept as history:* `docs/encoding-work/`, the data it cites, the older errata, `docs/CHANGES-SINCE-R20.md`, and the optimiser of `docs/codegen-data/encoding/` (its scripts that read two grammars by tag run from the tag `pre-code-removal`). *pcc:* the branch `single-pdp64` of the clone `status-check/pcc` (not pushed; the author decides) builds only the 32-register target and the name `pdp64`, with one `ccom` (it was `ccom` and `ccom32`); `tools/build-pcc.sh` of this repository needs that branch and refuses a tree that still has the `PDP64_REGS32` switch. *Not removed, and why:* the numbering of `spec/pdp64-mnemonics.hbnf` (opcode groups and sub-opcodes of the original encoding) is the vocabulary that the executor, the assembler's parser, the manual's dictionary and pcc's name table are written on, so it stays; the `llvm/` back end (machine-code layer, 5,000 lines) encodes the original encoding throughout and is frozen with a note instead of being deleted. *Not decided here:* what becomes of `llvm/` (a port to the encoding of the pdp64, or deletion).

124. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): chapter 8 rewritten for the PDP-11 reader and renumbered.** *Old to new.* Sections: 8.1 (the decision) became 8.3 and 8.4; 8.2 (why not SIMD) became 8.3; 8.3 (inference) and 8.4 (array languages) became 8.5; 8.5 (number formats) became 8.6; new 8.1 (the PDP-11 and the FP11), 8.2 (what came between), 8.7 (what was checked). Statements: 8.1.1 to 8.3.1; 8.1.2 to 8.4.1; 8.1.3 to 8.4.3; 8.1.4 to 8.4.2; 8.1.5 to 8.3.4; 8.1.6 to 8.4.4; 8.2.1 to 8.3.2; 8.2.2 to 8.3.3; 8.3.1 to 8.5.1; 8.3.2 to 8.5.2; 8.4.2 and 8.4.3 to 8.5.3 and 8.5.4; 8.4.4 to 8.5.5; 8.5.1 to 8.6.1; 8.5.2 to 8.6.2; 8.4.5 is new (open item J.8.4). Citations changed: glossary (XNORPOP, POPCNT 8.3 to 8.5; APL, K 8.4 to 8.5) and the note of XNORPOP in 9.9 (section 8.3 to 8.5). *Errors fixed.* The old 8.2.1 said the first-page opcode map was "58 of 64 full": that was the figure of an earlier encoding, and the prefix code replaced the reserved-opcode map (9.21.2), so the clause is removed. The old 8.3.1 said inference "runs on memory-mapped devices, not in the processor"; the author's words (R139) are "whether in cpu or gpu whatever makes most sense", and 8.5.1 now says that. The old proposed text compared the 32-byte descriptor of chapter 5 with the accelerator's: the virtio descriptor is 16 bytes (5.4.2). The old text of J.8.2 and H.8 said that DMA chains go back to the RK11; the RK11 had no chain (the DEUNA ring of 4.3BSD is the first ring in the sources read). *Noted, not edited.* `docs/decisions-pending.md` says that V7 saved floating-point registers only for processes that used floating point, by a flag set on first use; the V7 sources show `swtch()` saving them for any process unless `u_fpsaved` says it was done since the last trap. *New:* figures vec-state, vec-shapes and vec-route (`tools/manual/vecfigs.py`), J.8.4 (the first accelerator).

125. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-10): the order of work after the single-CPU decision; the pcc RISC-V back end is a separate project.** *The author's words:* "no, don't write the pcc riscv backend. that will be a separate project, not any part of this one"; "once we've decided on any issue that might affect our opcodes etc, then we'll port llvm to the new encoding and the pcc and as64 as well. which means updating the hbnf grammar, because it generates the assembler, the tables for chapter 9 of the manual etc."; "we'll also keep sim64 and pcc current too; but it came to my attention there may be some opcode changes once we're done resolving all the open items"; "i want to get this thing in shape so you can then implement the llvm and qemu backends"; "that python script that went over all the mnemonics and figured out the optimal prefix encoding for pdp64 to fit the most used opcodes into the smallest number of 16 bit packets? that's the one I want translated to C ... use optimal algorithms, we'll be re-running it once the decisions are made and the mnemonics are settled"; "rewrite chapters 1, 7, 8 and 10 for the PDP-11 reader; then prepare a winless version of the status and decisions document such that I can choose options or type in questions and other decisions, so we can quickly do a feedback loop"; "keep all docs up to date with the current decisions, I want no confusion". *Order of work:* (1) resolve the open items, starting with those that affect the opcodes; (2) re-run the encoding optimiser (translated from its Python original to C, with optimal algorithms) once the decisions are made and the mnemonics settled; (3) update the hbnf grammar; (4) regenerate the assembler tables, the manual and the pcc headers; (5) port the LLVM back end, QEMU, as64, pcc and sim64 to the new encoding; sim64 and pcc are kept current meanwhile. *Separate project:* the pcc RISC-V back end is not part of this project.

126. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): chapters 1 and 10 rewritten for the two readers of errata 103.** *The author's words:* "rewrite chapters 1, 7, 8 and 10 for the PDP-11 reader"; "remove all ai slop, verify everything, and structure it nicely with good flow"; "make sure each chapter flows from pdp11 to pdp64 with relevant precedents in between"; "where the structure and flow is already good, keep it of course". *Chapter 1, old to new:* sections 1.1 (why the machine exists) to 1.3 (the road from the PDP-11; statements 1.1.1 to 1.1.4 are 1.3.1 and 1.3.4); 1.2 (PDP-10, PDP-11, VAX-11) to 1.4, statements 1.2.1 to 1.2.7 to 1.4.1 to 1.4.7; 1.3 (more minimal) to 1.5, statements 1.3.1 to 1.3.8 to 1.5.1 to 1.5.8; 1.4 (the principles) to 1.6, statements 1.4.1 to 1.4.5 to 1.6.1 to 1.6.5; 1.5 (what has been built and measured) removed, its statements 1.5.1 to 1.5.3 to `docs/implementation-status.md` and 1.5.4 (the finish line) to 10.5.1; 1.6 (does the instruction set matter) to 1.7, statements 1.6.1 to 1.6.4 to 1.7.1 to 1.7.4; 1.7 (how the manual is organised) to 1.2; 1.8 (how the encoding was chosen) unchanged, 1.8.1 to 1.8.3. New: 1.1 (what the pdp64 is; 1.1.1 to 1.1.5), 1.2 (who the manual is for and how to read it; 1.2.1 to 1.2.6), the table of the road (1.3, with the author's own account of the origin, 1.3.2 and 1.3.3). *Chapter 10, old to new:* 10.1 (FPGA plan) stays 10.1, its statements 10.1.1 (first implementation, harness) to 10.1.1 and to appendix H, 10.1.2 (resources) to 10.1.4, 10.1.3 (block RAM) to 10.1.5, 10.1.4 (clock) to 10.1.2 and the per-board estimates to appendix H, 10.1.5 (phases and cores) to 10.1.3 and the phases to appendix H, 10.1.6 to appendix H; 10.2 stays 10.2; 10.3 stays 10.3 for 10.3.1 and 10.3.2, its 10.3.3 and 10.3.3a become 10.4.2 and 10.4.1 of the new section 10.4 (security); 10.4 (finish line) becomes 10.5, 10.4.1 to 10.4.4 to 10.5.1 to 10.5.4; 10.5 (what remains) becomes 10.6. *Citations changed:* J.10.1 (Section 10.3.3 now 10.4.1 and 10.4.3), the glossary rows `fair corpus`, `pcc` and `sim64, QEMU`, `docs/priority-lists.md` (manual 1.4.x now 1.6.x), `docs/c64-devices-from-chat.md` (manual 1.2.5 and 1.2.6 now 1.4.5 and 1.4.6), `docs/decisions-pending.md` (manual 1.4 now 1.6), appendix G (the Velinsky link, now appendix H 1.1.5), the index page. *Facts corrected in the old text:* (1) "the bit unit and the UTF-8 decoder together cost less than 5% of the die" (10.2.2, and "the bit unit and block-move sequencer less than 5%" in 1.3): by the same table 3,500 + 1,200 is 4,700 of 92,200, 5.1%, and 3,500 + 1,500 is 5.4%; the three together are 6,200, under 7%; (2) 10.2: the 78032 "had neither" floating point nor memory management: only the absence of floating point is in the project's table, so the claim about memory management is dropped; (3) the table of 1.6 gave "pdp64: one register write" to start a second core, which 4.7.1 now leaves open (J.4.6): the pdp64 cell is removed; the system-call-path row (paper estimates for six architectures) has no source in the repository except the NetBSD m68k count and is removed; the code-size row compared the new compiler with the old one, and now compares the pdp64 with clang `-Os` on the same 841 files from `docs/encoding-work/arch-comparison.md` (1.21, 1.34, 1.22 and 0.91 times, with the constant-pool caveat); (4) 1.2: "RISC-V chose fixed-length register-to-register encodings": the base is four bytes with an optional two-byte compressed form (2.93 bytes an instruction at `-Os` in the corpus); (5) 1.4: "three mode bits are accepted" is four with `DIT`; (6) 1.5.2 gave 6.5% below clang and 1.19 and 1.10 times as many instructions for the original encoding, with no source in the repository (the 0.2% against `gcc -Os` is in `docs/roadmap.md`): removed with the state of the tools; (7) 1.8.3 "22 to 28%" and "7 to 8%" are 22.5 to 28.1% (fetch windows of 2 to 16 bytes) and 6.9 to 8.5% (4 to 16 bytes), from `docs/encoding-work/integration.md` 4.3; (8) 1.7.2: "a statement with no tag describes the architecture as it is built and tested" contradicted errata 120, and now reads as the architecture as it is to be; (9) 10.1.3: the 5.5 megabits of block RAM are the first board's, so the figure is in appendix H; (10) 10.4.3 said the Unix/32V fallback "would not reach the goal": the author's own statement is R230, and the missing network stack is now checked against the 32V tree on this machine. *Moved to appendix H (chapter 10):* the hard-core harness and the phase plan, which the old text tagged *(D)* and for which no decision of the author was found (`decisions-pending.md`, E6, "No erratum backs either"), the per-board clock estimates, the schedule of 10 to 19 months, the vendor-neutral directory and the multiplier. *Figures:* `tools/manual/introfigs.py` (`intro-machine`, `intro-words`, `intro-road`, `conc-budget`, `conc-finish`). *Not found:* the author's two quotations of 2026-11-10 about the origin ("we started off as a pdp16 ...", "the initial idea was ...") are in appendix G and are not rows of the decision register or lines of the message files; R393 carries the third.

127. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): chapter 7 rewritten for the two readers of errata 103.** *The author's words:* "rewrite chapters 1, 7, 8 and 10 for the PDP-11 reader"; "remove all ai slop, verify everything, and structure it nicely with good flow"; "make sure each chapter flows from pdp11 to pdp64 with relevant precedents in between". *Old to new, sections:* 7.1 (the seventeen operations) to 7.3; 7.2 (the memory model) to 7.5; 7.3 (the atomics in detail) to 7.4; 7.4 (per-CPU state) to 7.8; 7.5 (translation and instruction-cache coherence) to 7.9; 7.6 (waiting and signalling) to 7.10; 7.7 (cache coherence) to 7.6; 7.8 (core identification, classes, the secure core) to 7.11, its statements 7.8.1 and 7.8.2 (the inter-processor interrupt) to 7.10; 7.9 (a kernel's view) to 7.12; 7.10 (what was avoided) to 7.13; 7.11 (the road to out of order) to 7.7; 7.12 (the atomic and ordering instructions) merged into 7.3, 7.4, 7.5, 7.9 and 7.10. New: 7.1 (what you already know), 7.2 (what came between), 7.14 (what was checked). *Statements:* 7.1.1 to 7.1.4 are 7.3.1 to 7.3.4; 7.2.1 to 7.2.6 are 7.5.1 to 7.5.6; 7.3.1 to 7.3.5 are 7.4.1 to 7.4.5; 7.4.1 to 7.4.4 are 7.8.1 to 7.8.4; 7.5.1 to 7.5.3 are 7.9.1 to 7.9.3; 7.6.1 to 7.6.3 are 7.10.1 to 7.10.3; 7.7.1 to 7.7.4 are 7.6.1 to 7.6.4; 7.8.1 is 7.10.4, 7.8.2 is 7.10.5, 7.8.3 is 7.11.1, 7.8.4 is 7.11.2, 7.8.5 is 7.11.3, 7.8.6 is 7.11.6; 7.9.1 to 7.9.4 are 7.12.1 to 7.12.4; 7.10.1 is 7.13.1; 7.11.1 to 7.11.3 are 7.7.1 to 7.7.3; 7.12.1 is merged into 7.3.1 and 7.3.2; 7.12.2 into 7.4.1 and 7.4.2; 7.12.3 into 7.4.3; 7.12.4 is split into 7.5.7 (`FENCE`), 7.9.3 (`ISYNC`) and 7.10.1 (`WFE`, `SEV`, `HINT`); 7.12.5 is 7.4.6. New statements: 7.11.4 and 7.11.5 (the secure core's private window, locked translation buffer, clear at a domain switch and reserved bus slot, from the design of `docs/crypto-secure-core.md` section 1 that the author approved in errata 86: "i like your design. approved."). The statements that stayed in appendix H keep the labels they had (7.8.1 to 7.8.3, 7.9.1, 7.9.4; the fragment says so); open item J.7.6 (the reservation granule) is new, J.7.1 to J.7.5 keep their anchors and their pointers are corrected. The entries table that `tools/mkmanual.py` generates moved from section 7.12 to section 7.4. Citations corrected in chapters 3, 6, 9, appendices B and C, the fragments of chapters 4 and 6 and the proposed list of appendix B, `docs/pdp64-isa.md`, `docs/pdp64-q64-bus.md`, `docs/architecture-recommendations.md`, `docs/crypto-secure-core.md`, `docs/security-round2.md` and `docs/decisions-pending.md`; `docs/smp-decisions.md` and `docs/kernel-pain-points.md` are dated records and keep the numbers they were written with, except two lines each. *Errors found in the old text and fixed:* (1) 7.2.2 said a store buffer that is snooped by loads and drained in program order keeps sequential consistency; such a buffer lets a load pass an older store to another address, which is the store-buffering outcome of total store order, so a load must wait for older stores to other addresses (or be replayed, 7.7.3), and statement 7.5.2 now says so (the ISA text, `docs/pdp64-isa.md` section 17.1, has the same sentence; not changed here). (2) 7.2.3 said no misaligned access can be torn; the explicit unaligned moves of 9.6.5 are two accesses, each atomic and the pair not, and 7.5.3 now says so. (3) 7.2.4 said the machine is in order; it is the first implementations (7.7.2), and 7.5.4 says so. (4) The commentary of 7.3 said an Anderson lock was measured "155% worse" than the ticket lock; the source says the Anderson lock gave 155% more operations a second (2.3 to 5.9 million), and 7.2 now says that. (5) 7.4.2 said the field is "one six-byte instruction" for every per-CPU access; it is a load only, a store through `d(DP)` is not in the grammar (J.9.5), and 7.8 says so; `docs/pdp64-isa.md` section 17.4 still says four bytes and a 12-bit displacement (stale, not changed here). (6) 7.12.2 and 7.3.1 repeated the same reservation rule; merged. (7) The `LL` granule size (naturally aligned, at least 8 bytes) and the constrained-sequence rule (no other memory access or exception) were in errata 45 and missing from the chapter; added to 7.4.1 and 7.4.2. *Claims now marked unverified or dropped:* the 11/74 as a multiprocessor and the names of its instructions, `DATIP` and `DATO`, the 11/782, 8300 and 8800, the VAXcluster, Lamport's definition, the litmus table for weak models, the big and little cores, the history of snooping and directory machines; "about 600 per core" for the coherence hardware (no source) is dropped. *Read and added:* the V7 `plock()`, `sleep()`, `brelse()`, `trap()` and `idle()`; 4.3BSD's `locore.s` idle loop and `masterpaddr` comment and the absence of `BBSSI`; NetBSD's VAX multiprocessor code (`multicpu.c`: message bits with `bbssi`, `IPI_TBIA`; `ka820.c`, `ka88.c`, `ka6400.c`); Linux 2.6.33's shootdown, ticket lock, `kernel_lock.c` and MP table; Linux's atomics, `__cmpwait`, TLB flush and per-CPU registers; OpenBSD riscv64's `tp` for `curcpu()`. *Figures:* `smp-lock-race`, `smp-store-buffer`, `smp-coherence`, `smp-percpu` and `smp-shootdown` (`tools/manual/smpfigs.py`, hooked in `tools/mkmanual.py`).

128. **DONE (2026-11-10): dead rules of the earlier encodings cut; no other decision.** *The author's words:* "of course in general cut dead rules". *Removed from `spec/pdp64-mnemonics.hbnf` (3 rules, 57 words, 67 lines with their comments):* `lencode` (the length code of parcel 0, 4 words), `p2op` (the opcodes of page 2, 45 words) and `amode` (the addressing-mode nibble, 8 words); each was proved unused by a search of the generators, the tools, the tests, the executor, the assembler, the linker, the simulator and the QEMU target, and by a rebuild: `build/pdp64-tab.c` is byte-identical (sha256 `0caece67...`), the assembler, linker, simulator and encoding checks pass unchanged. The constants `LENCODE_*` and `AMODE_*` are no longer generated (only the frozen `llvm/` mentioned them). *Removed from the manual generator:* the definitions `PAGES`, `PAGE_INTRO`, `INDEX_INTRO`, `P2_INTRO`, `P2` (45 page-2 descriptions), `NOTATION` and `P2_STORY` of `tools/manual/isa_entries.py` (none was read by any code), the test `kind != 'p2'` in `tools/mkmanual.py` (every chart has kind `enc`), eight sentences about page 2 in the entry sources (`isa_part1.py`, `isa_part3.py`, `isa_stories.py`; the filter `memberprose.py` already left them out of every page), and the section "The addressing modes" of `docs/manual/dictionary/constants.md` (the table of the nibble, which the grammar of the encoding does not have; section 9.5 is unchanged). The generated manual differs in nothing else. *Kept, and why:* `syspg0` to `syspg5` are placeholders of the page-bit mechanisms (errata 84, chapter 9 held entries, `tests/sim/unimpl-syspg0.s`), not of page 2, and are leaves of `spec/pdp64/pdp64.hbnf`; `opgroup`, `movstore`, `sys-minor`, `subop` and the numbers of every mnemonic group are read by the executor and the timing model; `offset` documents the alias offset 0x100 that `mkenc.py` and `mkmanual.py` apply; `llvm/` is frozen (errata 123) and still holds its copy of the old constants. The mnemonics were not renumbered and the encoding grammar was not touched.

129. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-10): `sim64` is to use the output of the grammar to decode; no local names or paths in the documents and registers.** *The author's words:* "i want sim64 to also use hbnf output for opcode generation, but implementation of course can be by hand, since hbnf isn't expected to know what the opcodes actually DO, just that it should be able to parse the binary opcodes into the proper structs etc so the sim can simulate them efficiently"; and, of the documents and the registers, that all references to the local user names and other local unix users and all local pathnames must go. *State today:* `sim64` decodes through the generic table codec (`common/pdp64enc.c`, the tables generated from the grammar by `tools/mkenc.py`) into a decoded instruction (`struct einsn`), and the semantics of the executor are written by hand (`common/pdp64exec.c`, `common/pdp64exec_enc.inc`, a handler for each mnemonic). *Decided:* the grammar is to produce the decoder of `sim64` as well as the tables of the assembler: a decoder specialised to the encoding, which splits the binary instruction into the decoded structures the executor reads, so that the simulator runs efficiently; the grammar says where the fields are and not what the instruction does, so the executor stays hand-written. *Order:* this follows the decisions that affect the opcodes and the next run of the encoding optimiser (errata 125), so that the decoder is generated once, for the final encoding. *Done with the second statement:* the documents, the manual source, `README.md`, `handoff.md` and the registers of the ledger carry no local user name, host or session name or absolute path; the published ledger and register are produced by a step that rewrites paths and scrubs names and use register numbers only.

130. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): every pdp64 system has at least two cores; with corrections to chapter 7.** *The author's words:* "decision: minimum 2 cpus, so smp always gets some exercise."; on the seventeen operations: "i think the number 17 was in a particular context, if you find the thread where it came up, you should be able to find nearby not only the 10 referenced instructions, but figure out what the other 7 were i was talking about."; on the ordering of the block operations: "probably its probably something we didn't make any decisions on yet, verify that." *Decided:* there is no single-core configuration; the reason he gave is that the multiprocessor paths are always exercised; no maximum is set. Manual 7.1.1 *(D)*; 7.6.3 now reads at least two cores in every system, four enough for the first device, hoped to fit the FPGA (errata 92 stands for its four; the design still supports at least four and sixteen or more as the ceiling). Citations updated: 1.1.4, 4.7 and 4.7.1 (at least one other core exists, so the start mechanism is always used; J.4.6 stays open in mechanism), 10.1.3 and 10.1.8 of its fragment (a board must fit at least two cores, about 32,000 lookup tables by the estimate of 10.1.4), 7.6.1, 7.10.3, 7.11 commentary, J.7.2 and J.7.5. *Corrections (no decision):* (1) the seventeen operations of 7.3.1 are the count of folded operations that the author asked for on 2026-10-21 (the thread of that day; its question is R163 of the register, "how many folded opcodes in system and smp? i thought atomics and smp would be in the same umbrella"; the answer: 93 folded operations of the 193 words of the grammar, of which the SMP umbrella is 17, ten atomics and seven others, and 12 are system operations that are not SMP); the count was re-made against the grammar and the dictionary and holds: the same ten atomics (22 words: twenty with a plain and a `_DB` form, and `LL` and `SC`) and the same seven others (nine words: `FENCE`, `WFE`, `SEV`, `TLBI` and `TLBIA`, `TLBSYNC`, `ICINV` and `ICINVA`, `MFCPUID`), all still in the grammar; `ISYNC` and `HINT`, added a day later (errata 39), are system operations and are not counted; chapter 7 now carries the table and the folding rule. (2) 7.3.2 said every atomic has a `_DB` form; `LL` and `SC` have none. (3) The block operations: errata 39 item 7 decided that the accesses inside `MCPY`, `MMOV` and `MSET` (and the other block words) are unordered with respect to one another, only the final memory and the register progress being defined, to be written into the instruction-set text; it was written nowhere, and the old 7.5 said the opposite by inference ("each in the single order"). It is now 7.5.8 *(D)*; the rest (tearing, intermediate states, publishing, faults, overlap, reservation, device memory) is open as J.7.7 (new, with 7.5.9). Pointers: 9.11 and its Cf. line, and `pdp64-isa.md` section 4.7. (4) The progress category of faults is 6.4, not 6.8. (5) `pdp64-isa.md` section 17.1 said that sequential consistency survives a store buffer provided loads snoop it and stores drain in program order; without the rule that a load does not pass an older store to another address in the buffer, two cores that each store and then load the other location can both read the old values (store buffering, total store order); the sentence is corrected to match 7.5.2 (the decision, sequential consistency, is unchanged). *Not decided:* what a two-core minimum does to the dedicated cores of 7.11.6 (J.7.5), and whether `HINT #1` belongs under the umbrella. Withdrawn by errata 132: the author cancelled the minimum of two cores (his words: "if we already decided minimum 4, then we'll stick to that, cancel minimum 2"); errata 92 stands.

131. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the vector and accelerator engine is a standard part of the architecture, like the FPU, the MMU and the IOMMU.** *The author's words:* "fill me in on that, I want all the best options with rationale and precedents for each of those items. even if it doesn't involve specific opcodes 'in the cpu' still it must be built in as a standard part of the architecture, just as the fpu mmu and iommu are." (of the open items J.8.1 the operations and number formats of the vector device, J.8.2 its descriptor and the virtio descriptor, J.8.3 packed integer lanes in the general registers, J.8.4 which accelerator is built first); and, in the same message: "how does simd work, how many instructions are there, is simd just normal ops fused with a 'simd' bit or something like that? would it make sense to have simd ops that work on regions of memory instead of register files, since we'd be load/storing register files anyways? I guess then we'd be invisibly load/storing into a specialized register file of a size which user code doesn't need to worry about." *Recorded:* the engine is a standard part of the architecture; in the manual's terms every system has one, software can rely on it, it is discoverable (how is pending), its descriptor and operation set are specified by the architecture, and it is not an optional PCI Express card (inference from the comparison with the FPU, the MMU and the IOMMU). It is a device on the bus and an architectural component, as the IOMMU is: this does not supersede errata 59 (no vector register file and no SIMD instruction set in the processor, the memory-mapped device route), errata 78 and the author's remark R278 (any PCI Express card may be attached and supported) or errata 95 (virtio across the board). *Not decided:* the operations and formats (J.8.1), the descriptor (J.8.2), packed lanes (J.8.3), the first engine (J.8.4) and whether the instruction set gets operations on regions of memory (J.8.5, new: the author's questions about SIMD); the open items now carry five to six options each, with precedents. *Where:* chapter 8 (8.4.1 to 8.4.3 new, the statements of 8.4 renumbered: old 8.4.1 to 8.4.3 are 8.4.4 to 8.4.6, old 8.4.4 and 8.4.5 are 8.4.7 and 8.4.8, and 8.3.5 is new), appendix J (J.8.1 to J.8.5), appendix H (the number-format and operation-list proposals now covered by J.8.1 removed from H.8), the table of chapter 1 (row 8), the figures vec-engine and vec-region (`tools/manual/vecfigs.py`).

132. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-10): the minimum of two cores is cancelled and errata 92 stands; secure mode on at least one core, on every core if it is cheap, and the dedicated roles shrink.** *The author's words, on the chapter 7 report:* "if we already decided minimum 4, then we'll stick to that, cancel minimum 2. the idea of dedicated roles now shrinks, new decision, at least one core that can be in 'secure' mode; if it doesn't take too many gates, let that be a mode for all the cores, but if it slows things down too much then return to a single secure core. btw, the 10 22 and 12 are also mystical numbers, glad they somehow connect with the number 17! you don't have to discuss it, but make sure those numbers are visible in proximity to each other." *Decided:* (1) errata 130 is withdrawn: the design supports at least four cores, sixteen or more is the preferred ceiling that no decision may preclude, the initial device has four, hoped to fit the FPGA (errata 92, 2026-11-04: "yeah let's have 4 cores minimum, 16 or more preferred"; "for initial device 4 cores is enough hopefully that fits on the fpga"). (2) At least one core can run in secure mode; if the mode costs few gates it is a mode of every core, if it slows a core too much the machine has one secure core only; the thresholds and the measurement are open (J.7.8, new). (3) The idea of dedicated roles (errata 76) shrinks: what remains is open (J.7.5, rewritten). *Done (no other decision):* the sentences changed by errata 130 are restored to the position of errata 92: 7.1.1 and its paragraph removed, 7.6.1, 7.6.3, 7.10 (the `WFE` prose and 7.10.3), the 7.11 commentary, the Kconfig item of 7.14, 1.1.4, the prose of 4.7 (4.7.1 keeps "at least one other core exists, so a way to start it is always used", which four cores keep true), 10.1.3, 10.1.8 of its fragment (a board must fit the four cores of the first device, about 4 x 16,000 lookup tables by the per-core estimate of 10.1.4, an estimate), J.7.2, J.7.5 and J.4.6; the corrections of errata 130 (the table of the seventeen, `_DB`, the block operations 7.5.8 and J.7.7, the fault category, the store buffer of `pdp64-isa.md` 17.1) stand. *Chapter 7.11:* the secure core becomes the secure mode: 7.11.3 states the mode and the rule, 7.11.4 and 7.11.5 (approved in errata 86: "i like your design. approved.") now speak of a core in secure mode with their details to be determined kept, and the relation to `DIT` (9.3.7) is stated; 7.11.6 (one core each for graphics, network and audio) and statement 3.8.2 of the graphics chapter (one core dedicated to the graphics device) move out of the body to Appendix H (7.11.6 and 3.8.2 there) with the note that they are shrunk by this decision. *Citations changed:* 3.8.2 removed, 5.7.1, 7.10.4, 9.9.7, 10.4.3 and its commentary, the glossary (secure core and dedicated core become secure mode), J.7.5 and J.7.8. *The numbers of 7.3:* the section now shows, side by side, the ten atomics (22 words, 12 operations before the signed and unsigned variants are folded), the seven other multiprocessor operations (17 in all) and the twelve system operations that are not multiprocessor (the list of 2026-10-21, R163), with `MFSR` and `MTSR` (errata 32), `PUSHX` and `POPX` (errata 37) and `ISYNC` and `HINT` (errata 39), added on 2026-10-22, said to be after the count. *Not decided:* the criteria and the threshold of J.7.8; whether `RET`, which the grammar places in the system group, belongs to the twelve (the list of 2026-10-21 does not name it).

133. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): no core is dedicated to a role, except possibly one secure core; the dedicated roles of errata 76 are cancelled.** *The author's words:* "other than potentially one core dedicated as secure, no more dedicated cores." (after the report that the dedicated roles shrink, errata 132). *Decided:* the recommendation of errata 76 (2026-11-04: one core for the graphics device, one for the network interface running the full TCP/IP stack, one for audio) is superseded and withdrawn, and so is the shrunk form of errata 132 item (3); the only core that may be dedicated is a single secure core, the fallback of the secure-mode rule of errata 132 (secure mode on every core if that is cheap, one secure core if it slows the cores too much; J.7.7). *Done (no other decision):* the manual's 7.11 states in one sentence that apart from the possible single secure core no core is dedicated to a role; 7.11.6 and 3.8.2 of the earlier proposals are deleted from Appendix H (the ledger and this log keep the record); open item J.7.5 (what remains of the dedicated cores) is deleted, and J.7.6 to J.7.8 are renumbered J.7.5 to J.7.7 (the citations in the manual, the ISA text and the decision form follow); options that depended on dedicated cores are removed from J.7.2 (option 5, non-coherent partners) and J.6.3 (option 6, polled devices on dedicated cores), and the reference to dedicated cores is removed from J.7.4 and J.8.4. The records (`smp-decisions.md`, `decisions-pending.md` Part F, `crypto-secure-core.md`, `security-round2.md`) keep their text and carry the date in their banners. *Not decided:* nothing new; the thresholds of J.7.7 stay open.

134. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the secure mode's private window and locked translation buffer (7.11.4 and 7.11.5) stay in the body of the manual.** *The author's words:* "show me what both would look like, although I lean toward keeping them in the body." (on moving 7.11.4 and 7.11.5 out to Appendix H, offered because they go beyond the approval of errata 86, "i like your design. approved."). *Decided:* 7.11.4 and 7.11.5 stay in chapter 7, tagged decided with their details to be determined, as errata 132 left them; recorded as the author's lean and kept. *Not decided:* the details (window size, latencies, the clear engine and the reserved bus slot).

135. **CORRECTIONS (no decision, 2026-11-10): the author's words on the count of seventeen and on the ordering of block operations, and what the check found.** *The author's words:* "do the full count and verify. 17 is a mystical number so cool to keep it, but if it isn't true, nix it."; "i think the number 17 was in a particular context, if you find the thread where it came up..."; "probably, its probably something we didn't make any decisions on yet, verify that." *Result* (the details are in errata 130 items (1) and (3), which stand): the count of seventeen was verified against the grammar and the dictionary and is true (ten atomics and seven others; twelve system operations that are not multiprocessor), so it stays; the only decision on the ordering of block operations is errata 39 item 7 (the accesses inside one block operation are unordered with respect to one another: 7.5.8), and everything else is open (J.7.6). The numbers 10, 22, 12 and 17 are shown side by side in 7.3 (errata 132). *Not decided:* whether `RET` belongs to the twelve; whether `HINT #1` belongs under the seventeen.

136. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-10): the encoding optimiser keeps no code-space reserve; its corpus reader is corrected; the three priorities are shown.** *The author's words:* "what do you mean what mix? just show the options for static, fetch, and live priorities, static byte size, bytes fetched, and actual operations executed"; "no reserve."; "good." (on the correction of the corpus reader that read `set cc, rd` as a label). *Decided:* (1) no reserve: the optimiser's budget is the whole first-parcel space (65,536 patterns) less the forced forms, the 5% policy is withdrawn; (2) the reader fix is approved: `set cc, rd`, `mfsr` and `mtsr` are read as numbers, not labels, so the 342 `set` instructions that counted as unencodable are encodable; (3) the optimiser reports the three objectives, static byte size, bytes fetched and operations executed. *Done:* `tools/encopt` 1.1 (`-O static|fetch|exec`, the weights file, the profile collector run with `sim64 -P` and decoded by `encdis -k`, five profile programs, the leave-one-out check, tests and man pages); the table is in `docs/encoding-work/optimizer.md` section 11. *Finding (the assistant's, a measurement, not a decision):* the static optimum is 1.73% smaller in bytes than the committed grammar and cuts bytes fetched by 3.3%; the fetch optimum gains a further 1.0% fetched but costs 5.3% in static size, and on a program left out of its training it fetches 0.1% to 8.5% more than the committed grammar (the fetch and exec optima are fitted to the five-program profile). *Not decided:* which objective the real run uses (the options are in the decision form); whether a larger profile with Lua and a real C library is wanted; the candidate forms and mnemonic classes are defined by hand in `cat.py`.

137. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-10): the LLVM back end's tables are generated from the hbnf grammar, as the assembler's and the manual's are; the LLVM port goes ahead in the order of errata 125.** *The author's words:* "of course we do llvm port, once ops are settled and pcc backend, as64 ld64 and sim64 and the hbnf are updated. the hbnf should also be used to emit tables and such that the llvm backend will also use. two birds, one stone." *Decided:* the LLVM port is a standing item, done after the operations are settled and after the pcc back end, `as64`, `ld64`, `sim64` and the grammar are updated (the order of work of errata 125 stands); the tables of the LLVM back end are emitted from the hbnf grammar, so the one grammar serves the assembler, the manual and LLVM. *Not decided:* the form of the generated LLVM tables and the generator, and the date.

138. **NO DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-13): range checks on a register value, and taint tags like Perl's; two open items, both affecting opcodes.** *The author's words:* (1) "new design features from another linux kernel developer: make some instructions or easy way to do range checks on a register value, a trap on out of range, similar to overflow, or set a bit in a sticky register, exception register, etc. same thing as on floating point math when you have nan/inf etc, you don't trap you set bits in a status register. figure out what is most efficient from a coding point of view. would this be more efficient than using the cse select min/max functions?" (2) "also perl taint mode, bits for each word in ram, to mark where the bytes came from, tagging.... do we have something like that already?" (3) "so right now, tell me how hard this would be to do with current opcodes (as per the full design), the taint thing as in perl". *What was done:* no decision was taken. The first request is the open item J.9.13 (trap, sticky flag, clamp, mask or nothing, with the instruction and byte counts of each form, the price of each in opcode patterns, and the answer to the comparison with `MIN` and `MAX`); the second and third are the open item J.9.14 (what the design already has: poison, secret taint, the valid-pointer tag, colours and the page bits; what an input-taint lane of the Perl kind would be; and the answer to the third question, a cost estimate for a compiler-inserted shadow scheme on the current opcodes and the other routes). Both are the first two entries of the feedback form (they change opcodes, and the encoding is to be re-run after the open items are settled); the body names them in 9.7.7, 6.4.4 and 9.21.1. Errata 85 had recorded "no compare-and-trap form" for the overflow study; J.9.13 says so and asks whether the new request changes it.
139. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-16): as64 is extended to accept AT&T syntax and the familiar spellings of ITS/PDP-10, MASM, Turbo Pascal, ca65 and early Unix assemblers beside the present syntax; `vec` is a prefix for vector operations; the details of both are to be determined.** *The author's words:* on the assembler: "decided, as64 will be extended to support att syntax as well as current syntax, where they don't conflict"; "let's make as64 more like microsoft style and the old its pdp-10 style, let's make it really sweet and readable I hate seeing % sprinkled everywhere"; "i accept your recommendations. decided."; "fine, we accept $ and # that also gets us m6502 right? and / comments become //"; "decided: accept the recommendations in that last paragraph"; "i accept all those things you listed as medium effort or less, and we are to skip all those things you recommended skipping" (the recommendations are the assistant's lists in the same conversation: readability changes for ITS/PDP-10, Turbo Pascal and C64 programmers, the operators of the early Unix assembler that as64 lacks, and the small additions that make as64 a relaxed superset of the classic assemblers; the author did not ask for slavish compatibility). On vectors: "how about a single 2 byte instruction that says \"the next instruction is simd/vector, and here are the two registers that mark the start and end of the memory region\"? add that to the pdp64 design"; "yes, \"vec\" as prefix is good"; "if we need to turn lb64 into a special unit with its own instruction set we will, no need to put it on the pcie bus it must always be present at a certain memory page. it can handle its own cache and memory loading etc."; "perhaps the 3 parcel opcodes are available for gemm ops and we could just keep the isa unified?"; "well if we're doing gemm and vec anyways, then we don't need to specify 2 or 6 byte encodings, the prefix coding optimizer will figure out what goes where"; "when i said gemm i assumed you were including predication and scatter/gather"; "nothing stops pdp64 from having hidden registers that the vector operations use"; "since block moves already use dw registers, would it make sense for all the vector operations to do that also?" *Decided:* (1) **The assembler.** as64 accepts, besides every present spelling (which keep their meaning: where a new spelling would change what a present form means, the present form stays): registers by bare name, and an optional `%` before a register name, ignored; `#` and `$` before an immediate; `//` as a second comment to the end of the line (`;` stays); the operators `!` (or-not, `a ! b` is `a | ~b`) and `[ ]` as grouping; `db`, `dw` and `proc`/`endp` beside the directives; `equ` for `=`; `.name` and `@name` scoped local labels; `r5+8` for `8(r5)`; the high and low byte operators; case-insensitive mnemonics under an option; the string forms of the classic assemblers; `.macro`/`.endm` with positional arguments, `.if`/`.else`/`.endif` and `.rept`. Skipped, as the author said: the full macro engines of MACRO-10 and MASM, strict octal-default numbers, column-oriented card formats, the illegal-opcode and 65816 size-tracking quirks, exact expression type tracking.
