(2) **The vector operations** (the open item J.8.5, option 1, in the form of a prefix). `vec` is a prefix instruction, one parcel, that makes the next instruction operate element by element over the memory region `[Rs, Re)` named by two registers; progress is kept in ordinary registers, as the block operations keep it, so that a fault leaves `Rs` at the first element not done and the instruction restarts, and nothing is saved at a context switch beyond the ordinary registers; an implementation may keep hidden vector registers; the encodings of `vec` and of the GEMM-family operations are left to the encoding optimiser, not fixed at 2 or 6 bytes; the accelerator `lb64` (the LB64 of `docs/pdp64-lb64-accelerator.md`, proposed for LittleBit inference; the author's idea is that it may become a unit with its own instruction set that also takes the GEMM-family operations), if it becomes such a unit, is always present at a fixed memory page and is not a device on the bus. *Details to be determined:* for the assembler, the accepted spellings of AT&T operands beyond those listed, the juxtaposition-as-addition of the early Unix assembler (low priority, it conflicts with operand lists), `^` as the type-carrying operator (it is the exclusive-or in as64 and stays so), `r5` for register-indirect (it is register-direct and stays so), the spelling of the ABI aliases `a0` to `a7` and `s0` to `s7`, the argument syntax of macros, and which of the early Unix spellings (`\/`, `\<`, `\>`) are taken; for vectors, the instruction list that `vec` may precede, the predication and gather/scatter forms, the GEMM operations and their number, whether the vector operations use double-word register pairs, the fault and restart rules for each, the effect on context switching, and the test suite that conforming implementations must pass (the author: "pdp64 could come with a test suite that all conforming implementations must pass to show it fulfills all guarantees"). *What was done:* recorded here, in the decision register (R424 on), the ledger and `docs/asm-syntax.md`; the assembler changes are made in `spec/pdp64-asm.hbnf` and `as64` (see ChangeLog); the vector decisions are recorded in J.8.5 (the decision picks its option 1) and are not yet in the grammar or the chapters, and J.9.13 and the encoding re-run are unchanged by them.
140. **NO DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-16): the corpus compiled for LoongArch64, and the bounds-check question measured against it.** *The author's words:* "compile the corpus for loongson64 so we have real data to compare against it looks like they have some of the bounds checking fused ops my italian kernel developer asked about, so see if adding them is a net win, and is the loongson64 form the optimal form, i assumed they would do a bandpass but they look more like only an upper or lower bound." *What was done:* no decision was taken. The 841-file corpus was compiled for LoongArch64 with clang at -O1, -O2, -O3 and -Os (the pdp64 row first reproduced to the byte, 964,158 instruction bytes) and at -O2 and -Os with the bounds sanitizer; the checks were counted (834 in 840 files) and classified, and each form was priced for pdp64; `docs/encoding-work/loongarch.md` is the record, the figures are in J.9.13 and its option 7 is LoongArch's own forms. Findings (the assistant's, not decisions): LoongArch's `ASRTLE`, `ASRTGT` and the fused `LDLE`, `LDGT`, `STLE`, `STGT` are one-sided (an upper or a lower bound, as the author read), no compiler emits them and no corpus or Linux LoongArch code uses them; 69% of the checks in the corpus are one unsigned compare, 21% are a signed index with an offset (a true band); a compare-and-trap ties `CBHIS` in the check itself and saves only the failure stubs (see errata 143); a two-bound `CHK2` saves 176 instructions and 704 bytes of the corpus and is the only new form that pays; LoongArch's form is not the best one (no immediate form, no band, the fused forms check an address). Dynamic counts were not made. The decision on J.9.13 stays with the author.
141. **NO DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-17): a corpus that exercises AVX2, NEON and RISC-V vector code, so that the vector decision is the best one.** *The author's words:* "i guess we also need something in the corpus that exercises the avx2 and simd instructions, perhaps that video codec decoding library, and if it exercises arm and riscv vector ops, even better for our comparison, i want our vector decision to be the best one." *What was done:* no decision was taken. dav1d (the AV1 decoder) was fetched and its pixel kernels compiled for pdp64 and for AVX2, AArch64, RISC-V vector and LoongArch LASX; its hand-written NEON and RISC-V vector assembly was assembled and counted (the x86 assembly was not: NASM is not installed); the loops of the pdp64 code were classified by the script `tools/arch-compare/vecwin.py`, which is the script the author asked for on 2026-11-16 to find where `vec` would win. `docs/encoding-work/vector-study.md` is the record and J.8.5 has the figures. Findings (the assistant's, not decisions): a `vec` on one existing instruction serves 21% of the codec's map loops (they are filters with about 8 loads for each store); chaining, a sliding-window multiply-accumulate, widening, min/max/clamp, a select and reductions are what the loops need; gathers were not needed and shuffles are 5%. Static counts only.
142. **CORRECTION (the author's, 2026-11-17): the history of the pdp64 is told in one sentence.** *The author's words:* "also update the pdp64 history; it started as a mashup of the pdp11 and c64 with secure extensions, then grew to a 64 bit smp cpu with even more security and convenience." *What was done:* no decision was taken; the account of the history was corrected. Appendix G (the evolution of the design) now begins with that sentence and with the three stages it names, each tied to the dated messages of G.1 (the mashup of the PDP-11 with the Blit terminal and the C64's graphics and audio, the step to 64 bits, and the growth to a multiprocessor with more security and more convenience), and the README says it in a line with a link to appendix G. The dated list of G.1 is unchanged; the first message in it that names security as a design goal is of 2026-10-22 (R253), and the author's sentence puts the secure extensions in the idea from its start.
143. **NO DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-17): what bounds-check instructions would be a net win, and a survey of what the architectures have.** *The author's words:* "so how do we find out what type of bounds check instructions would be a net win? I want a detailed document of what loong and other arches have in this department, why they aren't emitted, how much savings they could theoretically give, and what we could beneficially add to pdp64." *What was done:* no decision was taken. `docs/encoding-work/bounds-check-isas.md` gives the method, the catalogue (LoongArch, Loongson MIPS, MIPS, SPARC, PowerPC, s390x, x86 with MPX, m68k, VAX, and the architectures that have none), the reasons they are not emitted (verified in LLVM, GCC, rustc and GNAT: only s390x gets a compare-and-trap from ordinary code), the savings (on Ada's run-time library the checks add 21.3% to the instructions and the failure paths are 9.5% of the code; in Rust's generated parsers 3.1% of the code; on the C corpus 0.17%); Ada and Rust are added to the corpus (`tools/arch-compare/corpus-ada.txt`, `corpus-rust.txt`), and what the assistant would add (a compare-and-trap with a table of check sites, a trap-if-zero, a two-bound check, and a compiler path first). The correction of errata 140: a compare-and-trap ties `CBHIS` in the check itself and saves the failure stubs, which the pricing of `loongarch.md` did not charge. J.9.13 carries the figures. The decision stays with the author.
144. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-17): a trap-if-zero, a two-bound check and the pcc path first; the compare-and-trap with a table of check sites is explained and not yet decided.** *The author's words:* "i agree, decided. trap if zero, and two bound check, and pcc path first." and "for compare and trap with table of call sites, explain it. i don't understand it." *Decided:* (1) the pdp64 gets a **trap-if-zero** instruction, for the null checks (the largest class of check in Ada's run-time library, 45%); (2) the pdp64 gets a **two-bound check**, an instruction that tests a value against a lower and an upper bound together, for an index whose lower bound is not zero; (3) **the pcc path comes first**: before either instruction is added to the encoding, pcc learns to emit the check (an explicit built-in or a fusion pass), so that the instructions are used. *Details to be determined:* the names, operand forms and encodings of both instructions (the opcode price is measured in J.9.13: the two-bound form takes no pattern in the three-register group), what the trap reports and how (whether a table of check sites carries the location: the next item), whether the two-bound form takes registers or immediates for its bounds, the shape of pcc's path (a built-in such as `__builtin_chk`, or a pass that fuses a compare and a branch to a failure block), and which language front ends reach it (pcc has none for Ada or Rust). *Not decided:* the compare-and-trap with a table of check sites (J.9.13 option 2; errata 85 recorded no compare-and-trap form). The author asked for an explanation; it is in `docs/encoding-work/bounds-check-isas.md` section 5.0, and the choice stays open. *What was done:* recorded here, in the register (R441 and R442), in J.9.13 and in the ledger; nothing is added to the grammar or the encoding yet, by the decision of the order.
145. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-17): the compare-and-trap with a table of check sites is approved and added; the details are to be determined.** *The author's words:* "compare trap with table is approved, add it." and "how to make linux kernel use it, since its already doing something similar." *Decided:* the pdp64 has a **compare-and-trap** (an unsigned compare of a register with a register or an immediate bound that raises a trap when the condition holds) and a **table of check sites**: for each such instruction the assembler writes a record (the address of the instruction, the kind of check, the file and line) into a section that is not loaded, so that the trap handler finds which check failed and the compiler needs no stub of its own for it (J.9.13 option 2, which errata 85 had recorded as not wanted; errata 85 is replaced on this point). It joins the trap-if-zero, the two-bound check and the order "pcc first" of errata 144. *Details to be determined:* the mnemonic, the conditions (both polarities are needed, the Linux `BUG_ON` traps when its argument is not zero, the null check when it is zero), the register and immediate forms, the length and encoding; the format of the table (the assistant proposes the format of Linux's `struct bug_entry`, so that the kernel's `report_bug()` finds the sites with no new code: `docs/encoding-work/bounds-check-isas.md` section 6); what the trap frame holds (the address and the length of the trapping instruction, so that a warning can resume); where the kind of check is carried (in the table, or in a field of the instruction as x86's `ud1` and arm64's `brk` carry it); the pcc path (a built-in or a fusion pass) and the LLVM pattern. *What was done:* recorded here, in the register (R443 and R444), in J.9.13, in 9.7.7 and in the ledger; the grammar and the encoding are not changed yet, by the order of errata 144; section 6 of the bounds-check record answers the author's question on the Linux kernel.
146. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): the pcc path of the run-time checks is started.** *What was done* (the order of errata 144: the compiler first): in the pcc clone, on the branch `chk-builtins` (commit 2e7b590 on `single-pdp64`, not pushed), three built-ins: `__builtin_chk(i, n)` (an index against a length, unsigned), `__builtin_chkz(p)` (a null check) and `__builtin_chk2(i, lo, hi)` (an index against a lower and an upper bound); each is the value when the check holds and otherwise a call of `__chk_fail(kind, file, line, value, limit)`. They are written as trees, so that they work now and the compare, the branch and the failure block can later become one instruction and a table entry. The test `arch/pdp64/test/c/chk.c` and the whole pcc suite pass (81 of 81 under three flag sets). *Found:* `__builtin_chk(i, 100)` compiles to one instruction on the path of a passing check (`cbcs r8,#100,ok`), the failure call's arguments are set up in the failure block; `__builtin_chk2(i, 10, 50)` compiles to `lea -10(r8),r0` and `cbcs r0,#40,ok`, which is the `lea` that the two-bound check saves; two costs remain that the instructions will remove (the value is kept in a register that survives the call, which makes a leaf function push and pop it, and each failure block holds about five instructions of set-up). *To do* (details to be determined): an option that checks every subscript of an array of known size and every pointer dereference of a checked type; the lowering to the instructions and the table, which waits for their mnemonics, forms and encodings; the LLVM pattern.
147. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): pcc's option `-xchecks`, the automatic check of the subscripts of arrays of known size, and its measured cost.** *What was done:* in the pcc clone, branch `chk-builtins` (commit a076474, not pushed): `ccom -xchecks` checks `a[i]`, `i[a]` and each subscript of `b[i][j]` against the size of the array, as `__builtin_chk` does (not for arrays of one element or none, constant subscripts in range, array parameters, or `a[i]` under `&`); the test `arch/pdp64/test/c/chkauto.c` and the whole pcc suite pass (84 of 84). *Measured* on the 841-file corpus (`arch-data/pdp64-checks-summary.txt`): **9,782 checks in 176 files; instructions 296,082 to 364,745 (+23.2%), instruction bytes 964,158 to 1,220,032 (+26.5%); 7.0 instructions and 26.2 bytes for each check.** One of the seven is the check, six are its failure block (the call's set-up). With the compare-and-trap and the table of check sites (errata 145) each check would be one instruction of 4 bytes: +9,782 instructions (+3.3%) and about +39 KB (+4.1%): **about 85% of the cost of the checks as pcc compiles them today is the failure blocks.** pcc has no range analysis, so none of the 9,782 is removed as provable; the sanitizer of clang found 834 checks in the same files because its optimiser had removed the provable ones. *To do* (details to be determined): the lowering to the instructions and the table, a pass that removes the provable checks (done, errata 148), checks for pointer dereferences of a checked type.

148. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): the pass of `-xchecks` that drops the checks it can prove (the author asked for it), and what is left.** *What was done* (pcc clone, branch `chk-builtins`): a subscript is not checked when its range lies inside the array: from the subscript alone (a constant; `x & 7`; `u % 10`; an unsigned char or short; sums of such), or from a fact about a loop variable, for `for (i = c; i < K; i += s)` (also `<=`, unsigned compares, `for (int i = 0; ...)`) with c >= 0, s > 0 and K constant, the body sees c <= i < K. Such a check is built, noted, and decided when the function is complete (in pass 2): it goes if the body never wrote `i` (any assignment, `++`, `+=`, address-of), no user label is inside the loop (a `goto` could enter past the test), and the address of `i` is taken nowhere in the function; the branch and its failure block are then deleted. Only locals the compiler keeps in temporaries are followed. Test: `arch/pdp64/test/c/chkopt.c` (a loop in range is silent, one bound too large still traps once, a loop that changes `i` keeps its check); the whole pcc suite passes (87 of 87). *Measured* on the same 841 files: **checks 9,782 to 6,980 (2,802 dropped, 29%); instructions 296,082 to 345,444 (+16.7%, was +23.2%); instruction bytes 964,158 to 1,147,316 (+19.0%, was +26.5%).** With the compare-and-trap and the table of check sites (errata 145) the 6,980 checks left would be about +6,980 instructions (+2.4%) and +28 KB (+2.9%). A variable limit is handled too (`i < n`): a narrow type of `n` bounds it (unsigned char, unsigned or signed short), and a local written exactly once in the function, with a constant, before the loop counts as that constant (tests in `chkopt.c`: a bound of 5 is silent, 11 traps, a second write of `n` keeps the check); on the corpus this changes nothing (still 6,980 checks), the loops there take their limits from parameters and data. Guard tracking is in too: the condition of an `if`/`while` (with `&&`, `||`, `!`, and `(unsigned)n >= N`) gives what is known in the then-part, the else-part and the loop body, and an `if` without else whose then-part never reaches its end (`if (n >= N) return;`) gives the negation to the end of the enclosing statement; a write to the variable, its address, a label (a case label for the guards before the switch) ends a guard (`arch/pdp64/test/c/chkguard.c`, the suite passes: 90 of 90). On the corpus it removes 12 more: **6,968 checks, instructions +16.7% (345,369), bytes +19.0% (1,147,042)**; the corpus has few guarded subscripts because the compiler does not see across calls and most subscripts come from data. Guards inside one expression are covered too: the right operand of `&&` (left true), of `||` (left false), the arms of `?:` (`n < 10 && a[n]`, `c ? a[n] : 0`), tested in `chkguard.c` (suite 90 of 90); on the corpus it changes no count (6,968; one file, zlib's `d_code`, gets a different branch layout, +2 instructions). The remaining cases are in too: an `if`/`else` of which only one part exits (the facts of the other hold after it, with what that part did to them), a loop left by its condition and not by a `break` (`while`, `for`, `do`-`while`: the negation of the condition holds after it), facts that relate two variables (`m <= 10 && i < m`, `for (i = 0; i < m; i++)` after `if (m > 10) return;`, the limit taken from what is known of `m`; a derived fact stands only while the facts it came from do), and decreasing loops (`for (i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--)`, `i > 0`, `i -= 3`; an unsigned variable only with step 1 and a limit of 1 or more, so that it cannot wrap). Measured: **6,929 checks (from 9,782, 29% dropped); 345,131 instructions (+16.6%), 1,146,166 instruction bytes (+18.9%)**. A bug in the loop facts found on the way is fixed: a `for` with an empty first part took the last constant assigned to the variable before the loop, which is wrong if the assignment was in a branch (`i = 0; if (c) i = 7; for (; i < 10; i++) a[i]`); only the form with a declaration (`for (int i = 0; ...)`) or an assignment in the first part counts now. Still not provable, by any rule of this kind: a subscript that comes from a call's result or from data (the compiler has nothing to prove from), arrays reached through pointers (their size is not known), and checks whose guard is in another function. Added to `doc/PDP64-OPT.md` of the pcc clone; `docs/encoding-work/arch-data/pdp64-checks-summary.txt` has the numbers.

149. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): the `vec` forms written by hand for eight kernels, the vec corpus extended, and a correction about NASM.** *Asked by the author:* "write the vec forms by hand for three kernels and also lgbm mamba3 and ffmpeg"; "benchmark the corpus for the vec savings across the main architectures that have vec forms"; "what is our vec corpus?" (the corpus was dav1d only; it now has FFmpeg, LightGBM and mamba.c; cngpt and cugpt, which call OpenBLAS, were done next, see errata 150). *What was done* (`docs/encoding-work/vec-forms.md`, data in `arch-data/vec-forms/`): eight kernels (dav1d `prep`, 8-tap horizontal `put`, DC predictor sum and fill; FFmpeg `vector_fmac_scalar` and `pixel_sad`; LightGBM histogram construction; the selective scan of mamba.c) written in a proposed block notation of the `vec` prefix (`vec Rs,Re` ... `vend`, hidden vector registers; **the assistant's proposal, not a decision**; none of it assembled or run), counted against pcc's scalar code and against clang's code for AVX2, AVX-512, NEON, SVE, RISC-V V and LoongArch LASX, and against the hand-written x86, NEON and RVV functions. *Results:* the single-instruction prefix of errata 139 expresses 2 of the 8 kernels (a fill and a reduction), because pdp64 allows one memory operand per instruction; the other 6 need the block form; the 8 vec forms are 111 instructions against 186 for pcc's scalar functions (97 with a sliding-window multiply-accumulate in the filter), and a block operation count of 0.03 to 2.4 for each element against 3 to 48 for the scalar loop; clang vectorized no loop of the LightGBM histogram on any target, and its vector code for the filter is 3 to 5 times the scalar size; no speed was measured. *Correction:* `vector-study.md` and the notes said building NASM from source "was not allowed"; the author never refused it: the first attempt was stopped by the automatic permission check of the assistant's tool and was not retried. NASM 2.16.03 was built on 2026-11-17 and assembles the x86 assembly of dav1d and FFmpeg; the wording is corrected in `vector-study.md`. *To do* (details to be determined): the instruction list and semantics of the block, validated by an assembler and a simulator; the gather and scatter-add rules; `vexp`; a loop census of the C of FFmpeg and LightGBM; dynamic counts.

150. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): the OpenBLAS calls of cugpt and cngpt written as `vec` forms (the author asked for them), and a correction.** *Asked by the author:* "do the cngpt and cugpt openblas gemm forms too". *What was done* (`docs/encoding-work/gemm-forms.md`, data in `arch-data/vec-forms/blas/`): cugpt calls BLAS levels 1 and 2 only (`dgemv` 2, `dger` 1, `ddot` 5, `daxpy` 9, `dscal` 3, vectors of 4 to 16 doubles); cngpt calls `sgemm` at 11 sites (GPT-2 sizes 768 and 1024 by default). Forms (the assistant's proposal, unassembled): the six level 1 and 2 calls in the block notation of errata 149; the cngpt linear layer in four shapes: a dot product for each output (0.5 block operations for each multiply-add), a register-blocked micro-kernel with eight hidden accumulators (0.141), a hidden tile with an outer-product instruction (0.047), and the whole GEMM as one `gemm.f` of the `lb64` unit with a descriptor (17 set-up instructions for any size). Against clang's autovectorized naive loop (1.06 to 3.5 instructions for each multiply-add) and OpenBLAS's hand-written kernels assembled from its sources (Haswell AVX2 0.25, NEON 0.45, LASX 0.24, RISC-V V 0.29 compiled from intrinsics, SVE 2.6/VL). *Finding:* a plain `vec` dot product is two to three times worse than the hand kernels; the register-blocked block form beats them on instruction count by dropping pointer bumps, prefetches and broadcasts; a block that holds partial sums in hidden registers has no resume rule after a fault or an interrupt inside the K loop (errata 139 says nothing is saved beyond the ordinary registers): the main design question for GEMM in the block, which the descriptor of the `lb64` unit avoids by holding the progress. *Correction:* `vec-forms.md` (as first written) said that `cugpt.c` calls `sgemm` and `dgemm` at 27 sites; it calls neither (the count came from a command whose output was misread). *Amended 2026-11-17, at the author's questions ("what does gemm stand for", "why wouldn't lb64 use progress registers", "i have no idea what lb64 stands for either, check the history in the db"):* (1) form 4 was rewritten twice: first with the progress in three ordinary registers (row, column, K offset) and a K-block commit, then, after the author asked whether starting over from zero would do (analysis in `gemm-forms.md` section 5a: a whole GEMM restarted from zero livelocks against the timer tick, a tile does not), with **one-tile restart**: the constants in a descriptor, the progress in two ordinary registers (the row and column of the next tile), the tile computed over all of K and written once with its pages checked first, the write-back and the advance of the registers one uninterruptible step, all other state internal and droppable; 19 set-up instructions. The first version kept the progress in the descriptor, which the decision does not say. (2) **"lb64" is the assistant's name, first used on 2026-10-20 (claude_web, thread 533, message 52, 20:34), for the sign-flip accelerator for LittleBit inference that the author had asked about at 20:28 (an accelerator on the bus for "samsung's littlebit-2 encoding"); no document expands it; it has no multiplier and no instruction set in its proposal, and an `sgemm` of floats is not its work (the proposal's engine for that is ATT64).** The author's use on 2026-11-16 is the idea of growing it into a unit with its own instruction set. This entry, errata 139 and J.8.5 had called it "the GEMM unit `lb64`"; they now say "the accelerator `lb64`", and `gemm-forms.md` §0 has the history. *To do* (details to be determined): the block's restart rule for accumulators, packing, edge tiles and the other transposes, a simulator and an assembler for the forms.

151. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): step 1 of the check instructions, a proposal of their names, operand forms, trap and table, for the author to accept.** *Asked by the author:* "yes, start on step 1 for the checks" (step 1: fix the mnemonics and operand forms that errata 144 and 145 left to be determined, before pcc lowers `-xchecks` to them and the encoding is re-optimised). *What was done* (`docs/encoding-work/check-instructions.md`; nothing is in the grammar, the assembler or the compiler): the census of the 6,923 checks that `-xchecks` leaves in the corpus (`tools/arch-compare/checksites.py`, `arch-data/pdp64-checksites.txt`): all are `cbcs i, #bound` (an unsigned index against an array length), **all bounds are immediates, 98.3% fit 8 bits and 100% fit 12**, 6.2% fit the 5 bits of the short compare-and-branch leaves, 89% share one bound (88, one project). *Proposed:* the family **`ct<cc>`** named like `cb<cc>` (`cthis` an alias of `ctcc`, the index check); an immediate form of 12 bits for four conditions (`cc`, `hi`, `eq`, `ne`; 8 patterns) and a register form for all fourteen (the `R2` group, no patterns); **the trap-if-zero as the one-parcel `cteq rs` / `ctne rs`** (64 patterns, 32 with a hot register); **the two-bound check `chk2 rs, rlo, rhi`**, signed and half-open (`rlo <= rs < rhi`, as pcc's `__builtin_chk2` already means), in the `R3` group, no patterns, bounds in registers first; about 72 new first-parcel patterns in all; no flags read or written; a new fault vector 16 `check` with `EPC` the address of the instruction and the length decoded by the handler; the table of check sites in the section `__bug_table` as Linux's 12-byte `struct bug_entry` (verified in the kernel source on this machine), the kind of check in bits 5 to 7 of its flags (which Linux leaves free; bits 8 and up are the taint), filled by a directive `.chk KIND, "file", LINE` that pcc emits before each check; and a way to use them before the encoding changes: `as64` expands `cthis rs, #n` into `cbcs rs, #n, .Lok`, `bpt`, `.Lok:` with the table record for the `bpt`, which is 2 instructions and 6 bytes against the call-and-stub's 7.1 and 26.3, so that pcc's output, the table and the kernel's handler can be tried now. *Estimate:* with one instruction of 4 bytes a check the checked corpus would add +2.3% instructions and +2.9% bytes instead of +16.6% and +19.0% (not measured: the instruction does not exist). *To be decided by the author:* the six points of section 7 of the record (the family and names, `chk2`'s semantics, the immediate, the trap and its vector, the table and what "not loaded" meant in errata 145, and whether `as64` may expand the mnemonics now). Until then the details stay to be determined (errata 144, 145).

152. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-17): the check instructions are accepted as proposed in errata 151; as64 expands them now; the encodings are the prefix optimiser's.** *The author's words:* "accept all six, expand in as64 now"; and, when the assistant had priced the forms in patterns and groups: "but our prefix optimizer will decide the encodings anyways". *Decided:* (1) the family `ct<cc>` named like `cb<cc>` (`cthis` an alias of `ctcc`, `ctlo` of `ctcs`), the trap-if-zero as the one-parcel `cteq rs` and `ctne rs`, the two-bound check as `chk2 rs, rlo, rhi`; (2) `chk2` is signed and half-open (`rlo <= rs < rhi`) with registers for its bounds; (3) the immediate of the compare-and-trap is 12 bits, for the conditions `cc`, `hi`, `eq` and `ne`, and every condition has a register form; (4) the trap is a fault on a new vector 16, `check`, with `EPC` the address of the check instruction and the length decoded by the handler; (5) the table is Linux's 12-byte `struct bug_entry` in the section `__bug_table`, the kind of check in bits 5 to 7 of its flags, filled from a directive `.chk KIND, "file", LINE` that precedes the instruction; the assistant's reading of "not loaded" in errata 145 (kept out of the instruction stream and the hot memory, but present for the handler) is accepted; (6) **as64 expands the mnemonics now** into `cb..` and `bpt` with the table record for the `bpt`, so that pcc's output, the table and the kernel's handler can be tried before the encoding changes. **The encodings are not decided here**: the shapes, widths, groups, prefixes and sub-opcodes are the prefix optimiser's output (R434), and the pattern counts of `docs/encoding-work/check-instructions.md` are estimates for pricing only (that record is reworded). *Details still to be determined:* the encodings; the kernel's handler.

153. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): pcc emits the check instructions from `-xchecks` and the built-ins, and the corpus measured with them.** *Asked by the author:* "make pcc emit ct and .chk from -xchecks" (the pcc path of errata 144, 145 and 152). *What was done* (pcc clone, branch `chk-builtins`, commit 3f0d3a7, not pushed): two new tree operators, `CHKT(value, bound)` (traps unless the value is below the bound, unsigned) and `CHKZ(value)` (traps if zero), each its own value; pass 2 prints `cthis rs, #imm` (the 12-bit immediate), `cthis rs, rb` (a bound that does not fit) or `cteq rs`, each after a `.chk KIND, "file", LINE` line; the place of a check travels in a node attribute (`ATTR_CHKSITE`: the node's `n_name` shares its storage with the type's structure and was overwritten); the pass that drops provable checks removes `CHKT` and `CHKZ` nodes as it removed the call-and-stub blocks; the operators are kept as having an effect (a check whose value is unused is not deleted as a useless statement); `-xchk-call` selects the old form, which the run-time tests use (`chk.c`, `chkauto.c`, `chkopt.c`, `chkguard.c`: a program cannot count a trap), and the new tests `chkinsn.c` and `chkinsn.sh` check the instruction form; the suite passes (93 of 93 under three flag sets); `__builtin_chk2` keeps the call form (a three-operand tree has no template yet). *Measured* on the 841 files with `as64`'s expansion (a compare-and-branch over a `bpt`: 2 instructions and 6 bytes for a check): **6,932 checks; instructions 296,082 to 326,934 (+10.4%, was +16.6% as call-and-stub), instruction bytes 964,158 to 1,053,790 (+9.3%, was +19.0%)**; the expansion accounts for 13,864 of the 30,852 added instructions (2 for each check) and about 17,000 come from elsewhere (not analysed: the copy of the index into a register that the check may overwrite, the bound loaded into a register, hoisting); with the real one-instruction `cthis` and no copies the figure would be about +2.3% and +2.9% (errata 147's estimate); the table of check sites is in `__bug_table` and the file names in `.data`, not in the instruction bytes. *To do* (details to be determined): the copy that the destructive template makes (a form that does not overwrite its operand), `chk2` as an operator, the instructions in the grammar and the encoding (the prefix optimiser, errata 152), the kernel's handler.

154. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-17): the pdp64's GPU, when there is one, uses unified memory; a region of RAM may be fast video memory, which is to be determined; the design does not depend on it and allows it. And a scope rule for the CPU's instruction set.** *The author's words:* "we aren't stepping into gpu territory are we? that's a different fish right? now wanting to add stuff that only makes sense on a gpu while rarely adding value on the cpu." and "and i decide now, our gpu will do unified memory; perhaps some chunk of ram will be fast vram, not sure, that is tbd and our design doesn't depend on it but does allow it". *Decided:* (1) **unified memory**: the GPU (an accelerator on the bus that uses the same RAM and does its own DMA, as the author defined it on 2026-10-20) addresses the same memory as the CPUs, through the same page tables or a mapping of them, with no separate copy of the data; (2) **a part of RAM may be fast video memory** (a chunk with different speed or placement): **to be determined**; the design must not depend on it and must allow it (for instance by the memory type of a page, `MT` in the page-table entry, which already names cached, uncached and device memory). *The author's point, taken as a rule* (the assistant's reading; **the author corrected it at once, errata 155: nothing is removed or ruled out of the CPU until its cost and benefit are analysed**): **the CPU's instruction set gets only what pays on a CPU**, in ordinary code and not only in machine learning or graphics; what makes sense only on a GPU belongs to the accelerator, not to the CPU. *What follows for the studies of errata 149 and 150:* the **`vec` block** (a loop over a region with hidden registers: loads, lane operations, reductions) is CPU SIMD, the same class as RISC-V vector or ARM SVE, and its kernels come from the codec and DSP code of ordinary programs (dav1d, FFmpeg) which a CPU runs; the **tile with an outer-product instruction** (`vtfmopa`), the **whole-GEMM instruction** (`gemm.f`), the **scatter-add** of the histogram and the **vector exponential** are accelerator operations (matrix engines and GPUs have them; CPUs mostly do not) and are **not proposed for the CPU**: they stay in the records as what an accelerator would take, with the `lb64` idea of errata 139 (a unit that is always present at a memory page and may have its own instruction set). *Not decided:* which accelerator operations exist, the memory model between CPU and GPU (coherence, who owns a page) beyond "unified", the fast-VRAM region.

155. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-17): the GEMM and vec work stays in the design until a proper cost-benefit analysis; until there is a GPU, SIMD on the CPU is a goal for games and for language-model inference on machines without a large GPU (a "gpu-lite").** *The author's words:* "don't get rid of gemm or other vec stuff until we properly analyse cost benefit; until we get a gpu, basic simd type stuff could be very beneficial for games and llm stuff for people who don't have a huge power guzzling gpu available. like a gpu-lite." *Decided:* (1) nothing that errata 139, 149 and 150 recorded is dropped: the `vec` block, the sliding-window multiply-accumulate, the gather and scatter-add, the vector exponential, the tile with an outer-product instruction and the whole-GEMM unit all stay open; (2) **a cost-benefit analysis comes before any is removed or accepted**; (3) the **goal** for them is the CPU-only machine: games and language-model inference for people who have no large, power-hungry GPU (the pdp64 as a "gpu-lite"); the GPU of errata 154 comes later and with unified memory. *This overrides the scope rule that errata 154 and `gemm-forms.md` section 0a drew* (the assistant's reading that the tile and the GEMM instruction are "not proposed for the CPU"): they are open, not out; section 0a is reworded. *To do* (details to be determined): the analysis itself: for each operation, the workloads that use it (games: physics, skinning, audio, software rasterization; inference: the matrix-vector and matrix-matrix products of quantized and floating-point models, the scan of a state-space model), the instructions and bytes it saves, its opcode and state cost (hidden registers, tiles, the resume rule), its power against a GPU's, and the measurement on real kernels (dynamic counts, a simulator).

156. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): the cost-benefit table of the vector, GEMM and accelerator operations is begun (the author asked for it, errata 155).** *Asked:* "start on the cost-benefit table". *What was done* (`docs/encoding-work/vec-cost-benefit.md`, data `arch-data/vec-forms/games/`, `tools/arch-compare/costbenefit.py`): seven kernels of games and of language-model inference (the int8 and 4-bit dot products of a quantized layer, a normalization, a softmax, alpha blending, audio mixing, a vertex transform) written as pcc scalar code and as hand-written `vec` forms and compiled by clang for five vector instruction sets; a model of a frame and of a token with the assumptions stated (blending a 1080p frame: 149 million scalar instructions, about 50 ms, against about 2 ms in the block; decoding a 7-billion-parameter model on one core is compute-bound by 3 to 8 times on plain widen-multiply-reduce SIMD, memory-bound only for a small model or with an int8 dot-product instruction at two operations a cycle; none of these figures is measured); a table of fifteen operations with benefit, cost, evidence grade (measured, estimated, unverified, not studied) and the assistant's provisional lean: the block with reductions, widening and saturating operations, the sliding window and the nibble operations pay on every kernel at little state; the broadcast multiply-add and the int8 dot are likely; gather, segment loads, predication, number formats and the vector exponential are open or not yet studied; the tile and the whole-GEMM instruction have the largest benefit and the largest state and are open. *Not done:* dynamic counts (no simulator runs the operations), power and area, a real frame or a real token, segment loads, predication and number formats. The decision stays with the author.

157. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-17): the yes and likely operations of the cost-benefit table are added to the design; the open ones keep being studied; the segment loads study was done; the gate counts were asked and given.** *The author's words:* "add the segment loads study; add all the yes and likely leans, keep studying the open ones. update pcc and the prefix optimizer"; and "make sure to tell how many gates those will add". *Decided:* (1) **added to the design** (the instruction set of the `vec` block, details to be determined: `docs/encoding-work/vec-isa.md`): the block with hidden vector registers and the decided prefix (rows 1 and 2), reductions into a register (3), widening, narrowing and saturating operations (4), the sliding-window multiply-accumulate (5), the broadcast multiply-add with the larger register file for the micro-kernel (6, the resume rule to be settled), the int8 dot-product (7), bit-field and nibble operations (8), gather (10, scatter-add stays open), and segment loads and stores (11, now measured); (2) **kept under study**: scatter-add, predication, number formats, the vector exponential and its accuracy, the tile with an outer-product and the whole-GEMM instruction; (3) pcc and the prefix optimiser are to be updated to these (what was done and what was not: below). *Segment loads* (`arch-data/vec-forms/seg/`): four kernels of interleaved data, 1 to 5 block operations (7 to 25%) fewer with segment loads than with strided loads, each field read once; the NEON code of clang (`ld2`, `ld4`, `st4`) is half the size of AVX2's shuffles; 2,000 to 5,000 gates. *Gates* (`docs/encoding-work/vec-gates.md`; blocks synthesised with yosys, the rest estimated): the block about 100,000 NAND2 equivalents with 16 integer lanes and about 180,000 with 8 floating-point lanes; the yes rows beyond the block 24,000 to 30,000; the likely rows 71,000 to 76,000; together about 195,000 to 280,000; the tile 260,000 to 665,000; the whole-GEMM unit 1.2 to 1.7 million. *What was done for pcc and the optimiser:* see errata 158.

158. **NO DECISION (2026-11-17): what was done for pcc and the prefix optimiser after errata 157, and what was not.** *The prefix optimiser* (the author asked for it to be updated): the model of the check instructions was added to `docs/codegen-data/encoding/cat.py` (the file the exporter and `encopt` read): the fourteen register-register compare-and-trap words in the group `R2`, `chk2` in `R3`, the trap-if-zero `cteq` and `ctne` as a forced one-parcel form `CT1`, and the immediate forms as candidates (`CTI` of 3 to 8 bits, one parcel, and `CTM` of 6 to 28 bits, two parcels), like the `cmp` immediates. *Run* (`tools/encopt/export.py` and `encopt`, on the 841-file corpus compiled by pcc with `-xchecks`, 6,932 checks, 319,993 instructions; and on the plain corpus): **the proved optimum chooses `CTM12/a`, a register and a 12-bit unsigned immediate in two parcels with a prefix of 13 bits (8 first-parcel patterns), and `CT1` takes 64 patterns (prefix 10); the register-register words and `chk2` take free sub-opcodes of `R2` and `R3` (no patterns)**: 72 patterns in all, the estimate of errata 151. No one-parcel immediate form is chosen: 89% of the checks have the bound 88 and a one-parcel form that holds it would cost more code space than the 2 bytes saved on 6,144 checks. Bytes of the corpus at the optimum: plain corpus 947,562 with the old model and 947,684 with the new (**the check instructions cost 122 bytes, 0.013%, in code space they displace on a corpus that has no checks**); corpus with checks 1,006,384 (the checks make it 58,700 bytes larger than the plain corpus, which includes the other instructions that the checked code needs, errata 153). With the old model the same checks are unencodable. *Not done at the time (done in errata 160):* the **grammar was not regenerated** (`spec/pdp64/pdp64.hbnf`): the wiring from `encopt`'s result to the grammar is not done in this tree (`optimizer.md` section 7.6), a regeneration renumbers every opcode and every generated file and test, and the new instructions need `sim64` and QEMU to execute them (a fault on vector 16) before the assembler's expansion into `cb..` and `bpt` can go; until then pcc's output is assembled by that expansion. *pcc:* it prints `ctcc` (the canonical name; `cthis` is an alias) and `cteq`, with the `.chk` lines (errata 153); **it has no vectorizer** and therefore emits none of the vector instructions of errata 157: the choices are in `vec-isa.md` section 5 (built-ins for hand-written kernels, a vectorizer for the shapes of the study, or general auto-vectorization); the corpus for the optimiser contains no vector instruction until one of them exists, so the optimiser can only price them by estimate (`vec-isa.md` section 3: tens of patterns). *To do* (details to be determined): a decision on adopting the regenerated grammar and the wiring to do it; the executors of the check instructions in `sim64` and QEMU; the form of the pcc vector path; the vector instructions in the model of the optimiser (shapes for the vector registers) once loops exist.

159. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-17): the output of the prefix optimiser is used (every opcode is renumbered), and the LLVM back end is made now and becomes the standard compiler for development.** *The author's words:* "so renumber every opcode duh, why run hte optimizer without using the output?" (on errata 158, which had run the optimiser and left the grammar unchanged because it would renumber every opcode); and "if pcc has no vectorizer, it is time to do that llvm backend and switch to it as our standard for development". *Decided:* (1) **the regenerated grammar is adopted**: the optimiser's selection, with the check instructions, becomes `spec/pdp64/pdp64.hbnf` and everything generated from it (the order of errata 125: the optimiser, the grammar, the assembler tables, sim64, then the others), with the tests regenerated; renumbering is accepted; (2) **the LLVM back end is to be built now**, the machine-code layer (milestone M1, frozen on the original encoding since errata 122) ported to the new encoding from the generated tables, then a target machine and a code generator (`llc`, `clang`), vectorization through LLVM's own vectorizers; (3) **LLVM (clang) becomes the standard compiler for development**; the role of pcc afterwards (a bootstrap and reference compiler, kept current or frozen) is to be determined. This replaces "LLVM back end: frozen until the port" (errata 125, R396, R420) now that the opcode decisions that matter for the port are taken. *To be determined:* the schedule; the machine for the LLVM build (the checkout `llvmorg-21.1.8` and a build are not on this machine: R217); how the `vec` block maps to LLVM (see below); the C library and the OpenBSD port that the compiler serves.

160. **DONE (2026-11-17): the regenerated grammar is adopted (errata 159); the encoding has the check instructions; the executors, the manual and the tests follow; the gates of the scalar core were counted.** *Grammar:* `tools/encopt/export.py --write-grammar RESULT --grammar-out DIR` writes the selection of an `encopt` result as `spec/pdp64-enc.hbnf` and `spec/pdp64/pdp64.hbnf` with the writer that wrote the committed grammar (re-writing the committed grammar reproduces it but for comment lines); the corpus is the 841 files compiled by pcc with `-xchecks` (319,993 instructions, 6,932 of them checks), the distances iterated until the selection stopped changing (the third round equals the second), the budget the whole code space (no reserve, R452). **The new grammar has 159 forms and 217 leaves (was 207 and 268), 1 of the 65,536 first parcels is illegal (was 3,278), and every opcode is renumbered.** The optimum for the corpus with checks is 1,005,814 bytes in the model against 1,011,390 for the committed selection (-0.55%). *Measured with the real assembler and linker* (`pdp64-static.py`, the 841 files): the plain corpus **296,076 instructions, 950,058 bytes (the old encoding: 964,158: -1.5%)**; with the checks **320,000 instructions and 1,005,728 bytes**, which is **+8.1% instructions and +5.9% bytes over the plain corpus** with the real instructions (+9.8% instructions and +9.3% bytes with the expansion into `cb..` and `bpt`, errata 153; the one-instruction-a-check ideal of errata 147 was +2.3% and +2.9%: about 17,000 of the added instructions are not the checks, not yet analysed). The optimiser chose `CTM12/a` (8 patterns) and `CT1` (64 patterns). *What changed with the grammar:* the forms that mention `pdp64/32` in the names; `check` is vector 16 of `spec/pdp64-system.hbnf`; the tables, the vectors and the expected words of the tests were regenerated by the study's scripts (`mk_vectors.py`, `mk_asm_tests.py`, `mk_ld_tests.py`; `mk_vectors.py` has the check instructions), the generated manual (1,353 figures, the entries `CT` and `CHK2` and the vector), `tests/enc/generated.sha256`; the drift check no longer demands a reserve of 5% (the author's "no reserve"); `as64` now **writes the real instructions** (`ctcc`, `cthis` and `ctlo` as aliases, `cteq`, `ctne`, `chk2`) and the record of the table of check sites for each, no longer the expansion into `cb..` and `bpt`; an immediate bound assembles for `ctcc`, `cthi`, `cteq` and `ctne` up to 4095, every other case takes a register. *Executors:* the shared executor (`common/pdp64exec_enc.inc`: sim64 and the QEMU target) executes `ct<cc>`, `cteq`, `ctne` and `chk2` as a fault on vector 16 with `EPC` and `EVA` the address of the instruction (tests `tests/sim/checks.s` and `sys-check.S`). *Scalar core gates* (`docs/encoding-work/vec-gates.md`): the core was synthesised block by block, with the decoder generated from the grammar: **about 220,000 to 310,000 NAND2 equivalents** (no caches, no branch predictor); the yes and likely vector operations add 195,000 to 280,000: **1.6 to 2.3 times the core's logic**; the block alone with integer lanes a third to a half. *Not done:* the LLVM tables from the grammar and the port (errata 159); a vectorizer; the vector instructions in the grammar; the 17,000 non-check instructions of the checked code.


161. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-17): the vec, tile and GEMM family is added now, to be benchmarked and pruned; the measure is the most work for the fewest instructions, under the priority on safety, security, restarts and precise exceptions; LLVM tests the vector and GEMM approaches, GCC (the third back end) the bounds-check methods.** *The author's words:* "the point of adding them now is so we can benchmark and the remmove any that are duds"; "basically we want the vector/gemm/simd ops that compress our code the most, the most work for the least instuctions, but of course taking into account our priority on safety, securit, restarts, precise exception, etc"; "oh right..... llvm has the vector optimizer, so really we'll have to run the prefix optimizer again based on llvm output"; "llvm lets us test different approaches to vector/gemm type ops, and gcc let's us test bounds checking methods"; "gcc will be third backend."; and to the assistant's list of choices (mnemonics as dotted words, the assistant's leanings for the open rules of `vec-isa.md` 4, the tile and `gemm.f` added too): "go ahead with those choices". *Decided:* (1) **the family of `vec-isa.md` section 2 and the tile and whole-GEMM forms of `gemm-forms.md` are in the grammar, the assembler, the executors and the tests** (errata 157 had kept the tile and `gemm.f` under study: they are added as the author asked, for the benchmarks); (2) **what survives is decided by measurement**: the instructions that give the most work (elements) for the fewest instructions and the least code, never at the price of restartability, precise faults and the hidden state not being saved at a context switch (R435); an instruction that fails the measure, or cannot be made restartable and precise, is removed by deleting its word from `docs/codegen-data/encoding/vecfam.py` and regenerating; (3) **the back ends**: pcc (no vectorizer), LLVM (its vectorizers test the vector and GEMM approaches: the loops it vectorizes are the data for the next optimiser run), and GCC as a third back end (bounds-check methods; GNAT, with its non-call exceptions and `ctrap` patterns, is why). *Done:* (a) **the grammar**: 25 forms of 372 distinct words as forced forms, built from the committed grammar by `tools/encopt/addforms.py` because the corpus of the optimiser (the 841 compiled files) is not on the machine that did this: one low-value candidate, `CMPIh46/a` (1,024 patterns; 206 static bytes on the statistics of 2026-11-10), was dropped to make room (the family takes 214 first-parcel patterns, 810 are free); every opcode was renumbered by the shifted prefix assignment, and the tables, vectors and expected words were regenerated; **the encodings are provisional: the optimiser is to be run again on the corpus compiled by the LLVM back end, with the vector loops it makes**; (4) **the operands**: a vector register `v0` to `v15` (4 bits) and a tile register `t0` to `t7` (3 bits) are hidden registers with their own operand kind (`vregW`, `tregW`); `v0` to `v15` and `t0` to `t7` are therefore reserved words in `as64`, no longer usable as labels (three labels of `tests/sim/sys-trace.S` were renamed); the gather's `(rb)[vI]` is one operand of the assembler and two of the codec; the strided forms take `(Ra)+` and a stride; (5) **the executor** (`common/pdp64exec_vec.inc`, shared by `sim64` and the QEMU target): a block runs whole in one step (an interrupt waits for its end); the strip is 32 bytes and `VL` is that over the widest lane of the block; the registers a strip changes are committed when it ends and its stores are held until then, after their addresses are checked (a fault leaves the registers as they were at the start of the strip); **a block with hidden accumulation (a `vclr`, an accumulator named before it, a tile written) restarts from its entry with the accumulators discarded** (`vec-isa.md` 4.1, the second option, the assistant's leaning that the author accepted), any other block goes on from the strip it stopped at; the counters (`vblocks`, `vstrips`, `vops`, `velems`) are in the processor structure for the benchmarks; (6) **choices the documents left open**, made by the assistant and open to change: the sliding window's centre is `(taps-1)/2` (the hand-written kernel reads 3 before and 4 after for eight taps; `vec-isa.md` says `taps/2`); a floating-point value in an integer register is binary32 in the low 32 bits (`f`) or binary64 (`d`); the gather's index lanes are signed byte offsets in lanes of the destination's width; `gemm.f rd, rr, rc` takes a descriptor of twelve 64-bit words at `rd` (A, B, C addresses, M, N, K, three strides in bytes, alpha, beta as binary32, flags) and does the output tiles from the row `rr` and the column `rc` until the column reaches N; the unsigned `vminu`, `vmaxu` and `vabdu` were added for the bytes of pixels (they are not in `vec-isa.md`); `vend` takes two parcels because a leaf of no field gets no record from hbnf; (7) **the manual** has an entry for each family (`tools/manual/isa_vec.py`, built from the same lists), all tagged (P) and held back from the printed manual until the benchmarks keep them (errata 120). *Not done:* the optimiser run on LLVM output; the `d(Ra)+` operand of the strip memory operations (it is `d(Ra)` and an add); the two-operand shorthand of the hand-written kernels (`vadd.l v1, #32`); a printed report of the counters by `sim64`; the vectorizer and the LLVM and GCC back ends (the LLVM scalar back end is being built); the prose of the manual for the family. *Evidence:* `tests/sim/vec-basic.s` (a float multiply-add with a short last strip, a widening and saturating byte map, reductions, a strided load, `vlen`) passes on `sim64` and on QEMU; `tools/check-all.sh` passes (the format vectors, the drift check, the hbnf decoder cross-check and the exhaustive executor test over the first parcels).

162. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-17): as64 accepts NASM's small spellings, all in the grammar; the numbers with an `h` or `b` suffix are added.** *The author's words:* "also in an agent what would be needed to let as64 be nasm compatible too"; "for as64/nasm, do the small changes. and add the h and b suffixes for numbers. if that is a conflict explain."; "I mean, A6h means 0xA6 and 1001b means 0b1001 what is the conflict?"; "i want all the language in the grammar, not in a preprocessor". *What was done* (a research agent listed what NASM has and what each piece costs; its report is the source of the list below, its estimates are not verified): (1) **in the grammar** (`spec/pdp64-asm.hbnf`; the first version of this work rewrote the lines in the macro layer, `asmpre.c`, and was removed: the layer is as it was): the statements `section`, `segment`, `global`, `extern`, `bits`, `default`, `cpu`, `align`, `alignb`, `dd`, `dq`, `resb`, `resw`, `resd`, `resq`, `times`, in lower and upper case, a datum named without the colon, the strings `'text'` and `` `text` ``, the multi-character constant `'abcd'`, the numbers with a suffix, and `$` and `$$`; the parser glue (`as64/asmp.c`) turns each into the dotted directive of the same meaning; `.fill count, size, value` is the one new directive (it is what `times` means); (2) **the overlaps** and how they are settled (docs/asm-syntax.md): `1b`, `10b`, `101b` are also the nearest numeric local label before the line: a numeric label that is defined before the use wins, so no program that uses local labels reads differently; `A6h` and `beadh` may be names: a symbol of that name wins, and the names an assignment, `.globl`, `global`, `extern` or `.comm` gives are known before the first pass so that the answer is the same in every pass; `$name` stays AT&T's immediate, so `$-2` is the immediate -2; the NASM words are now reserved; (3) tests: `tests/asm-equiv/nasm` and `nasm-numbers` (the NASM spellings assemble to the same object as the native ones), `tests/asm-err/nasm-section` and `nasm-bits`. *Not done, and why:* the operand order (NASM's destination first needs an explicit syntax switch and a table of the roles of every instruction's operands, the agent's estimate is 3 to 4 days of the 6 to 9 for the useful subset), the `[ ]` memory operands and the size words, NASM's `%` macro language (the author declined full macro engines in errata 139; a separate pass is what the agent suggests, against the rule of this entry), `seg` and `wrt`.

163. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-17): a valid number is never a name; `A6h` is a number and cannot be a label (this reverses one rule of errata 162).** *The author's words:* "i think a valid number should never be a label. So A6h or a6h just shouldn't be allowed as a label." *Decided:* the numbers of the grammar (`0x1f`, `0b101`, `0o17`, and with a suffix `A6h`, `1001b`, `17q`, `10d`) are never names: the rule `symbol` of `spec/pdp64-asm.hbnf` begins with `!numtok`, so a label, an assignment or a declaration with such a spelling is a syntax error (the message says "something other than numtok"); a name that must be spelled like a number is written in double quotes.  In errata 162 a symbol named `A6h` won over the number; that is withdrawn: `A6h` is 0xA6 wherever it stands.  The numeric local label (digits only, `1:`, `1b`, `1f`) stays, and a defined numeric label still wins over the binary reading of `1b`, `10b`, `101b`.  *What was done:* the grammar, the evaluator (`as64/as64.c`), `docs/asm-syntax.md`, the tests (`tests/asm-err/number-label`, `number-assign`; the lines of `tests/asm-equiv/nasm-numbers` that had labels named `beadh` and `fadeh` are removed).  *Not decided:* the message is the generator's own and not friendly; a name such as `ah` or `fh` (a hexadecimal digit and an `h`) is now a number too, the NASM rule that needs a leading digit was not adopted, as the author wrote.

164. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-17): the check-site table moves into the assembler, the macros are dropped, and `asmpre.c` is gone; expressions are to be evaluated from the parse tree.** *The author's words:* "do 1 and 2, then drop the macros"; "i mean.... llvm and gcc never emit asm macros anyway right? nor does pcc"; "good... when done, we shouldn't need as64pre.c at all, right?"; and, on whether the grammar tool could do them: "hbnd implements cpp as a grammar.... so why couldn't it implement asm macros?" *Done:* (1) **the check layer**: `.chk KIND, "file", LINE` is a directive of the grammar (`dname`), and the assembler writes the record of the table of check sites itself for each `ct<cc>`, `cteq`, `ctne` and `chk2` (a label at the instruction, the file name once in `.data`, the 12 bytes of `.bugrec`; `as64.c`: `chk_site`); `cthis` and `ctlo` are words of the grammar (`mn-ext` takes aliases numbered 0x1000 plus the number of their word: the words of `mn-ext` now go past 256, so the 0x100 of the other groups would collide); (2) **the macros are dropped**: `.macro`, `.endm`, `.rept`, `.endr` and the macro calls are gone, with `as64/asmpre.c` and its line map; the tests of them were removed; neither GCC, LLVM nor pcc writes them (pcc's pdp64 back end has none; a hand-written assembly file uses the C preprocessor, `.S`); the docs say so (`docs/asm-syntax.md`, appendix E of the manual); errata 139 had decided them, this supersedes it for the macros and `.rept`. *Not done:* (3) **expressions from the parse tree**: `as64.c` still lexes the text of every expression again (`eval_primary` and the rest), so the syntax of numbers is in the grammar and in C; it is to be made one (the evaluator walks the tree that the grammar builds). *Noted:* hbnf can write a preprocessor as a grammar (`corpus/c/cpp.hbnf`: definitions as facts, a call as a `=>` rewrite, function-like macros with `#`, `##` and `__VA_ARGS__`); my first answer that neither `->` nor `=>` could define a rule at run time was wrong (the table in `ABNF.md` was out of date); if macros are wanted again they are an hbnf grammar of that kind, not a layer in C.

165. **DONE (2026-11-17): stage 1 of the LLVM back end, a scalar code generator that writes assembly for as64 (errata 159, 125, 137).** *What exists* (`llvm/`, `tools/gen-llvm-td.py`): the target `PDP64` of LLVM 21.1.8 as an experimental target (`llc --version` lists it), built from tables that the generator reads out of the grammar through `encgram` (the 692 mnemonics, `r0` to `r31` with the hot tables, the field widths; the `.td` files hold no encoding and no opcode number, so the next optimiser run renumbers nothing here); the code generator: the calling convention of appendix D with pcc's callee-saved `r24` to `r31` and its frame layout, frameless leaves, `cbCC` with immediates of 16 bits, the addressing modes, `(rN)+` and `-(rN)`, the literal pool, jump tables, byval and sret, 128-bit integers, floating point; output is `as64` text in `.text`, `.data` and `.bss` (no `.rodata`, `.local` or `.comm`: read-only data goes to `.data`). One edit to LLVM's own files: `.align` for `.p2align`, in `llvm/patch/llvm-core.patch`. *Verified* (by running `llvm/check.sh`): 37 of 37 programs in `llvm/tests/`, whose expected values come from the same IR compiled by the host's x86 clang, and 5 of 5 of `tests/c` and 21 of 21 of `tests/c-new` through clang and `llc`, on `sim64`. *Not done:* varargs (a fatal error), tail calls, PIC, TLS, debug information, GlobalISel; `fma`, `fmin`, `fmax`, `frint*`; the integrated assembler and encoder (`llc -filetype=obj` and `llvm-mc` do not work for pdp64); `TargetTransformInfo` (the vectorizers need it); atomics (they become `__atomic_*` calls the crt does not have); **stage 2: the vector, tile and GEMM instructions and the check instructions**, which is where the vectorizers are tested; QEMU was not run on its output; `fsqrt32` was not run. The frozen machine-code layer of the original encoding is in `llvm/target/frozen-M1/` and is not built.

166. **DONE (2026-11-17): the assembler evaluates expressions from the tree the grammar builds, no longer by lexing the text again (the third part of errata 164).** *What changed* (`as64/`): `asmir.h` has an expression tree (`struct xnode`); `asmp.c` builds it from the generated parse tree (`build_primary`, `build_unary`, `build_term`, `build_expr`), taking each expression from its field of the tree (immediate, absolute, pool, bare, base, indexed, post-increment, assignment, directive arguments, `times`) instead of cutting the text by position; `as64.c` evaluates it (`eval`), and the second lexer (`struct ex`, `eval_primary` and the rest) is deleted, so the syntax of numbers, character constants, `$`, `$$` and the operators is in the grammar only.  What the grammar cannot say stays in the evaluator because it needs the symbol table: `1b` is the nearest numeric label before the statement when one is defined, else the binary number 1; the error for an undefined local label.  *Verified:* `tools/check-all.sh` exits 0; the objects, the exit codes and the messages of the assembler before and after were compared over 72 sources in both relaxation modes (`tests/asm`, `asm-equiv`, `asm-err`, `ld`, `relax`, `timing`, the simulator sources and `tests/c`, `c-new`): 144 comparisons, no difference. *A bug fixed:* the old code took the first `(` of an operand for the start of the base register, so a displacement that is itself an expression in parentheses (`movq ('a')(r2), r1`, `movq (1+2)*3(r2), r1`) silently lost the displacement, and `-(3)(r2)` was an error; the new code evaluates them (test `tests/asm-equiv/paren-disp`; the old assembler refuses the last line of it, and the others it assembled wrongly).  *Where the grammar is still read twice:* the lookahead `!idchar` after a number in the rule `primary` skips blanks first, so in `times 5 db 0` it sees the `d` of `db`, the number fails and `5` comes out as a `symbol` (and `5 h` is let through as a hexadecimal suffix); the assembler works around it (a number token that contains a blank is a bad expression; a symbol that begins with a digit and is a number is read as one: `digit_name` in `asmp.c`).  Putting the lookahead inside the number rules did not help, hbnf skips the blanks there too; it needs a way to say that two tokens are adjacent, which is a request to hbnf.  *Not verified:* `tests/c-new` through `tools/pdp64test.sh` (only its `.s` files were compared); after a first bad subexpression the new evaluator stops where the old one sometimes went on (which later errors print could differ; no test does).

167. **DONE and PARTLY DONE (2026-11-17): stage 2 of the LLVM back end: the check instructions are selected; the vector instructions are reached by a loop-to-block pass, not yet by LLVM's vectorizers (errata 161, 165).** *The check instructions:* `llvm.trap` and `llvm.ubsantrap` lower to a check that always traps (`ctcc r0, #0`); the machine pass `PDP64CheckFold` turns a compare-and-branch to a block that only traps into one `ct<cc>`, `cteq`, `ctne` or `chk2`, with no branch and no failure block, and writes the `.chk KIND, "file", LINE` line before it (kind from the trap's argument or the compare, file and line from debug information, else the module name and line 0); the immediate form is 12 bits unsigned and exists for `ctcc`, `cthi`, `cteq` and `ctne` only. Measured on a bounds-checked C loop (`-fsanitize=bounds -fsanitize-trap=bounds`), instructions executed / bytes executed / image text bytes: no checks 23,687 / 74,818 / 320; checks as `cbCC` to one shared trap block 27,727 / 99,058 / 344; folded 27,727 / 90,978 / 328. So a check costs one instruction either way (+17%) and the fold saves 2 bytes for each executed check and the failure block; the 85% of errata 147 was pcc's call and stub and is not what LLVM generates. A block that calls a noreturn function (panic, bounds-fail) is not folded. *The vector instructions:* the pass `PDP64VecLoop` (`-pdp64-vecloop`, off by default) rewrites an innermost counted loop, whose body is consecutive or strided loads and stores, widening and saturating forms, integer and floating-point lane operations, `vfma` and reductions, into one `vec Rs, Re` ... `vend` block (inline assembly), with a run-time overlap test that sends the loop to the scalar original when the arrays may overlap; `-pdp64-vecloop-strip` makes one block for each strip inside a scalar loop (the shape a fixed-width vectorizer would give). `sim64 -V` prints the counters. *Measured* (`llvm/bench`, 31 kernels, 1,000 elements, the results of all three variants equal): the geometric mean of the instructions executed against the scalar code is 1/11.4 for one block and 1/6.4 for a block for each strip; 29 of the 31 kernels become blocks (`k_hist` has a gather, `k_ssm` calls `expf`); **the code is larger**: 2,132 bytes for the 29 kernels as scalar code, 6,754 with the block and its fallback and overlap tests, 8,924 for a block for each strip. `llvm/vector-usage.md`: of the 372 vector words 72 are used by these kernels, 33 of 55 base mnemonics; **never used (22):** `vlen`, `vclr`, `vfir`, `vlseg`/`vsseg`, `vldx`, `vdot`, `vmla`, `vfmab`, `vabdu`, the tile instructions and `gemm.f`. *Findings for the optimiser and the design:* (1) the strip's `VL` is that of the widest lane type of the block, so a widening reduction narrows it: sums of bytes and dot products run 8 elements for each operation, not 32; (2) hidden registers do not survive from one block to the next and non-accumulators are zero at each strip, so a `vclr` accumulator cannot serve a loop compiler, only reductions inside the loop; (3) the prefix form (one instruction without `vend`) is of use only to a load or store, which has the `(Rs)+` stream. *Open question for the author:* what the suffix of `vacc`, `vmaxacc` and `vminacc` means: today it is the lane type of the source register and the sum is signed, so `vld.bu.w v0, (r1)+` then `vacc.l` reads pairs of word lanes (sum of the bytes 1 to 8: 16, with `vacc.w`: 36); the hand-written kernel `k_sad` uses `.l`. *Not done:* the vector and tile register classes, instruction selection and `TargetTransformInfo` (so LLVM's own vectorizers do not vectorize for pdp64: this is what the owner's purpose for the back end needs next), tiles, GEMM and scalable vectors, recognisers for `vfir`, `vlseg`, `vldx`, `vdot` and `vmla`, spilling of vector registers (more than 16 live values leaves the loop scalar), a restart test through an operating-system handler, dynamic counts of pcc's code; `check_range` does not form `chk2` at -O0. *Verified:* `llvm/check.sh`: 50 programs, `tests/c` 5 of 5, `tests/c-new` 21 of 21, `tests/vec` 27 of 27 (9 kernels, three variants), and `tools/check-all.sh` passes.

168. **DECISIONS (the author's, 2026-11-17): the reductions name the source lane type and the accumulate width; the assembler checks the lane types of the vector registers in a block; the accumulate widths are the fewest the lanes need.** *The author's words:* "option 3 and 4. why were these even questions, why didn't you do this by default?"; "and what is this limitation of 8 or 32? I thought vec ops operated over unlimited regions of memory, if the process has the permissions?"; "also, I want reasonable performance while minimizing silicon footprint". *Decided and done:* (1) **`vacc.<src>.<acc> Rd, vS`, `vmaxacc.<src>` and `vminacc.<src>`**: `<src>` is the lane type the register holds, with its signedness (`b w l q` signed, `bu wu lu` unsigned, `f d`), and the lanes are extended as it says, so unsigned bytes are summed straight from byte lanes with no widening load; `<acc>` is the width the sum is made in and wraps in; the sum is added to the 64-bit register; the strip's `VL` counts `<src>` and never `<acc>`; 30 words (`vacc`: 12, the fewest widths: `l` for `b`, `bu`, `w`, `wu` sources, `l` or `q` for `l` and `lu`, `q` for `q`, and `vacc.f`, `vacc.f.d`, `vacc.d`; `vmaxacc` and `vminacc`: 9 each), replacing the 18 of `vacc.<t>`; (2) **the assembler follows the lane type of each vector register between `vec` and `vend`** (`vec_typecheck` in `as64/as64.c`) and refuses an instruction that reads a register as lanes of another size or kind than the last write in the block made (`vacc.bu.l` over what `vld.bu.w` wrote, `vadd.w` over `vld.b`); a register not written in the block, and signedness, are not checked; tests `tests/asm-err/vacc-lane` and `vadd-lane`; (3) **silicon**: the accumulate widths were cut to the fewest so that there is one adder tree for byte and halfword sources and not two (a sum of 32 bytes or of 16 words fits 32 bits, of eight 32-bit lanes needs 35); this is now a standing criterion of the pruning together with the work for each instruction: **reasonable performance for a small silicon footprint**; (4) the hand-written kernels `k_sad`, `k_q8dot`, `k_q4dot` were corrected (`k_sad` now sums bytes: `vld.bu`, `vabdu.b`, `vacc.bu.l`, strips of 32); (5) the LLVM pass `PDP64VecLoop` spells the reductions the new way and takes an unsigned extension as `bu`, `wu`, `lu`. *On the strip:* **the 8 and the 32 are not a limit on the region.** A block covers any length from `Rs` to `Re`, as far as the process may touch memory; `VL` is how many elements one pass over the block (a strip) handles, and it is the implementation's to choose (`vec-isa.md` 1: the program never names it): `sim64` models a strip of 32 bytes, so a loop over 1,000 bytes is 32 strips with byte lanes and 125 with 8-byte-wide ones: the cost is more passes through the block, not a shorter region.  Two things are not decided: `vec-isa.md` 4.2 (a limit on the strips of one block, to bound the time before an interrupt: today `sim64` runs a block whole and stops it only at a guard of 2^26 strips) and the rule that `VL` is that of the widest lane of the block (a `vdot.b` followed by `vacc.l.l` still runs with 32-bit lanes, 8 to a strip: the alternative is a count of lanes for each register, which is state to keep). *Measured:* the benchmark of `llvm/bench` is unchanged by this: the pass cannot yet use byte lanes for a sum such as `k_sad` because C promotes the bytes to `int` before the arithmetic and the pass does not narrow (LLVM's own vectorizer does; it is the reason to build the register classes and `TargetTransformInfo`); the change is available to hand-written code, and `tests/sim/vec-basic.s` has the sums of the 200 bytes 0..199, unsigned (19,900) and signed (1,468), in strips of 32. *Verified:* `tools/check-all.sh` passes; `llvm/check.sh`: 50, 5 of 5, 21 of 21, 27 of 27.
169. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-18): the words of the assembler are special only where the grammar expects them, so a C program may call a global `dd`, `v0` or `resb`.** *The clash:* with the NASM spellings (errata 162) and the vector and tile registers (errata 161), `lea dd, r10` was read as the label `lea` before a datum `dd` (NASM's `msg dd 5`), `lea v0, r10` took `v0` for a vector register, and `.quad resb` read `.quad` as a label before the datum `resb`. Nothing found it before the LLVM stage 2b agent tried every word as a global; pdp64 symbols have no leading underscore (`abi.md`), so a compiler's names reach the assembler as they are. *The fix, in the grammar and no new feature of hbnf:* the label before a datum is not an instruction word (`insnword`) and not a directive (`!( "." dname )`); the vector registers, the tile registers and the gather's `(base)[vI]` are operands of the vector instructions only (`vecinsn`, whose operands are `voperand = vregop | tregop | gatherop | operand`: a word that starts with `v`; every other instruction takes `operand`, which no longer has them). The glue numbers the alternatives of `stmt` and `operand` again (`as64/asmp.c`, `asmir.h`). *What stays a word:* a register name (`r0` to `r15` and `r<digits>`, `sp`, `fp`) and a valid number (a name of hexadecimal digits and a final `h` or `H`: `ch`, `ah`, `deadh`; errata 163): a compiler writes those in double quotes, and the LLVM back end now does for every such name (it quoted only `ch` and the words that are plain symbols now). `tests/sim/reserved-names.s`, `llvm/tests/sym_reserved.ll`. pcc's `exname` quotes the registers only: it still has to quote the names that are valid numbers (`ch`, `ah`, ...), and cannot be built here (no yacc); see the report.
170. **DECISION (the author's, 2026-11-18): wave 1 of the revised vector family is implemented: the gather, the tile, `gemm.f`, the lane variants nothing uses and all but one `vlen` are removed; 18 words and three operand forms are added.** *The author's words:* "i accept all your recommendations and leanings" (of `docs/encoding-work/vec-survey.md` and the silicon check `vec-silicon.md`, which found that no recommendation fails and that the conversions are to be built only with floating-point lanes; wave 2, `vceq vcgt vcgtu vbsl` and `vcvt.f.l vcvt.l.f`, is **not** accepted yet and is not implemented). *Removed (55 words, 384 to 347 words of the family, 55 base mnemonics to 58 with the new ones; the survey's 372 and 335 counted 12 fewer words than the grammar had):* `gemm.f`; `vtclr`, `vtfmopa`, `vtld`, `vtst`, `vtadd`; the 22 words of `vldx`; `vlen.w .l .q .f .d`; `vmul.q vmin.q vmax.q vminu.q vmaxu.q vabd.q vabd.f vabd.d vabdu.l vabdu.q`; `vmla.b vmla.q`; `vclr.b .w .q` (`.l .f .d` stay); `vfir.b .q .d` (`.w .l .f` stay). *Kept, ranked last:* `vfir`, `vfmab`, `vclr` and the 16 hidden registers (the only state that crosses a strip). *Added:* `vavg.b .w`, `vavgu.b .w`; `vmulh.w`, `vmulhu.w`, `vmulhr.w`; `vsarr.w .l`, `vshrr.w .l`; `vdot.w`; `vsad.bu`; `vtbl.b`; `vdacc.b .bu .bsu .w` (18 words); the rounding shift immediate of `vsts` (`vsts.<lane>.<m> vS, (Ra)+, #sh`); `vmla` by a vector (`vmla.w .l vD, vA, vB`); the scalar and immediate forms of `vmulh*` and `vsarr`, `vshrr`. *Decided by the owner's criteria where the survey left it open* (each in `vec-isa.md` section 6): **(1)** `vmulhr` saturates (-32768 x -32768 gives 32767, as `sqrdmulh`; `pmulhrsw` wraps): safety over bit-compatibility with one ISA; **(2)** the rounding shifts and the rounding store are exact, round half up, with the sum not wrapped; a count above the lane width gives 0; **(3)** `vdacc` is the exact 64-bit sum of all the products of the strip, not the wrapped sum of 32-bit lanes (it differs from `vdot.w` then `vacc` only at 2^31 in one lane), and the strip stays 32 bytes of byte lanes; **(4)** `vsad.bu` and `vdacc` add to a scalar register that is committed when the strip commits, as `vacc`: no hidden state; **(5)** `vtbl.b`: an index of 16 or more gives 0 (not `pshufb`'s bit 7); the register form takes its table per group of 16 lanes as the survey says, **and a memory form `vtbl.b vD, (Ra), vI` was added**, with exactly 16 bytes at `(Ra)` for every lane and strip: the survey's register form has a trap that the survey did not name, a table loaded into a register by a strip load has only the lanes of the strip, so on the last, short strip the table is truncated and the lookup is wrong; the memory form does not depend on the strip's length (the silicon is the same multiplexer: `vtbl_one` 12,663 against `vtbl_g16` 12,736 in `vec-silicon.md`); **(6)** `vlen` is `vlen.b Rd`: the bytes of a full strip (it was `vlen.<t>`, which inside a block returned the block's `VL` whatever the type); **(7)** `vavg` has the vector form only, the others have the register and immediate forms of the lane operations (a transform multiplies by a constant). *Done:* `docs/codegen-data/encoding/vecfam.py` (the forms; removed forms are `--drop`ped by `tools/encopt/addforms.py`, run as `addforms.py --grammar-out DIR --drop VLDX --drop VT.CLR --drop VT.MOPA --drop VT.LS --drop VT.ADD --drop GEMM`, then `tools/mkenc.py --md`, `tools/check-grammar-enc.sh --update`, `docs/codegen-data/encoding/mk_vectors.py`); the grammar (`spec/pdp64/pdp64.hbnf`: 183 forms and 241 leaves became 180 and 238: three forms added, `VDACC`, `VTBLM`, `VSTS.R`, and six removed, `VLDX`, `VT.CLR`, `VT.MOPA`, `VT.LS`, `VT.ADD`, `GEMM`; `spec/pdp64-enc.hbnf`: the mnemonic numbers); **the numbers were compacted** (the encodings are provisional, errata 161): every first parcel moved, so the golden vectors (`tests/enc/vectors.txt`, 18 lines), the expected words of 9 tests of `tests/asm` and the checksums were written again, and no number was kept for a removed word; the tile registers `t0`-`t7` and the gather operand `(Ra)[vI]` are gone from the assembly grammar (`spec/pdp64-asm.hbnf`, `as64/asmp.c`, `asmenc.c`); the executor (`common/pdp64exec_vec.inc`, with `tr` and `vtouch` removed from `struct pdp64_cpu`; the QEMU copies are generated by `qemu/sync.sh`); the lane-type checker of `as64` for every new word; `tools/manual/isa_vec.py` (entries `VAVG`, `VMULH`, `VROUND`, `VDACC`, `VTBL` added, `VLDX`, `VTILE`, `GEMM` removed; all still held (P)); `docs/encoding-work/vec-isa.md` section 6; tests `tests/sim/vec-wave1.s` (generated by `tests/sim/gen_vec_wave1.py`: 73 tests against an independent model in Python, the expected values embedded as data, 77 elements so the last strip is short) and 21 tests in `tests/asm-err` (the lane-type checker on the new words, and the removed words refused).
